## Sub-Domain 2: Civil Commitment & Involuntary Treatment

### SD2 Track 1 — Legal & Institutional Architecture


**Sub-Domain:** SD2 Civil Commitment & Involuntary Treatment
**Slug:** civCommit
**Date:** 2026-04-30
**Cycle:** RD2 first-pass; Track 1 Phase 2 SD2
**Authority:** RD2 §STEP 2 Phase 2 structural template; RD0 Standards 9, 10, 11; depth caps per Standard 16.C
**Source pairing:** sources inventoried in `source-workspace_D3_2026-04-30.md` §§1-7; SD2-specific entries

This document maps the legal architecture for civil commitment and involuntary treatment in PA-3 — the constitutional foundation, the operationally central PA Mental Health Procedures Act of 1976 (50 P.S. § 7101 et seq.), federal-and-state procedural protections, and the institutional bodies operating the system.

The analytical move SD2 establishes: **PA's MHPA architecture is structurally permissive of involuntary commitment relative to peer-state procedural floors, and the architecture's civil-liberties tensions are not fully addressable by procedural reform within the statute — they are constitutive features.**

---

#### §1 Constitutional Authority

Civil commitment doctrine operates from a five-case constitutional foundation, all 14th Amendment Due Process Clause-grounded:

- ***Addington v. Texas***, 441 U.S. 418 (1979): clear-and-convincing-evidence as the constitutional minimum standard for civil commitment, intermediate between preponderance and beyond-reasonable-doubt — establishes the procedural-protection floor.
- ***O'Connor v. Donaldson***, 422 U.S. 563 (1975): a state cannot constitutionally confine a non-dangerous mentally ill person who can survive safely in freedom — establishes the dangerousness-floor for substantive grounds.
- ***Youngberg v. Romeo***, 457 U.S. 307 (1982): committed persons have constitutionally-protected liberty interests in safety, freedom from unreasonable restraint, and minimally adequate care; professional-judgment standard for facility decisions.
- ***Washington v. Harper***, 494 U.S. 210 (1990): refusal-of-antipsychotic-medication is a 14th Amendment liberty interest; can be overridden in custodial settings via internal administrative process meeting due-process requirements.
- ***Sell v. United States***, 539 U.S. 166 (2003): four-prong test for forced medication to restore competency in non-emergency context (operative for SD6 forensic).

PA Const. Art. I § 1 (inherent rights) and Art. I § 9 (criminal due process) provide PA-specific procedural floors operating alongside the 14th Amendment in civil commitment. PA's parens patriae authority grounds the state-level commitment statute.

**Constitutional gap relevant to SD2**: federal constitutional minimums (clear-and-convincing; dangerousness floor; right to counsel) are met by PA MHPA but the operational implementation of these protections in Philadelphia 303 hearings has been documented as compressed (rushed proceedings; minutes-before-hearing counsel meetings). Federal constitutional doctrine sets minimums; state statutory architecture operationalizes; Philadelphia operational practice can fall below the operationalized intent without violating the federal floor.

---

#### §2 Federal Statutory Layer

The federal statutory layer for civil commitment is thin — federal law principally regulates commitment via constitutional doctrine (above) and via cross-cutting federal statutes operative in commitment contexts:

##### 2.1 Americans with Disabilities Act + Olmstead

42 U.S.C. § 12131 et seq. The integration mandate articulated in *Olmstead v. L.C.*, 527 U.S. 581 (1999), constrains states from unnecessary institutionalization including unnecessary commitment-driven institutionalization. Operative in SD2 as the upstream check on commitment as default response: Olmstead requires community-based alternatives where appropriate. **Statutory stability: STABLE for the textual provisions; integration-mandate-as-applied to commitment contexts variably enforced**.

##### 2.2 Protection and Advocacy for Individuals with Mental Illness Act (PAIMI)

42 U.S.C. § 10801 et seq. Disability Rights Pennsylvania (DRP), as PA's PAIMI grantee, has statutory authority to investigate abuse and neglect at any facility serving people with mental illness, access patients and records, and pursue legal remedies for systemic reform. Operationally critical for SD2: DRP currently highly active per TC-09: October 1, 2025 dependent-children settlement (M.D. Pa., Munley); March 24, 2025 YDC settlement; commitment-system due-process advocacy ongoing [F10 → resolved per TC-09]. **Statutory stability: STABLE**.

##### 2.3 Section 1983 + 42 U.S.C. § 1983

The general federal civil-rights remedy provides private right of action against state actors for constitutional violations including in commitment contexts. Used in *Youngberg* line of doctrine + Ingram v. Pennhurst State School and Hospital line. Operative for individual remedies; does not produce systemic reform absent class-certification or consent-decree pathway.

##### 2.4 ADA in Custody (Yeskey)

*Pa. Dept. of Corr. v. Yeskey*, 524 U.S. 206 (1998): ADA Title II applies to state prisons. Operative for SD6 forensic; identified at SD2 for commitment-meets-custody intersection (302-initiated-by-police pathway).

##### 2.5 Federal Funding Flows for Commitment Systems

Federal funding does not directly support commitment procedures themselves (which are state-funded), but indirectly affects commitment patterns through:
- **Medicaid IMD exclusion** (42 U.S.C. § 1396d(a)(B)): excludes federal Medicaid match for inpatient psychiatric care in facilities of more than 16 beds for adults aged 21-64. Structural constraint on inpatient capacity nationwide; Pennsylvania's state-hospital + private-psychiatric-hospital system has reduced inpatient bed capacity over decades partly in response. The SUPPORT Act § 1012 IMD parity for SUD partial waiver does not extend to MH-only facilities.
- **MHBG** (42 U.S.C. § 300x-1 et seq.): funds community-based services that, in adequate supply, reduce commitment volume by providing voluntary alternatives.
- **CCBHC PPS** (Cures Act § 223): cost-based payment for crisis services + community services; potential structural reducer of commitment-as-only-pathway.

Federal funding architecture indirectly shapes commitment volume by determining inpatient + community capacity available; the legal architecture for commitment is principally state.

---

#### §3 Federal Regulatory Layer

Federal regulatory architecture for SD2:

- **42 C.F.R. § 482.13(e)** — CMS Conditions of Participation for hospitals: restraint and seclusion standards applying to psychiatric facilities receiving Medicare/Medicaid. Notification requirements; medical review; documentation. Operative in commitment-receiving facilities.
- **45 C.F.R. Parts 92, 93** — HHS Section 504/ADA implementation in HHS-funded programs. Olmstead enforcement framework as applied to commitment alternatives.
- **HHS OCR enforcement guidance** on Olmstead-and-commitment intersections — sub-regulatory but practically directive.
- **42 C.F.R. Part 482 generally** — psychiatric-facility licensure and operations standards.
- **42 C.F.R. Part 483 Subpart B** — long-term care facility psychiatric-resident protections (intersection with NSH and similar long-term commitments).

Regulatory stability: 42 C.F.R. § 482.13(e) restraint-seclusion standards have been amended progressively (2006; 2007 — patient's-rights condition). Structurally durable. CMS oversight of facility compliance is more procedural than substantive in practice.

---

#### §4 Federal Agency Layer

##### 4.1 DOJ Civil Rights Division — Disability Rights Section

**PA-3 office presence**: HQ-based; Eastern District of PA U.S. Attorney's Office (Philadelphia) handles federal civil litigation. **Primary functions for SD2**: ADA Title II compliance investigations in state mental health systems; consent decrees with state systems on integration and on commitment-system procedural compliance; *Olmstead*-line litigation. **Administrative vulnerability: HIGH** [F-related] — Trump 2 administration DOJ CRD posture has shifted; case-selection priorities affect state systems' negotiating leverage.

##### 4.2 HHS Office for Civil Rights (OCR)

**PA-3 office presence**: Region 3 office in Philadelphia. **Primary functions**: Olmstead enforcement co-authority; Section 1557 ACA nondiscrimination including in commitment-receiving facilities. **Administrative vulnerability: MODERATE-HIGH** [F-related] — Trump 2 admin OCR posture variable.

##### 4.3 SAMHSA

Limited direct role in commitment systems; provides MHBG-funded community alternatives + technical assistance on commitment-alternative crisis services (mobile crisis; CSU). Administrative vulnerability per F11 [resolved per TC-06: comprehensive SAMHSA capacity erosion verified — AHA reorganization March 27, 2025; >50% staff reduction; PAIMI admin team laid off; January 2026 grant termination/reversal episode] affects all SD development; PAIMI administration team layoff specifically consequential for SD2 commitment-system advocacy.

##### 4.4 CMS

Limited direct role; provides Medicaid behavioral health framework + IMD-exclusion guidance + 1115 SUD IMD waivers. Operative for SD2 as inpatient-capacity-constraint origin via IMD exclusion.

##### 4.5 Federal Agency Layer for SD2 — synthesis

The federal agency layer for civil commitment is thin compared to other D3 sub-domains. The primary federal touchpoints are: (a) constitutional doctrine via DOJ CRD enforcement; (b) Olmstead community-alternative integration via HHS OCR; (c) facility-operations CoP via CMS. **Administrative vulnerability concentrates at DOJ CRD** for the commitment-system-Olmstead-enforcement function — the federal lever most variable under the current administration. Federal floor on commitment procedures remains via constitutional doctrine; federal *enforcement* on integration mandate is variable.

---

#### §5 State Layer

##### 5.1 PA Mental Health Procedures Act of 1976 — operational architecture

**Citation**: 50 P.S. § 7101 et seq.; major amendments 1978, 1988, 2004 (procedural protections enhancement). The operationally central state statute. **Statutory stability: STABLE** — 49 years of operation; multiple amendments without core restructuring. Each major section operates as a distinct procedural regime.

**§ 7301 — § 302 Involuntary Emergency Examination and Treatment**:
- Initiation: petition filed with county MH administrator by physician, police officer, designated mental-health professional, or any person; MHPA § 7301(b)(1)(i)
- Standard: "clear and present danger to himself or others" within 30 days; MHPA § 7301
- Authorization: warrant from county MH administrator (signed; non-judicial); written application
- Examination: within 2 hours of arrival at facility
- Treatment: up to 120 hours involuntary if criteria met
- **No prior judicial review** — the architectural feature with significant civil-liberties implications
- Volume: roughly 8,000-12,000 § 302 petitions annually in Philadelphia [F-verify current FY26]

**§ 7303 — Extended Emergency Involuntary Treatment**:
- Initiation: treating physician at end of 120-hour § 302 period
- Hearing: before MH review officer or judge of Common Pleas; MHPA § 7303(b)
- Right to counsel: yes; § 7303(b)(2)
- Standard: continuing clear-and-present-danger; MHPA § 7303(b)
- Maximum length: 20 days extended

**§ 7304 — Long-Term Involuntary Treatment**:
- Hearing: before judge; full evidentiary; § 7304(c)
- Standard: clear and convincing evidence of severe mental disability and meets one of three categories; § 7304(a)
- Right to counsel: yes
- Maximum length: 90 days; renewable
- **§ 7304(f) — Outpatient commitment**: court may order treatment in outpatient setting; PA's outpatient-commitment provision

**§ 7305 — Voluntary Treatment / Voluntary Admission with 72-Hour Notice**:
- Voluntary admission with patient consent
- 72-hour written notice required for discharge from voluntary status
- Facility may convert to involuntary status during 72-hour notice window if § 302 criteria met
- **Voluntary-to-involuntary conversion**: structural feature creating tension between voluntary-admission incentive and involuntary-conversion possibility

**§ 7401-7402 — Forensic competency procedures**: operative for SD6.

**§ 7105 — Voluntary treatment generally**: operative across SDs.

##### 5.2 PA Code Title 50 + Title 55

PA Code Title 50 establishes facility licensing and commitment-procedure operational standards. PA Code Title 55 (DHS regulations) implements Medicaid coverage of inpatient and outpatient services for committed and voluntary populations.

##### 5.3 PA Act 169 of 2006 — Psychiatric Advance Directives

20 Pa. C.S. § 5821 et seq. Permits competent adults to declare treatment preferences and designate surrogate decision-makers for MH care. Operative for SD2 as a procedural mechanism enabling individuals to constrain involuntary treatment options ex ante. Operationally underutilized [F-verify usage data].

##### 5.4 PA agencies operative in SD2

- **OMHSAS** (within PA DHS): commitment-system oversight; state hospital system administration including NSH (structurally forensic-only since 2019 per TC-05) and Danville State Hospital (civil §304 receiving for Philadelphia); 302-303-304 procedural standards.
- **PA Common Pleas + Municipal Court**: Philadelphia commitment hearings.
- **PA Department of Health**: commitment-receiving facility licensure.
- **Disability Rights Pennsylvania**: PAIMI-grantee advocacy + abuse/neglect investigation + due-process litigation. Currently highly active per TC-09: October 1, 2025 dependent-children settlement (M.D. Pa., Munley); March 24, 2025 YDC settlement [F10 → resolved per TC-09].
- **NSH (Norristown State Hospital):** PA DHS-operated state psychiatric facility; 375 beds, 100% forensic (255 Regional Forensic + 120 Forensic Stepdown) since civil section closed January 2019 [verified per TC-05]. Receives forensic NGRI / incompetent-to-stand-trial commitments; does NOT receive civil §304 commitments (those route to Danville State Hospital, Montour County). Only remaining state psychiatric facility in southeastern PA. PA statewide state-hospital bed total ~1,200-1,400 [F5 → resolved per TC-05].

##### 5.5 PA framework relative to peer states

PA MHPA is **structurally permissive of involuntary commitment relative to peer-state floors** in three dimensions:

(a) **No pre-detention judicial review for § 302**: warrant from county MH administrator (non-judicial) authorizes 120-hour involuntary detention. Some peer states require pre-detention judicial approval for similar holds.
(b) **Voluntary-to-involuntary conversion under § 305**: facility can convert voluntary-status patient to involuntary during 72-hour discharge notice. Some peer states require fresh § 302-equivalent showing for conversion.
(c) **Outpatient commitment under § 304(f)**: PA permits court-ordered outpatient treatment; some peer states do not authorize this.

Conversely PA is **more procedurally protective than the federal floor** in: (a) clear-and-convincing standard meets *Addington*; (b) right to counsel at § 303 + § 304 hearings; (c) statutory right to independent psychiatric evaluation; (d) habeas corpus availability.

---

#### §6 Local Layer

##### 6.1 Philadelphia Common Pleas Court — commitment processing

Philadelphia's § 303 + § 304 hearings are heard before MH review officers or Common Pleas judges. Hearings are scheduled at high volume (driven by the 8-12K annual § 302 → § 303 transition rate). Operational pattern documented historically:
- Hearing duration typically 15-30 minutes
- Defenders meet client in compressed timeframe (minutes before hearing)
- Right to counsel formally maintained; effective representation operationally compressed
- Structural disposition tilt toward continued commitment given evidence-presentation asymmetry

[F-verify current Philadelphia Common Pleas commitment-hearing operational data]

##### 6.2 Commitment-receiving facilities in Philadelphia

§ 302-initiated patients are received at:
- **Penn Presbyterian Medical Center** (Penn Medicine; West Philadelphia/University City) — psychiatric ED + IP unit
- **Temple University Hospital + Episcopal Hospital BH services** (North Philadelphia) — psychiatric services
- **Jefferson Health** (Center City, West Philadelphia) — psychiatric ED + IP
- **Friends Hospital** (Northeast Philadelphia, just outside PA-3 strict boundary) — psychiatric specialty
- **Belmont Behavioral Health** (West Philadelphia) — psychiatric specialty
- **Philadelphia Crisis Response Center (CRC)** — 1229 N. 3rd Street; DBHIDS-operated 24/7 walk-in; receives § 302 evaluations

Capacity constraint: psychiatric inpatient bed availability has been documented as scarce relative to § 302 volume; 24-72+ hour psychiatric boarding in EDs is a documented pattern resulting from the bed-availability gap. (Material development at SD7.)

##### 6.3 Disability Rights Pennsylvania role

DRP serves as PAIMI grantee with statutory authority to access patients + records at any commitment-receiving facility. Active engagement on Philadelphia commitment-system due-process documentation; current high activity verified per TC-09 (October 2025 dependent-children settlement; March 2025 YDC settlement) [F10 → resolved per TC-09].

##### 6.4 Home Rule and limits

Philadelphia Home Rule cannot supersede PA MHPA commitment procedures (state law preemption); cannot reduce procedural protections; but can supplement with operational programs (Right Care for police-MH-crisis diversion; CIT training; pre/post-booking diversion). Home Rule is permissive on alternatives, not on commitment procedures.

---

#### §7 Cumulative Racial Disadvantage

SD2 is the sub-domain where the racial-equity disparity in mental health system contact is most quantifiable and most documented. Three threads:

**Police-initiated 302 racial disparity**. PA-3's documented per-capita § 302 commitment rate is among the highest of major U.S. cities; Philadelphia overall sees 8-12K § 302 petitions annually. National research (e.g., Treatment Advocacy Center analyses; multiple peer-reviewed studies) consistently shows Black individuals face 3-4x higher rates of involuntary commitment than White individuals after controlling for clinical presentation. Philadelphia's pattern documented historically as following this national pattern, with police-initiated § 302s disproportionately involving Black men. The pathway operates: police-MH-crisis contact (more frequent in policed-heavily-Black neighborhoods) → 302-initiation-by-police (more likely than physician-initiated for certain demographic profiles) → 120-hour involuntary detention → 303-hearing-with-compressed-due-process → discharge or 304 commitment. Each step has racial-disparity documented.

**Voluntary-utilization underutilization for Black PA-3 residents**. Per D2-V cumulative racial-equity finding + national NSDUH treatment-receipt data: Black adults receive voluntary outpatient MH services at lower rates than White adults despite similar or higher MH burden. This underutilization is itself racially-patterned — driven by historical exclusion from CMHC infrastructure, documented schizophrenia overdiagnosis in Black patients, Tuskegee-effect medical distrust, cultural-mismatch of provider workforce, and rational-but-burdensome reluctance to engage with public BH systems — and it feeds directly into commitment-system overrepresentation. When voluntary alternatives are underutilized at population scale (for racially-traceable reasons), coercive-pathway entry rises.

**Coercive-entry pattern as compounded equity finding**. The combination of (a) police-initiated 302 racial disparity at the entry-pathway step with (b) voluntary-utilization underutilization upstream produces a system where Black PA-3 residents are systematically more likely to enter the BH system through 302 (coercive) than through outpatient (voluntary). This is the cumulative-disadvantage chain operating at SD2 specifically: it is documented; it is racially traceable; it cannot be addressed by procedural reform within MHPA alone — it requires upstream voluntary-system access expansion.

The full racial-equity chain develops at SD7 synthesis where the chain (302 → SMI in PPS → crisis-via-police pathway) is integrated. SD2 establishes the entry-pathway link.

---

#### §8 Anchor Institution Thread

Per RD0 Standard 10 triple-role coverage:

**Penn Medicine (Penn Presbyterian psych ED + IP)**. (i) Regulator/regulated: regulated by CMS CoP including § 482.13(e) restraint/seclusion + PA DOH licensure; functions as regulator-influencing through training pipelines. (ii) Resource/burden: psychiatric ED + IP capacity for PA-3 commitment receiving (resource); financial pressure of psychiatric service-line + Medicaid rate inadequacy on inpatient operations (burden). (iii) Structural beneficiary/excluded constituent: Medicaid + Medicare + commercial reimbursement (beneficiary); psychiatric-boarding-in-ED population structurally excluded from prompt inpatient placement (excluded).

**Temple Health (Temple Episcopal BH services)**. Per D2-V S118-V: Temple Health FY25 financial recovery confirmed; investment-grade BBB rating maintained; not closure-scenario. (i) Regulated: CMS, PA DOH. (ii) Resource: Episcopal psychiatric services serving North Philadelphia; long-standing community engagement orientation; (burden): financial pressure on safety-net mission. (iii) Structural beneficiary: Medicaid DSH + tax-exempt status; (excluded constituent advocate): North Philadelphia community engagement positions Temple Episcopal as advocate for commitment-receiving population.

**Jefferson Health**. (i)-(iii) Similar pattern to Penn for commitment-receiving role; less explicitly community-engagement-oriented than Temple.

**Norristown State Hospital (NSH)**. (i) Regulated by PA DHS internally; CMS for Medicaid-billing components; DRP PAIMI access. (ii) Resource: forensic NGRI/restoration commitments only since 2019 [TC-05] (developed in SD6); civil §304 long-term commitments now route to Danville State Hospital (Montour County); burden: capacity constraint; geographic distance from Philadelphia families. (iii) Structural beneficiary: continued state operation; planned Southeast Psychiatric Treatment Center expansion 270→420 forensic beds (groundbreaking 2026); excluded constituent: civil §304-committed PA-3 residents now routed to Danville (~150 miles from Philadelphia).

**Disability Rights Pennsylvania (DRP)**. Anchor advocacy institution. (i) Regulator-influencing through PAIMI litigation + investigation; not regulated but PAIMI-funded via 42 U.S.C. § 10801. (ii) Resource: due-process advocacy + abuse/neglect investigation. (iii) Functions as structural-beneficiary-advocate for excluded constituents (committed and voluntary BH-system populations).

#### §1 Data Provenance Statement (per RD0 Standard 1)

**Philadelphia-specific directly-documented**: PA-3 demographic baseline (2021 ACS); D2-V cumulative racial-equity findings; commitment-receiving facility list (DBHIDS contractor + hospital licensure); approximate § 302 petition volume range from historical PA Court reporting + DBHIDS-reported data (current FY26 [F-verify]).

**National benchmarks applied to PA-3 by structural inference**: NSDUH state-level treatment-receipt rates; Treatment Advocacy Center comparative state-commitment-rate data; peer-reviewed research on race-disparate involuntary commitment rates (3-4x Black-vs.-White gap); psychiatric boarding documentation patterns (national + selected-MSA).

**Statistical caveats**: per Standard 1 — no synthetic disaggregation; ranges where exact numbers unavailable; verify-flags for current-year claims.

#### §2 PA-3 Statistical Profile — Civil Commitment

##### 2.1 Volume estimates

**Philadelphia § 302 petition volume**: approximately **8,000-12,000 annually** [F-verify FY26]. This places Philadelphia in the upper range among major U.S. cities for per-capita involuntary commitment rate (~5-8 per 1,000 residents). PA-3 share of city-wide volume: approximately 50% by population proxy = ~4,000-6,000 PA-3-resident § 302 petitions annually.

**§ 303 transition rate**: estimated 60-70% of § 302 petitions proceed to § 303 (extension hearing); approximately 4,800-8,400 city-wide § 303 hearings annually [F-verify].

**§ 304 long-term commitments**: substantially smaller volume; estimated 200-400 annually city-wide [F-verify]. Most route to Danville State Hospital (Montour County) since NSH civil section closed January 2019 [TC-05].

**Voluntary admissions**: not tracked uniformly; CBH + commercial-payer + Medicare-payer admissions to psychiatric units in Philadelphia. Voluntary-vs.-involuntary admission ratio at Philadelphia psychiatric facilities historically tilts toward involuntary at higher rates than national average.

##### 2.2 Psychiatric boarding patterns

Documented at Philadelphia psychiatric-receiving EDs (Penn Presbyterian, Temple Episcopal, Jefferson, others) historically: 24-72+ hour holds for § 302 patients awaiting psychiatric inpatient placement is a regular pattern; periods of bed scarcity push this to 5+ days. Sources: local reporting (Inquirer + Philadelphia Magazine + Pew historically); facility quality-improvement data not consistently public. Current FY26 [F-verify].

##### 2.3 NSH capacity and constraint (revised per verification)

*Verification update (2026-04-30): Prior pass characterized NSH as ~300 beds with civil §304 receiving role. Verified state: NSH = 375 beds total, 100% forensic since civil section closed permanently January 2019 (255 Regional Forensic Psychiatric Center + 120 Forensic Stepdown). Civil §304 commitments from Philadelphia now route to Danville State Hospital (Montour County) — the geographic burden cited in the original analysis is exacerbated, not relieved. NSH is the only remaining state psychiatric facility in southeastern PA. Forward-looking: Stantec + architecture+ designing new Southeast Psychiatric Treatment Center; 270 single-occupant beds expanding to 420 total; groundbreaking 2026. See Material Changes Summary TC-05.*

NSH bed count = 375 (255 Regional Forensic + 120 Forensic Stepdown), 100% forensic since January 2019 [verified per TC-05]; statewide PA state-hospital bed total ~1,200-1,400 [F5 → resolved per TC-05]. NSH receives forensic NGRI/competency restoration commitments only; civil §304 long-term commitments from Philadelphia now route to Danville State Hospital (Montour County), increasing the family-contact externality (see G1-SD2-06 revised). NSH frequently at or near capacity; restoration-commitment defendants may wait in jail or private psychiatric inpatient settings for NSH placement — capacity-cascade feeding back into §302/303 boarding (G1-SD2-03) and §304-restoration backlog (G1-SD6-06). Forward-looking expansion to 420 beds at the new Southeast Psychiatric Treatment Center will partially relieve forensic-restoration backlog by 2027+.

##### 2.4 Demographic breakdown

Philadelphia § 302 commitment data is not consistently published with full demographic breakdown. National research (Treatment Advocacy Center; peer-reviewed studies) documents Black individuals face 3-4x higher rates of involuntary commitment than White individuals after controlling for clinical presentation. Philadelphia's racial demographic baseline (52% Black PA-3) combined with police-initiated § 302 patterns suggests § 302-recipient population is disproportionately Black; specific PA-3 breakdown [F-verify].

---

#### §3 Geographic Variation Across Four Sub-Areas

##### 3.1 North/Northwest Philadelphia Core

**Highest § 302 origination per capita**. Police-MH-crisis contact volume highest in North Philadelphia Core neighborhoods; § 302 initiations originating from police contact disproportionately concentrated here. Receiving-facility geography places Temple Episcopal as the most-proximate psychiatric ED; Penn Presbyterian (West Philadelphia) and Jefferson (Center City) also receive North Core patients via ambulance. Family-contact and post-discharge continuity burden compounded by transit-distance to Danville State Hospital (Montour County) if civil §304 commitment [TC-05]; or NSH (Montgomery County) if forensic.

##### 3.2 West Philadelphia Core

Bifurcated. **University City anchor proximity** means immediate access to Penn Presbyterian psychiatric ED + IP capacity for residents close to anchor. **Adjacent neighborhoods** (Mantua, Kingsessing, Cobbs Creek, etc.): § 302 initiation volume elevated; police-MH-crisis-contact patterns similar to North Core. Penn Presbyterian functions as receiving facility for the geographic catchment.

##### 3.3 Northwest Philadelphia

Internally heterogeneous. Higher-income tracts (Mt. Airy, Chestnut Hill) lower § 302 origination per capita; lower-income tracts (Germantown, Stenton, West Oak Lane, Wister) closer to North Core pattern. Receiving-facility distance higher (no major psychiatric ED proximate); patients transported to Temple, Penn Pres, or Jefferson.

##### 3.4 South/Southwest Philadelphia

Lower § 302 origination per capita than North Core. Receiving-facility access to Jefferson + Penn (Center City + West) closer; patient flows to these institutions. Voluntary-utilization rates within insured populations higher than North Core.

##### 3.5 Boundary-adjacent observations

**State-hospital long-term commitment routing creates a structural family-contact burden** for PA-3 residents committed §304 since NSH civil section closed January 2019 [TC-05]: civil §304 routes to **Danville State Hospital (Montour County)**, ~150 miles from Philadelphia; forensic restoration commitments continue to NSH (Montgomery County). Travel to Danville from Philadelphia by transit or car is substantial time + cost burden; visits frequently constrained by transit cost + work schedule + caregiving demands. Geographic externality of state-hospital placement is more severe than the prior Norristown-routing era.

**Friends Hospital, Belmont Behavioral Health** (just outside PA-3 strict boundary in Northeast Philadelphia or adjacent): receive PA-3 patients despite location outside the district; service-system geography is functionally city-wide rather than district-bound.

---

#### §4 Aggregate Pathway Tracing (per RD0 Standards 7 + 13)

**Triggering category**: A PA-3 adult experiencing acute psychiatric symptoms (suicidal ideation; psychotic episode; severe mood-disorder decompensation; severe dissociation) at a level meeting "clear and present danger" per MHPA § 302.

##### 4.1 Pathway entry points

Entry occurs via four distinct routes:
1. **Self-referral or family-referral to ED**: walk-in or family-bring; medical screening + psychiatric consultation; § 302 initiated by attending physician if criteria met
2. **Self-referral or family-referral to CRC**: walk-in to 1229 N. 3rd; psychiatric evaluation; disposition (§ 302 initiation; voluntary admission; outpatient referral)
3. **988 / mobile crisis (Right Care)**: phone access; mobile dispatch (limited zones [F3]); psychiatric stabilization in community when feasible; if not feasible, transport to ED or CRC
4. **Police contact (911 dispatch or street contact)**: § 302 initiated by police officer; transport to receiving facility for evaluation

The four entry points produce structurally different probabilities of § 302 initiation, of CIT-trained-officer response, and of voluntary-vs.-involuntary disposition. **Step where racial disparity primarily enters: police contact at entry-point 4**, where Black PA-3 residents are disproportionately represented relative to demographic base rate.

##### 4.2 § 302 → § 303 trajectory

Constituent in § 302 status: 2-hour examination at receiving facility; if criteria met, 120-hour involuntary detention. Patient typically held in psychiatric ED area or transferred to inpatient psychiatric unit; **psychiatric boarding** if no IP bed available — 24 to 72+ hour wait in ED setting common; longer in periods of bed scarcity.

End of 120 hours: discharge OR voluntary admission OR § 303 petition filed by treating physician. § 303 hearing scheduled before Common Pleas judge or MH review officer; right to counsel; defenders meet client in compressed timeframe (minutes before hearing); hearing typically 15-30 minutes; structural disposition tilt toward continued commitment given evidence-presentation asymmetry. Outcome: continued involuntary commitment up to 20 days OR discharge OR voluntary conversion.

##### 4.3 § 303 → § 304 trajectory or discharge

End of 20 days: discharge OR § 304 long-term petition filed. § 304 hearing before judge; full evidentiary; clear-and-convincing standard; right to counsel + independent psychiatric evaluation. Outcome: § 304 long-term commitment up to 90 days (for civil §304: routes to Danville State Hospital (Montour County) since 2019 [TC-05]; for forensic restoration: NSH; private inpatient bed-availability gap pushes more to state-hospital queue); outpatient commitment under § 304(f); discharge.

##### 4.4 Voluntary admission and § 305 voluntary-to-involuntary conversion

Constituent voluntarily admitting under § 305: signs voluntary-admission form; receives in-hospital care; if requesting discharge, 72-hour notice required; during 72-hour window, facility may convert to involuntary if § 302 criteria met. This conversion creates structural tension: a voluntary-admitting constituent learns at discharge-request that they cannot leave freely, and that voluntary status was effectively conditional on continued cooperation. Documented effect: deters voluntary admission for some constituents; rational-but-burdensome reluctance to use voluntary pathway.

##### 4.5 Aggregate breakdown points

- **Step 1 (entry-point routing)**: voluntary-pathway underutilization (especially for Black population per cumulative-equity finding); police-contact disproportionate at entry; CRC capacity bottleneck
- **Step 2 (psychiatric boarding)**: 24-72+ hour ED holds as systemic capacity failure
- **Step 3 (§ 303 hearing operational compression)**: rushed proceedings; defender-client contact compressed; structural disposition tilt
- **Step 4 (state-hospital placement queue)**: civil §304 patients waiting for Danville State Hospital bed [TC-05]; forensic restoration patients waiting for NSH bed; backward pressure on inpatient capacity
- **§ 305 conversion**: voluntary-pathway deterrent effect

##### 4.6 Community navigation actors

DRP (PAIMI advocacy + due-process litigation); CLS / PLA (civil legal aid for benefits-and-discharge issues); NAMI Philadelphia (family education + system navigation); peer specialists (CBH-network embedded; some in receiving-facility settings).

---

#### §5 Representation Question (per RD0 Standard 8 — four required elements)

##### Element 1: What the framework formally provides

PA MHPA architecture provides PA-3 constituents in commitment contexts: (a) clear-and-convincing-evidence procedural protection (Addington-derived, statute-codified); (b) right to counsel at § 303 + § 304 hearings; (c) right to independent psychiatric evaluation; (d) habeas corpus availability; (e) Olmstead-derived right to community-based alternatives where appropriate; (f) Disability Rights Pennsylvania advocacy + investigation access; (g) post-2004 amendments procedural protections enhanced; (h) PA Act 169 psychiatric advance directives mechanism.

##### Element 2: What PA-3 constituents actually receive

Receipt is highly variable and structurally compressed:
- **Procedural protections formally maintained but operationally compressed** at § 303 hearings: 15-30 minute proceedings; minutes-before-hearing counsel meetings; structural disposition tilt
- **No pre-detention judicial review for § 302**: 120-hour involuntary detention can occur without prior judicial approval (warrant from county MH administrator only); peer states with pre-detention judicial review provide stronger procedural floor
- **§ 305 voluntary-to-involuntary conversion** undermines voluntary-admission incentive
- **Psychiatric boarding 24-72+ hours** in ED settings — outside the contemplation of MHPA (which assumes prompt psychiatric inpatient placement)
- **§ 304 → state-hospital placement queue** geographically removes long-term-committed PA-3 residents from family-contact range (civil §304 to Danville since 2019 [TC-05]; forensic to NSH)
- **Olmstead community-alternative availability** structurally constrained by community-services capacity (developed in SD3)
- **Black PA-3 residents disproportionately enter system through coercive pathways** (police-initiated § 302) and underutilize voluntary alternatives — racially-traceable cumulative-disadvantage chain

##### Element 3: What accounts for the gap

- **Statutory architecture itself**: PA MHPA's no-pre-detention-judicial-review for § 302 + § 305 conversion are constitutive features of the statute; reform requires legislative action, not enforcement
- **Operational capacity at § 303 hearings**: defender resourcing + judicial scheduling + evidence-presentation infrastructure; structural compression reflects court-system + defender-system capacity, not just protocol-design
- **Inpatient psychiatric capacity scarcity**: IMD exclusion + state-hospital reduction + private psychiatric facility consolidation (e.g., Hahnemann July 2019 closure) → boarding crisis
- **Voluntary-system capacity inadequacy**: SD3 community-services gap forces commitment-as-default for some categories of MH crisis
- **Race-disparate police-MH-crisis contact patterns**: upstream of MHPA; reflects policing patterns + voluntary-system underutilization combined
- **DOJ CRD + HHS OCR enforcement posture**: federal enforcement on Olmstead-and-commitment integration is the variable forward lever; Trump 2 admin posture variable [F-related]
- **NSH bed capacity** + state-hospital sustainability under PA fiscal pressures

##### Element 4: What the gap means for the representation analysis

D3 SD2 represents the sub-domain where the **legal architecture itself contributes to the representation gap** (vs. SD1 where the gap is primarily delivery-side). The MHPA architecture has constitutive features (no-pre-detention judicial review § 302; § 305 voluntary-to-involuntary conversion) that produce civil-liberties tensions not addressable by procedural reform within the statute. The architecture's permissive features can only be addressed by **legislative reform of MHPA** — a state-level political project requiring legislative-leadership engagement.

Federal-rep leverage points for SD2 are more limited than SD1: (1) **DOJ CRD + HHS OCR enforcement posture** on Olmstead-and-commitment integration; (2) **PAIMI funding adequacy for DRP** to support due-process advocacy; (3) **CMS CoP enforcement** on restraint/seclusion + facility quality at commitment-receiving facilities; (4) **Medicaid IMD exclusion** revisiting in any future federal Medicaid reform; (5) **MIOTCRA expansion** for crisis-system + diversion-system alternatives reducing commitment-as-default.

The DBHIDS Right Care + CRC infrastructure represents Philadelphia-specific innovation that reduces police-as-commitment-pathway by providing alternatives. Federal-rep advocacy for community-based crisis service expansion (988 implementation; mobile crisis funding; SAMHSA capacity) feeds back to reduce SD2 commitment volume by addressing the upstream voluntary-pathway underutilization.

---

### SD2 Gap Analysis


**Sub-Domain:** SD2 Civil Commitment & Involuntary Treatment
**Slug:** civCommit
**Date:** 2026-04-30
**Cycle:** RD2 first-pass; Gap Analysis SD2
**Authority:** RD2 §STEP 4; RD0 Standards 5.B/C/D/E
**Inputs:** track1-phase2-SD2; track2-phase2-SD2; substructure cross-cutting threads

---

#### §1 SD2 Gap Findings

##### G1-SD2-01: PA MHPA no-pre-detention-judicial-review for § 302 as architectural civil-liberties tension

**Characterization.** PA MHPA § 7301 authorizes 120-hour involuntary detention based on county MH administrator warrant (non-judicial); peer states require pre-detention judicial review for similar holds. The architectural feature is constitutive — addressable only by legislative reform — and produces a procedural floor below the strongest peer-state protections.

**Evidence trace.** T1P2 §5.1 (§ 302 architecture); T1P2 §5.5 (PA framework relative to peer states); T2P2 §5 Element 2 (no pre-detention judicial review).

**Evidence type:** [D] direct documentation of MHPA architecture.
**Confidence:** HIGH.
**Representation implication.** State-level legislative reform is the lever; federal-rep leverage limited to indirect (DOJ CRD enforcement; PAIMI funding for DRP advocacy).

---

##### G1-SD2-02: § 303 hearing operational compression as procedural-protection-erosion

**Characterization.** Right to counsel at § 303 hearings is statutorily maintained but operationally compressed: 15-30 minute proceedings; minutes-before-hearing defender-client meetings; structural disposition tilt toward continued commitment given evidence-presentation asymmetry. The procedural protection exists formally; its operational expression falls below intent.

**Evidence trace.** T1P2 §6.1 (Philadelphia Common Pleas commitment processing); T2P2 §4.2 + §4.3 + §5 Element 2.

**Evidence type:** [SD] structural derivation from documented operational pattern.
**Premises:** (1) 8-12K Philadelphia § 302 petitions per year; (2) ~60-70% transition rate to § 303 produces 4.8-8.4K hearings; (3) defender + judicial system capacity for adequate counsel preparation + evidence presentation at this volume requires resources documented as insufficient; (4) the compression is therefore predictable from capacity-vs.-volume mismatch.
**Confidence:** MEDIUM-HIGH; documented historically though current FY26 data [F-verify].
**Representation implication.** State + city-level lever (defender resourcing; judicial scheduling); federal-rep limited to PAIMI funding for DRP litigation pressure.

---

##### G1-SD2-03: Psychiatric boarding crisis as IMD-exclusion + state-hospital-saturation + private-bed-scarcity compound

**Characterization.** 24-72+ hour psychiatric boarding in Philadelphia EDs is documented systemic outcome of three compounding capacity failures: (a) Medicaid IMD exclusion (42 U.S.C. § 1396d(a)(B)) limiting federal Medicaid match for adult inpatient psychiatric facilities >16 beds; (b) PA state-hospital bed reduction over decades (current ~1,200-1,400 statewide; NSH = 375 forensic-only since 2019 per TC-05); (c) private psychiatric inpatient facility consolidation including Hahnemann July 2019 closure. The boarding crisis is not a procedural failure but a capacity failure transmitted to ED settings.

**Evidence trace.** T1P2 §2.5 (IMD exclusion); T2P2 §2.2 (psychiatric boarding); T2P2 §2.3 (NSH constraint); SD7 boarding analysis develops further.

**Evidence type:** [D] direct documentation of architecture + [SD] structural-derivation of compound effect.
**Confidence:** HIGH for the structural compound; MEDIUM-HIGH for current scale [F-verify FY26].
**Representation implication.** Federal-rep direct leverage at IMD exclusion modification (federal Medicaid reform); MIOTCRA + CCBHC expansion as community-services capacity inputs; state-level lever at state-hospital sustainability. Cross-cutting to SD7 synthesis.

---

##### G1-SD2-04: § 305 voluntary-to-involuntary conversion as voluntary-pathway-deterrent

**Characterization.** PA MHPA § 305 permits facility to convert voluntary-status patient to involuntary during the 72-hour discharge-notice window if § 302 criteria met. Operates as voluntary-admission deterrent: a constituent considering voluntary admission knows that voluntary status is conditional on continued cooperation; this deters voluntary engagement at population scale, reinforcing coercive-pathway entry.

**Evidence trace.** T1P2 §5.1 (§ 305 architecture); T2P2 §4.4 (voluntary-to-involuntary conversion).

**Evidence type:** [SD] structural derivation; the deterrent effect predicted from rational-actor model of voluntary-admission decisions.
**Premises:** (1) § 305 conversion is statutorily permitted; (2) constituents and family members aware of conversion risk; (3) voluntary-admission decision is rational-actor calculation incorporating discharge-flexibility; (4) conversion-risk reduces voluntary-admission rate.
**Confidence:** MEDIUM-HIGH; structurally predicted; specific PA-3 voluntary-admission-rate documentation [F-verify].
**Representation implication.** State-level legislative reform; federal-rep limited.

---

##### G1-SD2-05: Police-initiated § 302 racial disparity as cumulative-equity entry-pathway link

**Characterization.** Black PA-3 residents experience police-initiated § 302 commitment at rates 3-4x higher than White residents after controlling for clinical presentation, per national peer-reviewed research applied to Philadelphia's documented pattern. The racial disparity is the entry-pathway link in the cumulative-disadvantage chain that culminates at SD6 (PPS as functionally largest psychiatric facility) and SD7 synthesis.

**Evidence trace.** T1P2 §7 (cumulative racial disadvantage); T2P2 §2.4 (demographic breakdown); T2P2 §3 (sub-area variation); T2P2 §4.1 (entry-point routing); T2P2 §5 Element 2.

**Evidence type:** [SD] structural derivation from national research applied + Philadelphia documented pattern.
**Premises:** (1) National peer-reviewed research consistently documents 3-4x Black-vs.-White involuntary commitment rate gap; (2) Philadelphia demographic baseline 52% Black PA-3; (3) police-initiated § 302 vs. physician-initiated § 302 patterns racially disparate at population scale; (4) the chain compounds with voluntary-utilization underutilization documented in D2-V cumulative-equity finding.
**Confidence:** HIGH for the disparity pattern; specific PA-3 quantification [F-verify].
**Representation implication.** Multi-level: upstream voluntary-system-access expansion (D3 SD3); police-MH-crisis-response reform (Right Care expansion; CIT coverage); commitment-system reform (state legislative); federal-rep leverage at MIOTCRA crisis-system grants + DOJ CRD pattern-or-practice investigations.

---

##### G1-SD2-06: State-hospital geographic location burden as long-term commitment family-contact externality (revised per TC-05)

*Verification update (2026-04-30): The geographic externality finding holds but applies primarily to Danville State Hospital (Montour County) for civil §304 commitments since NSH civil section closed January 2019. NSH continues to apply for forensic restoration commitments. See Material Changes Summary TC-05.*

**Characterization.** Pennsylvania state-hospital placement for PA-3 residents creates structural family-contact burden along two distinct geographic axes since the January 2019 NSH civil-section closure: (a) **Civil §304 long-term commitments** now route to **Danville State Hospital (Montour County)** — approximately 150 miles from Philadelphia — substantially exacerbating the family-contact externality compared to the prior Norristown-routing era. (b) **Forensic restoration commitments + NGRI commitments** continue to route to **NSH (Montgomery County)**, with a forensic-bed-shortage backlog that pushes some defendants to extended jail-based wait (see G1-SD6-06). Both pathways create transit-cost + work-schedule + caregiving constraints on family contact.

**Evidence trace.** T1P2 §5.4 + §6 (NSH role; revised characterization per TC-05); T2P2 §3.5 (boundary-adjacent observations); pa.gov NSH agency page; Stantec / architecture+ Southeast Psychiatric Treatment Center planning documentation.

**Evidence type:** [D] direct documentation of geographic structure + [SD] structural-derivation of family-contact effect.
**Confidence:** HIGH.
**Representation implication.** State-level lever (state hospital geography; planned Southeast Psychiatric Treatment Center 270→420 beds, groundbreaking 2026, would partially relieve forensic backlog and reduce some Danville routing if civil scope expanded — but currently scoped as forensic facility). State-hospital-sustainability conversation is the primary lever; not a primary federal-rep lever.

---

#### §2 Cross-Cutting Gaps (G1-XC-) added at SD2

##### G1-XC-03: Coercive-entry pattern compounds with voluntary-pathway underutilization (cumulative-equity finding)

**Characterization.** D3 SD2's coercive-entry pattern (police-initiated § 302 racial disparity) compounds operationally with the voluntary-utilization underutilization documented in D2-V cumulative-equity finding. The compounded effect: Black PA-3 residents are systematically more likely to enter the BH system through § 302 than through outpatient. This compounding is the entry-pathway link in the racial-equity chain feeding SD6 + SD7 synthesis.

**Evidence trace.** D2-V cumulative-equity finding; T1P2 §7; G1-SD2-05.

**Evidence type:** [SD] structural derivation; cumulative across domains.
**Confidence:** HIGH for cross-domain pattern.
**Representation implication.** Cross-cutting; resolves at SD7 synthesis.

---

- 1 cross-cutting finding added (G1-XC-03)

## Sub-Domain 3: Community Mental Health & SUD Treatment Services

### SD3 Track 1 — Legal & Institutional Architecture


**Sub-Domain:** SD3 Community Mental Health & SUD Treatment Services
**Slug:** commMHSUD
**Date:** 2026-04-30
**Cycle:** RD2 first-pass; Track 1 Phase 2 SD3
**Authority:** RD2 §STEP 2 Phase 2; RD0 Standards 9, 10, 11; depth caps Standard 16.C
**Source pairing:** source-workspace_D3_2026-04-30.md

This document maps the legal architecture for community mental health services + clinical SUD treatment infrastructure in PA-3. Per substructure boundary update (DOMAIN_FRAMEWORK rev 2): SD3 covers community-services + clinical SUD treatment; D2-V SD7 covers harm reduction policy + Schedule III + overdose surveillance + § 856/Safehouse litigation; PROJECT_RECORD WL2 tracks the boundary.

The analytical move SD3 establishes: **The gap between Olmstead-mandated community services + EPSDT entitlement + SUPPORT Act IMD parity and operational capacity (waitlists; HPSA-MH; rate inadequacy; X-waiver-elimination-without-workforce-expansion) IS the central representation gap in D3; the SUD-MH treatment integration challenge compounds this.**

---

#### §1 Constitutional Authority (≤200 words)

ADA Title II + Section 504 + the *Olmstead* integration mandate (*Olmstead v. L.C.*, 527 U.S. 581 (1999)) ground the federal community-services obligation: states must provide community-based services in the most integrated setting appropriate when treatment professionals so determine, the affected person does not oppose, and reasonable accommodation is possible given resources. The Olmstead Plan operationalizes this at state level. 14th Amendment Due Process operative narrowly through *Youngberg* (treatment standards in committed settings — operative when § 304(f) outpatient commitment is invoked).

PA Const. Art. I § 1 (inherent rights) provides PA-specific procedural floor; state's parens patriae and police-power authorities ground community-services administrative architecture.

---

#### §2 Federal Statutory Layer

**Public Health Service Act Title XIX-B Mental Health Block Grant** (42 U.S.C. § 300x-1 et seq.) and **Substance Abuse Block Grant** (42 U.S.C. § 300x-21 et seq.). MHBG funds community MH for adults with SMI + children with SED; SABG funds SUD treatment + prevention. PA OMHSAS receives MHBG; PA DDAP receives SABG; both pass through to DBHIDS (consolidated at city level). **Statutory stability: STABLE**.

**Medicaid behavioral health entitlement** (42 U.S.C. § 1396 et seq.; HealthChoices state plan). Medicaid covers community MH + SUD services as state plan + EPSDT (children) + § 1115 waivers (incl. SUD IMD waiver). Operative through HealthChoices BH (CBH MCO) for Philadelphia. **Statutory stability: STABLE; rate-setting administrative**.

**21st Century Cures Act § 223 (CCBHC)** (P.L. 114-255). Federally-certified community BH provider model with 9 required service components funded via Medicaid PPS (cost-based per-visit); PA participates with multiple Philadelphia certifications. Bipartisan Safer Communities Act 2022 (P.L. 117-159) expanded; **separate $1B BSCA school-MH grant stream targeted for termination April 29, 2025 [TC-02]; CCBHC component of BSCA distinct from school-MH stream**. **Statutory stability: GROWING**.

**Olmstead enforcement framework** via ADA Title II + Section 504 + DOJ CRD + HHS OCR enforcement. **Stability: STABLE textually; enforcement variable**.

**SUPPORT Act IMD parity for SUD** (P.L. 115-271 § 1012; 42 U.S.C. § 1396n(l)). Partial Medicaid IMD-exclusion erosion for SUD treatment in facilities up to 16 beds; 1115 IMD waivers extend further. **Stability: STABLE**.

**Methadone OTP framework** (42 C.F.R. Part 8). DEA + SAMHSA jointly regulate OTP operations; PA OTPs concentrated in specific geographic locations. **Stability: STABLE**.

**Consolidated Appropriations Act 2023 X-waiver elimination** (P.L. 117-328 § 1262 of Division FF). Effective immediately upon enactment Dec 29, 2022. Any DEA-registered practitioner can prescribe buprenorphine without prior special waiver. **Implementation gap [F-prospective]**: legal change has not produced proportionate workforce expansion; supply-side shortage persists.

**ACA EHB MH/SUD inclusion** (42 U.S.C. § 18022(b)(1)(E)). Mandatory inclusion in qualified health plans + individual market + Medicaid expansion population. **Stability: STABLE post-Braidwood**.

**EPSDT** (42 U.S.C. § 1396d(r)). Medicaid entitlement for children under 21 to all medically necessary services; covers community MH services for SED children + SUD treatment for adolescents. **Stability: STABLE**.

**HRSA HPSA-MH designation framework** + **NHSC loan repayment**. Operative for community MH workforce capacity in HPSA-designated areas including PA-3 sub-areas.

**FQHC § 330 integrated BH** (42 U.S.C. § 254b). Per D2-V S106-V: funding extended only through December 2026; OBBBA Medicaid cuts threaten multi-year horizon.

---

#### §3 Federal Regulatory Layer

- **42 C.F.R. Part 438** Medicaid managed care: CCBHC PPS rules; network adequacy floors; parity for Medicaid MCOs.
- **42 C.F.R. Part 8** OTP regulation: licensure; clinical operations; Schedule II prescribing requirements.
- **42 C.F.R. Part 96** block grant administrative provisions (MHBG + SABG).
- **42 C.F.R. § 438.68** network adequacy specifically.
- **45 C.F.R. Parts 92, 93** HHS Section 504/ADA implementation including Olmstead in HHS-funded programs.

---

#### §4 Federal Agency Layer

**SAMHSA**: MHBG + SABG administration; CCBHC certification + grants; SOR (State Opioid Response) discretionary grants. **Vulnerability: HIGH** [F11 → resolved per TC-06]: comprehensive capacity erosion verified (AHA reorganization March 27, 2025; >50% staff reduction; January 2026 grant-termination/reversal episode).

**CMS**: Medicaid MCO oversight (CBH parity + network adequacy); CCBHC PPS rule administration; IMD-exclusion guidance; 1115 SUD IMD waiver approvals. **Vulnerability: MODERATE-HIGH** [F13 → resolved per TC-03]; OBBBA implementation phases FY28-34; CCBHC stream protected by exemption from new cost-sharing; IMD framework unchanged.

**HRSA**: HPSA-MH designations; NHSC loan repayment; FQHC § 330 + integrated BH; THCGME residency. **Vulnerability: MODERATE**.

**DEA**: OTP registration + Schedule II oversight; X-waiver elimination administrative implementation. **Vulnerability: MODERATE**; cannabis Schedule III implementation [F-related D4 cross-ref].

**HHS OCR + DOJ CRD**: Olmstead enforcement co-authority. **Vulnerability: MODERATE-HIGH** under Trump 2 admin [F-related].

---

#### §5 State Layer

**PA Code Title 55 Chapter 5230** PROS (Psychiatric Rehabilitation and Recovery Services). Medicaid-covered psychiatric rehabilitation; skills training + community integration. **Stability: STABLE**.

**PA Code Title 55 + Title 50** community MH service standards generally; ACT team certification standards.

**PA Drug and Alcohol Service System Act** (71 P.S. § 1690.101 et seq.). Counties as SCAs; DDAP as state authority; **DBHIDS uniquely combines SCA + county MH authority**.

**PA Act 30 of 2020** BH-physical health Medicaid integration mandate. Implementation status [F9/P2].

**PA Medicaid HealthChoices Behavioral Health** delivery system. Multi-MCO PH; **single-MCO BH in Philadelphia (CBH)** [D2-V S131].

**OMHSAS** + **DDAP** state agencies; bifurcation at state level reconciled by DBHIDS at local level.

**Disability Rights Pennsylvania (DRP)**: PAIMI grantee; community-services integration advocacy.

PA PROS coverage breadth and DBHIDS SCA-MH consolidation are PA-state framework distinctive features beyond federal floor.

---

#### §6 Local Layer

##### 6.1 DBHIDS contract network architecture

DBHIDS operates as state-designated county MH authority + SCA for SUD. Contract network of 200+ providers including:

- **Major BH providers**: Horizon House; Resources for Human Development; NHS Human Services (closed/consolidated 2018-2019, partial reorganization); JEVS Human Services; Public Health Management Corporation (PHMC); Salvation Army Behavioral Health; Bridge Way (Tower Health affiliate)
- **Specialty SUD providers**: Gaudenzia (largest PA SUD residential network); Self Help Movement; NorthEast Treatment Centers; Eagleville Hospital (just outside Philadelphia)
- **Methadone OTPs**: NET Lehigh + others; geographic distribution concentrated in specific tracts; PA-3 OTP density [F-verify]
- **FQHC integrated BH**: PHMC; Esperanza Health Center (adjacent); Drexel 11th Street Family Health; Philadelphia FIGHT (integrating BH with HIV care); Spectrum Health Services
- **ACT teams**: 15-20 across providers; capacity ~$15-25K per person annually; waitlists 2-3K unserved estimate

##### 6.2 PROS sites + outpatient MH program network

~50-60 outpatient MH programs in DBHIDS network [F-verify]; PROS sites; partial hospitalization + intensive outpatient programs; recovery community centers (~300-400 certified peer specialists).

##### 6.3 CRC + Right Care + crisis residential

- **CRC**: 1229 N. 3rd Street; 24/7 walk-in; ~25-30 capacity; ~20-25K visits/yr [F-verify]
- **Right Care**: DBHIDS-PPD interagency mobile crisis + co-responder; partial geographic + 24/7 coverage [F3/P1]
- **Crisis residential** (alternative to inpatient): consistently full per documentation [F-verify capacity]

##### 6.4 Children's BH operational components (SD4 cross-ref)

CASSP office; BHRS provider network; school-based behavioral health (SBBH) + ESSER funding [F2].

##### 6.5 Home Rule authority

DBHIDS direct federal-grant relationships (MHBG, SABG, SAMHSA discretionary, HRSA grants); CBH nonprofit-MCO authority; Right Care interagency authority; CASSP coordination authority. Limits: cannot supersede PA OMHSAS clinical standards; cannot relax MHPAEA; subject to OMHSAS + CMS oversight.

---

#### §7 Cumulative Racial Disadvantage

Provider-density geography + workforce-cultural-competency gap + voluntary-utilization underutilization compound at SD3:

**Provider geography**: HPSA-MH designations cluster in North/Northwest Core + parts of Northwest Philadelphia + tracts of West Philadelphia outside University City — same neighborhoods documented as redlined-mapped (HOLC 1937) and Black-population-concentrated. The community-MH-services geography reproduces the cumulative-disadvantage pattern documented across D2 verification.

**Workforce cultural competency**: provider workforce historically diverged from population demographics; therapists of color, Spanish/Vietnamese/Arabic language capacity, trauma-informed care for Black communities all below proportional levels. Effect compounds the geography: even where providers are present, cultural-mismatch reduces voluntary utilization by Black PA-3 residents.

**Voluntary-utilization underutilization** chain (D2-V cumulative-equity): Black adults receive voluntary outpatient MH at lower rates despite similar/higher MH burden; reasons include schizophrenia overdiagnosis (Gara et al. 2019 AJOP [F-verify citation]), Tuskegee-effect distrust (D2-V cross-ref), historical exclusion from CMHCs, cultural-mismatch, geographic. The underutilization is racially-traceable, not coincidental.

**SD3-specific implication**: the community-services capacity gap is borne disproportionately by populations who are simultaneously most likely to need services AND least likely to access them voluntarily. This is the cumulative-disadvantage chain operating at SD3; community-services investment alone (without provider-diversity + cultural-competency + community-trust-rebuilding) does not resolve.

---

#### §8 Anchor Institution Thread

**DBHIDS contract network** as system anchor: regulator (contractor management) + regulated (state oversight); resource (system steward) + burden (fiscal authority); structural beneficiary (institutional persistence) + excluded-constituent advocate (asymmetric).

**Major BH providers (Horizon House, RHD, JEVS, PHMC)**: regulator-influencing through service-provision standard-setting; resource (service capacity) + burden (rate-driven sustainability); structural beneficiary (Medicaid + grant funding) + excluded-constituent service population.

**FQHC integrated BH (PHMC, Esperanza, Drexel 11th Street, FIGHT)**: regulator-influencing through HRSA + sliding-fee; resource (integrated BH for under-insured + Medicaid); structural beneficiary (340B + § 330 funding) + excluded-constituent service.

**Penn, CHOP, Drexel BH services** in University City: nominally high-resource; access-asymmetry (commercial/Medicare prioritized; Medicaid + uninsured access constrained).

**Major SUD residential providers (Gaudenzia)**: regulator (DBHIDS contract); regulated (state SCA + DEA); resource (residential SUD capacity) + burden (rate adequacy); structural beneficiary + excluded-constituent service.

---

### SD3 Track 2 — Outcomes, Geography & Representation


**Sub-Domain:** SD3 Community Mental Health & SUD Treatment Services
**Slug:** commMHSUD
**Date:** 2026-04-30
**Cycle:** RD2 first-pass; Track 2 Phase 2 SD3
**Authority:** RD2 §STEP 3 Phase 2; RD0 Standards 1, 6, 7, 8, 13

---

#### §1 Data Provenance Statement

Philadelphia-specific direct: PA-3 ACS demographic baseline; D2-V cumulative racial-equity findings; FQHC § 330 funding posture (D2-V S106-V); HealthChoices BH single-MCO Philadelphia model (D2-V S131); DBHIDS contract network composition (DBHIDS public reporting). National benchmarks applied with structural inference: NSDUH treatment-receipt rates; SAMHSA TEDS SUD treatment patterns; HPSA-MH designation methodology; ACT team national capacity benchmarks. Statistical caveats: ranges where exact numbers unavailable; F-flags for current-state.

---

#### §2 PA-3 Statistical Profile — Community MH + SUD Treatment

##### 2.1 Burden estimates (carry-forward from SD1 + D2)

- Adults with any MI in PA-3: ~130,000-145,000 (NSDUH applied)
- Adults with SMI in PA-3: ~35,000-42,000
- Adults with SUD in PA-3: per D2-V SD7 + NSDUH structural inference, ~80,000-100,000 with substance use disorder (alcohol + illicit + Rx); subset with opioid use disorder ~15,000-25,000 [F-verify]
- Co-occurring SMI+SUD in PA-3: ~20-25% of SMI population have co-occurring SUD = ~7,000-10,000 dual-diagnosis adults

##### 2.2 Service capacity baseline

- CBH Medicaid coverage: ~420,000 Philadelphia-wide recipients [verified per TC-10 from FY26 DBHIDS testimony]; PA-3 share per proportional methodology consistent with D2 SD6 framing
- DBHIDS network: ~50-60 outpatient MH programs; 15-20 ACT teams; multiple OTPs; PROS sites
- ACT team waitlist: ~2,000-3,000 unserved eligible across providers [F-verify]
- Outpatient MH wait time: 4-8 weeks adult; 3-6 months child/adolescent specialty [F-verify]
- HPSA-MH designations: multiple PA-3 census tracts in North/Northwest Core + parts West + parts Northwest [F-verify HRSA list]
- OTP capacity: PA OTPs concentrated regionally; PA-3 OTP density [F-verify]
- Buprenorphine prescribing post-X-waiver elimination: implementation gap; growth below expected [F-verify]

##### 2.3 SUD treatment specifics

PA SUD treatment system: SAMHSA SABG → DDAP → DBHIDS as SCA → contracted providers. Major Philadelphia SUD residential: Gaudenzia (largest network); Self Help Movement; NorthEast Treatment Centers. ASAM-level continuum theoretically available; capacity at higher levels (residential, MAT-integrated outpatient) constrained by rate inadequacy + workforce gaps.

---

#### §3 Geographic Variation

##### 3.1 North/Northwest Philadelphia Core
Highest SD3 service-need + lowest SD3 service-access. HPSA-MH coverage extensive. Provider density lowest. SUD service-utilization weighted toward Kensington-area providers (outside PA-3 strict boundary; PA-3 residents travel for services). Outpatient MH wait times longest in this sub-area.

##### 3.2 West Philadelphia Core
Bifurcated. University City anchor concentration (Penn BH, Drexel BH, CHOP) — nominal high density but commercial-priority access asymmetry. Adjacent neighborhoods (Mantua, Kingsessing, Cobbs Creek) closer to North Core pattern. PHMC + Spectrum FQHC integrated BH presence partial mitigation.

##### 3.3 Northwest Philadelphia
Heterogeneous. Higher-income tracts adequate provider access; lower-income tracts (Germantown, Stenton, West Oak Lane, Wister) closer to North Core pattern. PHMC FQHC sites partial mitigation.

##### 3.4 South/Southwest Philadelphia
Better access pattern than North Core. PHMC + Esperanza-adjacent + Jefferson + Penn Center City accessible. SUD treatment residential less concentrated; MAT outpatient through FQHCs available.

##### 3.5 Boundary-adjacent
Kensington (PA-1/PA-2): SUD treatment epicenter; Prevention Point; SOR-funded sites; PA-3 residents access services here. NET Lehigh (Northeast Phila) OTP serves portion of PA-3 residents.

---

#### §4 Aggregate Pathway Tracing

**Pathway A: Medicaid-enrolled PA-3 adult with SMI seeking outpatient MH**:
1. Call CBH Member Services
2. Routed to in-network outpatient program; appointment 4-8 weeks out
3. Initial appointment: medication management + therapy referral
4. Ongoing care: phantom-provider problem (provider full; new patient closed); session cap NQTL 8-10 sessions vs. medical/surgical no-cap; rate-driven workforce turnover
5. ACT team referral if SMI + high-utilization indicators: waitlist 6+ months
6. PROS access for psychiatric rehabilitation if appropriate

Breakdown: Step 2 wait time pushes some to crisis pathway by attrition; Step 4 phantom-provider + cultural-mismatch.

**Pathway B: Medicaid-enrolled PA-3 adult with OUD seeking MAT**:
1. Decision: MAT through OTP (methadone) vs. OBOT (buprenorphine in primary care + BH)
2. OTP: limited geographic locations; daily dosing requirement burdens transportation + work; PA-3 residents may travel to Kensington-area or NET Lehigh
3. OBOT: any DEA-registered prescriber post-X-waiver elimination; supply remains limited; FQHC integrated BH provides some access; private PCPs uncommon in Medicaid
4. Co-occurring SMI: DBHIDS-CBH integration through SCA-MH consolidation theoretically supports dual-diagnosis treatment; operational integration variable

Breakdown: OTP geographic + daily-dosing burden; OBOT supply-side gap despite legal-reform; dual-diagnosis coordination operational gaps.

**Pathway C: Uninsured PA-3 adult with SMI or SUD**:
1. FQHC integrated BH (PHMC, Spectrum, FIGHT, Drexel 11th Street) sliding fee
2. DBHIDS direct-funded base services through county-base-service stream — chronically capacity-constrained
3. Crisis-only access via CRC + ED + EMTALA — no continuity guarantee

Breakdown: FQHC + base-service capacity at every step; episodic-care-without-continuity pattern; insurance-eligibility cycling produces churn.

---

#### §5 Representation Question

**Element 1 (formal provision)**: Olmstead community-services mandate; Medicaid behavioral health entitlement (MH + SUD); EPSDT for children; CCBHC PPS; PROS Medicaid coverage; SAMHSA SABG SUD treatment; SUPPORT Act IMD parity for SUD; X-waiver elimination removing prior-restriction on buprenorphine.

**Element 2 (actual receipt)**: Medicaid-enrolled receive structurally comprehensive coverage but face 4-8 week wait times, 2-3K ACT team waitlist, phantom-provider network adequacy, OTP geographic burden, OBOT supply-side gap. Uninsured receive crisis-only access without continuity.

**Element 3 (account for the gap)**: federal funding inadequacy; Medicaid rate inadequacy driving workforce shortage; HPSA-MH designations confirming federally-recognized provider shortage; cumulative-disadvantage geography producing provider-density gap aligned with redlined-geography; X-waiver elimination not translating to workforce expansion at expected scale; ERISA preemption blocking state-level commercial-insurance reach for self-funded employees; OBBBA Medicaid cuts pressure forward.

**Element 4 (representation analysis meaning)**: SD3 represents the **central representation gap of D3** — the gap between Olmstead/EPSDT/SUPPORT Act mandates and operational community-services capacity. Federal-rep leverage points: (1) MHBG + SABG appropriations; (2) Medicaid rate-setting; (3) CCBHC expansion; (4) HRSA NHSC + workforce diversity programs; (5) IMD exclusion modification (federal Medicaid reform); (6) SAMHSA capacity restoration (Pattern 1 reversal); (7) OBBBA Medicaid cut implementation pace. SD3 is the federal-rep-leverage-richest sub-domain in D3.

---

Same as T1P2 SD3 §9 plus:
- F-verify: SUD treatment capacity by ASAM level current PA-3
- F-verify: dual-diagnosis program count + utilization in DBHIDS network
- F-verify: PROS utilization rate FY26

- D3 SD3: SD3 as federal-rep-leverage-richest subdomain in D3

#### §1 SD3 Gap Findings

##### G1-SD3-01: ACT team waitlist as Olmstead-mandate operational shortfall
**Characterization.** ~2-3K PA-3 residents with SMI eligible for ACT (Assertive Community Treatment) but unserved due to capacity constraints. ACT is the highest-intensity community-based service for SMI; waitlist directly contradicts Olmstead community-integration mandate.
**Evidence trace.** T1P2 §6.1; T2P2 §2.2 + §4.

**Confidence:** MEDIUM-HIGH; specific FY26 number [F-verify].
**Implication.** Federal-rep leverage at SAMHSA ACT expansion grants + Medicaid rate-setting + CCBHC PPS expansion (CCBHCs include ACT-equivalent services).

##### G1-SD3-02: Outpatient MH wait time as network-adequacy operational failure
**Characterization.** 4-8 week adult wait time + 3-6 month child/adolescent specialty wait time exceeds reasonable network-adequacy benchmarks. CBH formally compliant; operationally degraded.
**Evidence trace.** T2P2 §2.2; §4 Pathway A.
**Type:** [SD]; **Premises:** clinical urgency benchmarks + documented wait time + adequacy regulation interpretation.
**Confidence:** MEDIUM-HIGH; FY26 [F-verify].
**Implication.** Federal lever at Sept 2024 MHPAEA Final Rule network-adequacy NQTL; CMS oversight via 42 C.F.R. § 438.68; state OMHSAS enforcement.

##### G1-SD3-03: HPSA-MH provider shortage compound with cumulative-disadvantage geography
**Characterization.** HPSA-MH designations cluster in same PA-3 sub-areas (North/Northwest Core; parts West; parts Northwest) that are documented as redlined-mapped + Black-population-concentrated. Provider-density geography reproduces cumulative-disadvantage pattern.
**Evidence trace.** T1P2 §7; T2P2 §3; D2-V cumulative-equity finding.
**Type:** [D] designation data + [SD] derivation of correlation.
**Confidence:** HIGH.
**Implication.** Cross-cutting; resolves at SD7 synthesis. Federal-rep leverage at HRSA NHSC + workforce-diversity programs.

##### G1-SD3-04: X-waiver elimination implementation gap
**Characterization.** CAA 2023 X-waiver elimination removed prior restriction on buprenorphine prescribing; legal change has not produced proportionate workforce expansion. Supply-side shortage persists because rate inadequacy + training + cultural factors all separately constrain prescribing growth.
**Evidence trace.** T1P2 §2; T2P2 §4 Pathway B.
**Type:** [SD]; **Premises:** X-waiver elimination effective Dec 2022 + 3+ years post-elimination + national prescribing data + Philadelphia-specific data [F-verify].
**Confidence:** MEDIUM-HIGH for the gap pattern.
**Implication.** Federal-rep leverage at: (a) Medicaid OBOT rate-setting; (b) HRSA workforce programs; (c) state-level licensure simplification.

##### G1-SD3-05: Bifurcated SUD-MH treatment integration challenge
**Characterization.** Co-occurring SMI+SUD population (~7-10K dual-diagnosis adults in PA-3) faces the OMHSAS-DDAP state-level bifurcation reconciled at local level by DBHIDS SCA-MH consolidation; operational dual-diagnosis treatment integration variable. Patients fall through coordination gaps.
**Evidence trace.** T1P2 §5; T2P2 §4 Pathway B; substructure MC1 boundary.
**Type:** [SD]; **Premises:** state-level architectural bifurcation + federal-level SAMHSA-CSAT/CSAP separate centers + local-level DBHIDS consolidation + operational coordination challenges across providers.
**Confidence:** HIGH for architectural; MEDIUM-HIGH for operational quantification.
**Implication.** State + federal lever at integration; CCBHC nine-component model designed to address; Sept 2024 MHPAEA Rule + Act 30 of 2020 implementation.

##### G1-SD3-06: Phantom-provider network adequacy gap
**Characterization.** CBH network formally meets 200+ contracted providers but operational capacity reduced by phantom-provider problem (providers in network not accepting new patients; providers at reduced capacity; nominal participation without active practice).
**Evidence trace.** T1P2 §6.1; T2P2 §4 Pathway A Step 4. [Cross-ref G1-SD1-03.]
**Type:** [SD] derivation from documented pattern.
**Confidence:** MEDIUM-HIGH.
**Implication.** Sept 2024 MHPAEA Final Rule network-adequacy NQTL enforcement; CMS oversight; OMHSAS state enforcement.

##### G1-SD3-07: OTP geographic distribution + daily-dosing burden
**Characterization.** Methadone OTPs concentrated in specific Philadelphia geographic locations (e.g., Kensington-adjacent for SUD-treatment historical reasons); daily dosing requirement burdens PA-3 residents with transportation + work-schedule conflicts; PA-3 residents may travel to non-PA-3 OTPs.
**Evidence trace.** T2P2 §3.5 + §4 Pathway B.
**Type:** [D] + [SD].
**Confidence:** HIGH structural; MEDIUM specific PA-3 quantification [F-verify].
**Implication.** Federal-rep leverage at SAMHSA OTP regulation modernization (e.g., take-home dosing rule changes; mobile OTP authorization); state SCA-level OTP siting policy.
