## Sub-Domain 4: Children's Behavioral Health

### SD4 Track 1 — Legal & Institutional Architecture


**Sub-Domain:** SD4 Children's Behavioral Health
**Slug:** childBH
**Date:** 2026-04-30
**Cycle:** RD2 first-pass; Track 1 Phase 2 SD4
**Authority:** RD2 §STEP 2 Phase 2 structural template; RD0 Standards 9, 10, 11; depth caps Standard 16.C
**Source pairing:** source-workspace_D3_2026-04-30.md §§1-7; SD4-specific entries
**Regime:** training-data primary with [D2-V] inheritances; current-state items F-flagged

This document maps the legal architecture for children's behavioral health services in PA-3 — the EPSDT entitlement core; the parallel IDEA Emotional Disturbance education architecture; the FFPSA + Title IV-E child welfare-BH intersection; the CASSP/BHRS state framework; the Philadelphia DBHIDS-CASSP-SDP-DHS multi-system operational layer.

The analytical move SD4 establishes: **Pediatric BH operates under a distinct entitlement architecture (EPSDT) that is structurally stronger than the adult community-services framework analyzed at SD3, but the multi-system youth integration challenge (Medicaid + schools + child welfare + juvenile justice) reproduces operational shortfall at the integration seams; ESSER III sustainability + RTF placement geography + BHRS-quality-shift are the three first-order representation gaps within an otherwise robust formal architecture.**

---

#### §1 Constitutional Authority

The constitutional foundation for children's BH operates at three intersecting layers. *Olmstead v. L.C.*, 527 U.S. 581 (1999) extends to children: ADA Title II + Section 504 require community-based services in the most integrated setting, including residential-vs.-community placement decisions for children with serious emotional disturbance (SED). 14th Amendment Due Process operates in residential placement contexts (procedural protections in dependent-court placement; *Santosky v. Kramer*, 455 U.S. 745 (1982) for parental-rights termination intersecting with severe-MH placement decisions). IDEA's underlying constitutional grounding is Spending Clause + Section 504 ADA harmonization, with FAPE entitlement enforceable through state-level due-process hearing.

PA Const. Art. I § 1 (inherent rights) provides PA-specific procedural floor; state's parens patriae authority over dependent children + minor-MH-treatment decisions grounds CASSP + Act 147 of 2004 (minor consent for outpatient MH at age 14) state architecture. Constitutional gap relevant to SD4: the constitutional minimums for children's MH placement are met by federal + PA architecture, but the operational implementation at the schools-Medicaid-child-welfare-juvenile-justice intersection produces gaps without violating the federal floor.

---

#### §2 Federal Statutory Layer

##### 2.1 EPSDT — the central pediatric BH entitlement

42 U.S.C. § 1396d(r). EPSDT (Early and Periodic Screening, Diagnostic, and Treatment) is the Medicaid entitlement for individuals under 21 to all *medically necessary* services to correct or ameliorate physical or mental conditions, regardless of whether the service is included in the state Medicaid plan for adults. For pediatric BH this is the constitutive architectural feature: Medicaid-enrolled children have a federal statutory entitlement to behavioral health services without the dollar caps, session limits, or state-plan limits applicable to adults. CMS guidance (e.g., the 2014 CMCS Informational Bulletin on EPSDT BH; subsequent guidance series) operationalizes the entitlement. Operative through HealthChoices Behavioral Health (CBH MCO) for Philadelphia children. **Statutory stability: STABLE; rate-setting + medical-necessity-determination administrative**.

##### 2.2 IDEA + Emotional Disturbance category

20 U.S.C. § 1400 et seq.; Emotional Disturbance ("ED") at 34 C.F.R. § 300.8(c)(4). IDEA Part B entitles children with disabilities to a Free Appropriate Public Education (FAPE) with related services where required by the IEP, including counseling and psychological services. The Emotional Disturbance eligibility category — defined by inability to learn unexplained by other factors; inability to build/maintain relationships; inappropriate behaviors/feelings; pervasive unhappiness/depression; physical symptoms or fears associated with school problems — is the school-side route into BH services. ED placement decisions navigate inclusion (general-education + supports) vs. separate-program placement (emotional-support classroom; Approved Private School (APS); RTF-with-school). **Statutory stability: STABLE; ED eligibility-determination + placement-decision administrative + due-process-hearing-protected**.

##### 2.3 FFPSA + Title IV-E prevention services + QRTP constraint

Family First Prevention Services Act (P.L. 115-123 Title VII Subtitle A, 2018; effective 2019-2021 implementation). Two structural changes operative for children's BH: (a) Title IV-E reimbursement available for prevention services (mental health treatment; substance abuse treatment; in-home parent skill-based programs) for candidates for foster care under approved evidence-based programs; (b) Title IV-E reimbursement for non-foster-family placements (residential treatment) restricted to Qualified Residential Treatment Programs (QRTP) meeting specific criteria, with court-review requirements at 60 days and time-limited federal funding. The QRTP constraint reshaped the residential treatment landscape: facilities not meeting QRTP standards lost Title IV-E reimbursement; placements over 60 days face heightened review. **Statutory stability: STABLE textually; implementation effects continuing to develop**.

##### 2.4 CAPTA + Title IV-E child welfare-BH intersection

Child Abuse Prevention and Treatment Act (42 U.S.C. § 5101 et seq.) and Title IV-E foster care + adoption assistance frameworks coordinate child-welfare-system entry with mental health screening + service obligations. CAPTA-funded screening at intake; Title IV-E-eligible children (foster + kinship care) receive Medicaid coverage of BH services through EPSDT; the dependent-court system (Philadelphia Family Court) routes children between placement settings + BH service plans.

##### 2.5 CCBHC pediatric services

21st Century Cures Act § 223 (P.L. 114-255). CCBHC certification includes pediatric services as one of the nine required service components. Bipartisan Safer Communities Act 2022 (P.L. 117-159) expanded CCBHC nationally; PA participates with Philadelphia certifications. Pediatric CCBHC services include school-based BH, family therapy, intensive outpatient, crisis services. **Note per TC-02:** BSCA's separate $1B school-MH grant stream (School-Based Mental Health Services + Mental Health Service Professional Demonstration) targeted for termination via April 29, 2025 ED letters; CCBHC stream is structurally distinct from school-MH stream. **Statutory stability: GROWING (CCBHC); TARGETED FOR TERMINATION (BSCA school-MH)**.

##### 2.6 ACA § 2713 USPSTF preventive services + adolescent BH screening

42 U.S.C. § 300gg-13. ACA Section 2713 requires Grade A and Grade B USPSTF-recommended preventive services without cost-sharing in covered plans. USPSTF Grade B recommendations include adolescent depression screening (12-18) and adolescent anxiety screening (8-18). Per D2-V verified output (S134/MC-V-1; *Braidwood Management v. Becerra* resolved June 27, 2025; mandate upheld 6-3): the USPSTF recommendation framework is preserved. **Statutory stability: STABLE post-Braidwood; specific USPSTF recommendation persistence subject to USPSTF-internal-process changes**.

##### 2.7 ESSER III sustainability framework + BSCA school-MH funding freeze

*Verification update (2026-04-30): Prior pass identified ESSER III post-expiration replacement-funding as the primary verification flag. Verification surfaces a parallel disruption: Bipartisan Safer Communities Act (BSCA, P.L. 117-159, June 2022) allocated $1B for school-based MH grants (School-Based Mental Health Services + Mental Health Service Professional Demonstration), which the Trump administration moved to terminate effective with letters issued April 29, 2025. Both ESSER III replacement-funding and BSCA continuation-funding are now disrupted. See Material Changes Summary TC-02.*

Coronavirus State and Local Fiscal Recovery Funds and Elementary and Secondary School Emergency Relief III (ARPA 2021; P.L. 117-2). ESSER III provided ~$122B nationally for K-12 districts with significant share allowed for school-based BH personnel + services. **Obligation deadline September 30, 2024; expenditure deadline January 28, 2025** (extension to March 28, 2026 with US ED approval per US ED ESSER III liquidation extension guidance).

**BSCA school-MH funding (P.L. 117-159, 2022):** $1B authorized; ~260 grant recipients across 49 states. Trump administration termination letters April 29, 2025 cited "violation of the letter or purpose of Federal civil rights law" in grant applications referencing diversity in MH workforce. Per KFF (Sept 2025), federal grant share for school MH dropped from 53% (2021-22) to 33% (2024-25). 16-state AG lawsuit filed November 2025 challenging termination as APA violation. Post-expiration replacement-funding picture for both streams [F2 → partially resolved per TC-02; SDP-specific FY26 workforce numbers remain unverified per UV-02].

##### 2.8 Other federal anchors

- **Medicaid § 1115 SUD waivers + IMD parity for SUD** (P.L. 115-271 § 1012): SUPPORT Act partial IMD-exclusion erosion benefits adolescent SUD treatment (adolescent treatment in residential settings <16 beds; adolescent OTPs).
- **Indian Child Welfare Act** (25 U.S.C. § 1901 et seq.): operative narrowly for Native American children in dependency proceedings with BH-placement implications.

---

#### §3 Federal Regulatory Layer

- **34 C.F.R. Part 300** — IDEA Part B implementing regulations: ED eligibility; FAPE; LRE (least restrictive environment); IEP development; due process. § 300.8(c)(4) ED definition is the operative eligibility rule.
- **42 C.F.R. Part 438** — Medicaid managed care: applies to CBH including EPSDT-coverage adequacy for children; pediatric network adequacy.
- **42 C.F.R. Part 441 Subpart B** — EPSDT regulatory framework.
- **45 C.F.R. Part 1355-1357** — Title IV-E foster care + adoption assistance regulations including FFPSA QRTP implementation.
- **45 C.F.R. Part 1340** — CAPTA implementation.
- **42 C.F.R. § 438.68** — network adequacy with pediatric-specific provider type considerations.
- **42 C.F.R. Part 483 Subpart G** — psychiatric residential treatment facility requirements (overlaps with PA RTF regulation).

---

#### §4 Federal Agency Layer

##### 4.1 SAMHSA

Children's BH portfolio includes the System of Care discretionary grants (CASSP descendant); Project AWARE (school-based mental health awareness); CMHI (Comprehensive Mental Health Initiative for children) historic grants; National Child Traumatic Stress Network (NCTSN) coordinator role. Pediatric BH technical assistance + grant-making.

**Administrative vulnerability: HIGH** [F11 → resolved per TC-06]. Per verified comprehensive SAMHSA capacity erosion: AHA reorganization March 27, 2025; >50% staff reduction; **Children's Mental Health Initiative team on administrative leave**; Minority Fellowship Program admin branch laid off; FY26 budget proposal block-grant consolidation. Pediatric BH grants are discretionary and structurally vulnerable to capacity erosion at the agency level. The SAMHSA pediatric BH role is more grant-and-TA than direct-regulatory; capacity erosion produces slower grant cycles + less robust TA, more so than acute service-delivery disruptions, but the cumulative effect on pediatric BH workforce + system-of-care expansion is real. The Children's Mental Health Initiative team being on administrative leave is particularly consequential for SD4.

##### 4.2 ED — OSEP

Department of Education Office of Special Education Programs administers IDEA. Vulnerability: federal Department of Education was subject to executive-branch dismantling efforts initiated 2025; the implementation of IDEA enforcement by ED OSEP + the timing of federal special-education monitoring (state performance plans; child-count reporting; complaint investigations) is in transition. **Vulnerability: MODERATE-HIGH**; specific operational status [F-verify P2].

##### 4.3 ACF

Administration for Children and Families (within HHS) administers FFPSA + CAPTA + Title IV-E. Implementation guidance + state plan reviews + QRTP designation oversight. **Vulnerability: MODERATE**.

##### 4.4 CMS

CMS oversees Medicaid + EPSDT + CCBHC PPS. Pediatric-specific oversight runs through Medicaid managed care reviews of EPSDT compliance. Per OBBBA implementation [F13 → resolved per TC-03], CMS pediatric Medicaid posture: EPSDT framework unchanged; state-directed payment caps and 6-month redetermination requirement (effective Dec 31, 2026) introduce administrative complexity; CCBHC pediatric framework protected by exemption from new cost-sharing. **Vulnerability: MODERATE-HIGH**.

##### 4.5 HHS OCR + DOJ CRD

Olmstead enforcement co-authority operative for pediatric residential vs. community placement decisions. **Vulnerability: MODERATE-HIGH** under Trump 2 admin [F-verify civil rights enforcement posture].

##### 4.6 HRSA

HRSA Maternal and Child Health Bureau Title V block grant; school-based health center program; pediatric integrated BH at FQHCs. **Vulnerability: MODERATE**.

The federal agency layer for pediatric BH spreads across more agencies than adult BH (SAMHSA + ED + ACF + CMS + HHS OCR/DOJ CRD + HRSA), reflecting the multi-system character of children's BH. The administrative vulnerability profile is correspondingly distributed: erosion at any single agency produces partial gaps; cumulative erosion across multiple agencies compounds.

---

#### §5 State Layer

##### 5.1 PA CASSP — Child and Adolescent Service System Program

CASSP is the system-of-care framework PA pioneered nationally (1980s; Beverly Long-led federal initiative with PA among earliest state implementers). System-of-care principles: child-centered + family-focused; community-based; culturally competent; least-restrictive-appropriate; multi-agency coordinated. Operationalized in PA through county-level CASSP coordinators + interagency teams (Child and Family Service Plans for individual children). PA's CASSP framework is a representational asset: the formal architecture for pediatric BH coordination is among the strongest state frameworks in the country.

##### 5.2 PA BHRS regulation — 55 Pa. Code Ch. 5240

Behavioral Health Rehabilitation Services (BHRS, formerly BHRS-EPSDT in older terminology). Medicaid-covered children's BH including therapeutic staff support, behavior specialist consultation, mobile therapy. Historical concern: BHRS authorizations skewed heavily toward paraprofessional therapeutic staff support (TSS) services delivered by minimally-credentialed staff, with comparatively under-utilization of evidence-based modalities. PA + DBHIDS shift toward family-based therapy (multi-systemic therapy MST; functional family therapy FFT; trigeminal-based family treatments) reflects course-correction. **Stability: STABLE; operational implementation continuing to evolve**.

##### 5.3 PA RTF regulation — 55 Pa. Code Ch. 3800

Residential Treatment Facility regulation: licensure standards; child-resident protections; programming requirements. Intersects with FFPSA QRTP designation: PA RTFs that meet QRTP standards retain Title IV-E reimbursement; those that don't lose federal match for IV-E-eligible children. Philadelphia RTF placement pattern: PA-3 children placed in RTFs are frequently placed *outside* Philadelphia (regional or out-of-state RTFs) due to in-city RTF capacity constraints + specialized-needs matching — this geographic pattern has racial-equity dimension developed in §7.

##### 5.4 PA Act 147 of 2004 — minor consent for outpatient MH

50 P.S. § 7201 permitting minors age 14+ to consent to outpatient MH treatment without parental consent. Operative for adolescent BH access; reduces parental-consent friction for adolescent care.

##### 5.5 OMHSAS + DDAP + DHS pediatric coordination

OMHSAS (within PA DHS) administers children's BH alongside adult; PA DHS Office of Children, Youth, and Families (OCYF) administers Title IV-E + CAPTA + FFPSA. Coordinated through interagency mechanisms; child welfare-BH interface is one of the operational seams where multi-system coordination challenges manifest.

##### 5.6 PA ESSER state administration

PA Department of Education distributed ESSER III subgrants to LEAs including SDP. Post-expiration state replacement-funding posture limited; PA general fund + state special education funding levels do not structurally replace ESSER III.

---

#### §6 Local Layer

##### 6.1 DBHIDS CASSP office + Children's Bureau

DBHIDS CASSP office coordinates Philadelphia children's BH services across CBH, Philadelphia DHS (the local child welfare agency, distinct from PA DHS), SDP, Philadelphia Family Court juvenile probation. Children and Family Service Plan development; interagency team meetings; CASSP coordinator function at the city scale. The DBHIDS-CBH-DHS-SDP coordination architecture is a local representational asset; operational capacity to coordinate at scale across the Philadelphia child Medicaid population (population scale [F-verify FY26 child Medicaid enrollment Philadelphia]) is documented as straining at intersection points.

##### 6.2 CBH pediatric services + EPSDT operational implementation

CBH manages EPSDT-covered BH for Medicaid-enrolled Philadelphia children. Pediatric outpatient MH wait times: 3-6 months for child/adolescent psychiatry specialty per documented pattern [F-verify FY26]. Pediatric BHRS authorizations through CBH; family-based therapy expansion; school-based BH service authorization through CBH for SDP-located services. CBH children's BH provider network includes pediatric specialty practices, FQHC integrated BH (PHMC, Drexel 11th Street, Philadelphia FIGHT pediatric), Children's Crisis Treatment Center (CCTC), and major pediatric BH organizations.

##### 6.3 School District of Philadelphia school-based BH

SDP operates school-based BH services across district-traditional-school enrollment [F-verify FY26 SDP enrollment count] through a combination of district-employed school counselors + social workers + nurses, contracted school-based BH providers (Children's Crisis Treatment Center; PMHCC; Children's Service Center; community BH agencies), and federal Title I + IDEA-funded supports. ESSER III funded substantial school-based BH expansion 2021-2024 [F2 partially resolved per TC-02]. **Parallel BSCA disruption per TC-02:** BSCA $1B school-MH grant stream targeted for termination via April 29, 2025 Education Department letters. SDP's IDEA Emotional Disturbance category placements include emotional-support classrooms, Approved Private Schools (APSs), and partial-day/full-day RTF programs with school components.

##### 6.4 Philadelphia DHS + dependent court intersection

Philadelphia DHS administers child welfare services including foster care, kinship care, in-home services. ~5-7K children in Philadelphia DHS dependent-court population [F-verify]; majority Medicaid-eligible; BH service plan integrated with placement plan. DHS-DBHIDS interagency coordination through CASSP framework + dependent court orders.

##### 6.5 CHOP pediatric BH + Drexel + Children's Crisis Treatment Center

- **CHOP**: pediatric inpatient psychiatry capacity (limited); pediatric ED with psychiatric capacity; pediatric BH outpatient (commercial-priority access pattern; Medicaid access constrained).
- **Drexel** (Hahnemann pediatric BH legacy mostly closed July 2019): residual pediatric BH services through St. Christopher's Hospital for Children.
- **Children's Crisis Treatment Center (CCTC)**: anchor pediatric BH provider; school-based BH; outpatient + intensive outpatient for children + adolescents.
- **Public Health Management Corporation pediatric BH**: integrated BH at FQHC sites including Drexel 11th Street Family Health.

##### 6.6 Home Rule authority

DBHIDS CASSP coordination authority; interagency MOU authority with SDP + Philadelphia DHS + Philadelphia Family Court; CBH children's BH authorization framework; pediatric crisis-services coordination authority. Limits: cannot supersede PA OMHSAS clinical standards or PA OCYF Title IV-E rules; cannot relax IDEA federal requirements; subject to PA + federal oversight.

---

#### §7 Cumulative Racial Disadvantage

Pediatric BH cumulative-disadvantage chain operates across four threads:

**ACE burden distribution**. Adverse Childhood Experiences (ACE) burden in PA-3 is documented above national average per D2-V cross-domain inheritance: poverty exposure, parental incarceration, parental MH/SUD, neighborhood violence, housing instability cluster in same PA-3 sub-areas (North/Northwest Core; parts West; parts Northwest) that are redlined-mapped + Black-population-concentrated. ACE burden translates structurally to higher pediatric BH need at the population level in these neighborhoods.

**RTF placement outside Philadelphia + family-separation racial dimension**. Philadelphia children placed in RTFs are frequently placed outside the city due to in-city RTF capacity + matching-specialized-needs constraints. National + PA literature documents Black children disproportionately represented in RTF placement and in placement at greater geographic distance from family. The combined effect: PA-3 Black children with severe behavioral health needs are more likely than White children to be placed in RTFs outside Philadelphia, with documented family-separation effects + reduced family-contact frequency. This is the racial-equity dimension that anchors a structural finding.

**BHRS-quality-shift unevenness**. The shift from paraprofessional TSS services toward evidence-based family therapy is uneven across providers + neighborhoods; lower-resource neighborhoods receive a higher proportion of TSS-heavy authorizations vs. evidence-based therapy authorizations. The cumulative-disadvantage geography reproduces in service-quality variation.

**Schools-side disparity**. SDP's IDEA ED placement decisions have documented racial disparities at the national pattern (Black students overrepresented in ED placement; Black students more likely placed in restrictive settings vs. inclusion-with-supports); Philadelphia data follows pattern with SDP-specific quantification [F-verify].

**SD4-specific implication**: pediatric BH services formally operate under the strongest federal entitlement architecture in the domain (EPSDT), but the cumulative-disadvantage chain operates through service-quality variation, RTF placement geography, and schools-side disparity. The architectural formal strength does not, by itself, neutralize cumulative disadvantage; resolution requires resource allocation + provider diversity + family-engagement infrastructure aligned with the cumulative-disadvantage geography.

---

#### §8 Anchor Institution Thread

Per RD0 Standard 10 triple-role coverage:

**CHOP (Children's Hospital of Philadelphia)**. (i) Regulator/regulated: regulated by CMS CoP + PA DOH licensure + Joint Commission accreditation; functions as regulator-influencing through pediatric clinical standard-setting + research influence. (ii) Resource/burden: pediatric BH capacity (limited inpatient psych beds; pediatric ED with psychiatric capacity; outpatient pediatric BH); commercial + Medicare prioritization; (burden) financial sustainability of pediatric BH service-line at Medicaid rate inadequacy. (iii) Structural beneficiary/excluded constituent: Medicaid + Medicare + commercial reimbursement; tax-exempt status; (excluded) Medicaid + uninsured pediatric BH access asymmetry.

**School District of Philadelphia**. (i) Regulator-influencing through IDEA implementation + Title I administration; regulated under IDEA + Section 504 + state special education law. (ii) Resource: school-based BH for ~120K students; (burden) operational sustainability of BH workforce post-ESSER. (iii) Structural beneficiary: Title I + IDEA + ESSER federal funding; tax-base operational base; (excluded constituent advocate) operates partially as advocate for ED-placement + school-based BH access.

**Philadelphia DHS**. (i) Regulator-influencing via CAPTA + Title IV-E state plan compliance; regulated by ACF + CMS for IV-E + Medicaid integration. (ii) Resource: child welfare services + DBHIDS-coordinated BH services for dependent-court population; (burden) workforce + capacity constraints. (iii) Structural beneficiary: federal + state child welfare funding; (excluded constituent service) dependent-court population.

**Children's Crisis Treatment Center + PMHCC + similar pediatric BH organizations**. (i) Regulated by DBHIDS contract + state licensure; (regulator-influencing) clinical standard-setting. (ii) Resource: pediatric outpatient + crisis + school-based BH; (burden) rate-driven sustainability. (iii) Structural beneficiary: Medicaid + grants; (service population) PA-3 children + families.

The anchor institution profile for SD4 differs from SD1-SD3: schools + child welfare + pediatric specialty providers anchor SD4, with university medical centers (CHOP being the primary university-affiliated; St. Christopher's residual Drexel) playing more constrained roles than at SD1-SD3.

#### §1 Data Provenance Statement

Philadelphia-specific direct: PA-3 ACS demographic baseline (52% Black; ~18% Hispanic; child population structure); D2-V cumulative racial-equity findings (ACE burden; lead exposure); D2-V FQHC § 330 funding posture (S106-V; Dec 2026 horizon); D2-V Braidwood resolution (S134/MC-V-1; June 27, 2025 6-3 mandate upheld); CBH Medicaid management for Philadelphia children; SDP school-based BH operations including ESSER III period; DBHIDS CASSP office structural role.

National benchmarks applied with structural inference: NSDUH adolescent MH prevalence rates; CDC YRBSS adolescent suicide ideation + attempt rates; AAP child psychiatry wait-time benchmarks; FFPSA QRTP designation methodology; IDEA ED placement national patterns + racial disparity studies.

Statistical caveats: ranges where exact PA-3 numbers unavailable; F-flags for current-state. Pediatric BH data infrastructure (NSDUH adolescent module; SAMHSA TEDS for adolescent SUD; CMS T-MSIS for pediatric Medicaid utilization) provides national + state baselines but Philadelphia-specific FY26 currency varies.

---

#### §2 PA-3 Statistical Profile — Children's Behavioral Health

##### 2.1 Pediatric population baseline

PA-3 child + adolescent population (under 18): substantial share of the ~580K PA-3 total population, structurally weighted toward households in the cumulative-disadvantage geography per ACS patterns. Subset under 21 (the EPSDT-eligible age band) larger. Specific FY26 counts [F-verify ACS 2024 5-year tables].

##### 2.2 Pediatric BH burden estimates

NSDUH applied with PA-3 demographic baseline:
- Adolescents (12-17) with Major Depressive Episode in past year: ~15-17% of adolescent population (national NSDUH 2022 rate; trending upward post-COVID); applied to PA-3 adolescent population [F-verify FY26]
- Adolescents with serious thoughts of suicide past year: ~10-13% per CDC YRBSS pattern
- Children + adolescents with diagnosable MH condition: ~17-20% of pediatric population structurally
- Adolescents with SUD: ~3-5% of 12-17 population per NSDUH; with co-occurring MH burden subset
- ACE burden in PA-3 above national average per D2-V cross-domain: 4+ ACEs prevalence concentrated in cumulative-disadvantage geography

##### 2.3 Service capacity baseline

- CBH Medicaid coverage extends to Philadelphia children + adolescents through HealthChoices BH; pediatric BH provider network including outpatient pediatric psych specialty practices, FQHC integrated BH (PHMC, Spectrum, Drexel 11th Street, FIGHT pediatric), CCTC, PMHCC, Children's Service Center, hospital-based pediatric BH (CHOP outpatient + IP limited; St. Christopher's outpatient)
- Child + adolescent psychiatry wait time: 3-6 months for specialty (per documented pattern; FY26 [F-verify])
- Pediatric outpatient therapy wait time: 4-8 weeks adult-pattern + amplified for child specialty
- BHRS authorizations: shifted toward family-based therapy (MST, FFT) but TSS-heavy authorizations persist [F-verify FY26 mix]
- IDEA Emotional Disturbance placement count SDP [F-verify FY26]
- RTF placement count for Philadelphia children [F-verify FY26 + out-of-Philadelphia placement rate]
- School-based BH workforce: substantial ESSER III-funded expansion 2021-2024; post-Sept 2024 sustainability + BSCA $1B school-MH funding termination April 29, 2025 [F2 partially resolved per TC-02]

##### 2.4 Multi-system population estimates

- Philadelphia DHS dependent-court population: ~5-7K children [F-verify FY26]; majority Medicaid-eligible; BH service plan coordination
- SDP IDEA-eligible students (all categories): substantial share; ED-specific subset [F-verify]
- Juvenile justice-BH overlap: Philadelphia juvenile probation population with documented MH need overlay [F-verify]

---

#### §3 Geographic Variation

##### 3.1 North/Northwest Philadelphia Core

Highest pediatric BH need + lowest pediatric BH provider density. ACE burden + lead exposure (D2 SD5 inheritance) + housing instability + concentrated poverty all elevated. School-based BH at SDP schools partial mitigation; ESSER III cliff disproportionately affects schools in this sub-area. RTF-eligible children disproportionately placed outside Philadelphia. CCTC + PMHCC partial outpatient coverage; CHOP geographic distance from sub-area imposes access burden for hospital-based pediatric BH.

##### 3.2 West Philadelphia Core

Bifurcated. University City anchor concentration (CHOP nominally accessible; Penn pediatric BH limited outpatient) + adjacent neighborhoods (Mantua, Kingsessing, Cobbs Creek) closer to North Core pattern. PHMC + Spectrum FQHC integrated pediatric BH partial mitigation. Drexel/St. Christopher's pediatric BH remnant.

##### 3.3 Northwest Philadelphia

Heterogeneous. Higher-income tracts (Chestnut Hill, parts Mt. Airy) adequate pediatric BH access; lower-income tracts (Germantown, Stenton, West Oak Lane, Wister) closer to North Core pattern. PHMC FQHC sites partial mitigation; CCTC outpatient.

##### 3.4 South/Southwest Philadelphia

Better access pattern than North Core. Esperanza-adjacent + Jefferson pediatric + Penn Center City pediatric BH accessible; PHMC South Philadelphia pediatric services. SDP schools in this sub-area have relatively better school-based BH workforce density.

##### 3.5 Boundary-adjacent

Suburban Montgomery County RTFs (Devereux + others); Bucks County RTFs; out-of-state RTFs in Maryland, Delaware, New Jersey serve Philadelphia children placed outside city. PA-3 children in RTF placement outside Philadelphia experience family-contact burden similar to the adult civil §304 → Danville State Hospital pattern (T2P2 SD2 §3.5; revised per TC-05).

---

#### §4 Aggregate Pathway Tracing

**Pathway A: Medicaid-enrolled PA-3 adolescent with depression/anxiety seeking outpatient pediatric BH:**
1. Family contacts pediatrician; PHQ-A or USPSTF Grade B adolescent depression/anxiety screening administered (Braidwood-preserved)
2. Positive screen → referral to outpatient pediatric BH; pediatrician outreach to CBH
3. CBH Member Services routes to in-network pediatric BH; appointment 3-6 months out for child psychiatry specialty; 4-8 weeks for pediatric therapist
4. Initial appointment: medication management + therapy if indicated; family engagement
5. Ongoing care: BHRS authorization if intensive level; school-based BH coordination through SDP if school-aged; CCTC or PMHCC outpatient
6. Step-up if needed: intensive outpatient (IOP); partial hospitalization (PHP); inpatient pediatric psychiatry (capacity constrained — CHOP limited beds; out-of-city placement common); RTF placement at higher acuity

Breakdown points: Step 3 wait time pushes some to ED pathway by attrition (ED visits for pediatric MH crisis spiked post-COVID); Step 5 BHRS quality variation (TSS-heavy vs. evidence-based); Step 6 RTF placement geographic burden.

**Pathway B: SDP student with emerging behavioral health need:**
1. Teacher / counselor / nurse identifies behavioral concern
2. School-based screening + student support team (SST) review
3. Referral options: school-based BH (school counselor + contracted school-based BH provider — capacity tied to ESSER funding [F2]); IDEA evaluation if disability-suspected; Section 504 plan; outside referral to CBH
4. IDEA ED evaluation if pursued: 60-day timeline; eligibility determination; IEP development if eligible; placement decision (general education with supports; emotional-support classroom; APS; RTF-with-school)
5. School-based BH services delivery: counselor sessions; group therapy; crisis response
6. Parent/family engagement; coordination with outside CBH if dual

Breakdown points: Step 3 ESSER cliff threatens school-based BH workforce; Step 4 ED placement racial disparity; Step 4 APS/RTF placement separation from peers; Step 5 workforce-shortage-driven service inconsistency.

**Pathway C: Philadelphia DHS dependent-court child with BH need:**
1. Child entry to dependent-court system (abuse/neglect; voluntary placement)
2. CAPTA-funded screening at intake; trauma-informed care assessment
3. Title IV-E + Medicaid coverage activation; CBH BH service plan integration
4. CASSP coordinator engagement; Children and Family Service Plan development
5. Placement: kinship care + in-home services; foster care; congregate care (group home; QRTP-designated facility); RTF if highest-acuity
6. BH service delivery: outpatient + BHRS + family therapy; if RTF: facility-based BH; school placement coordination
7. Permanency planning: reunification; adoption; aging out

Breakdown points: Step 5 placement instability; Step 6 RTF placement out-of-city → family-contact gap + reentry-to-community challenge; Step 7 aging-out → adult services transition (loss of EPSDT entitlement at 21).

**Pathway D: Uninsured PA-3 child or family seeking pediatric BH:**
1. FQHC integrated pediatric BH (PHMC, Spectrum, FIGHT, Drexel 11th Street, Esperanza-adjacent) sliding-fee
2. SDP school-based BH for school-aged
3. Crisis-only access via ED + EMTALA — no continuity
4. Medicaid-eligible-but-unenrolled families: enrollment friction; CHIP eligibility check

Breakdown points: FQHC capacity at every step + § 330 funding horizon Dec 2026 [D2-V S106-V]; ESSER cliff for SDP school-based BH; episodic-care-without-continuity pattern.

---

#### §5 Representation Question

**Element 1 (formal provision):** EPSDT entitlement to all medically necessary BH services for under-21 (42 U.S.C. § 1396d(r)); IDEA Emotional Disturbance category + FAPE entitlement (20 U.S.C. § 1400 et seq.); FFPSA Title IV-E prevention services; CCBHC pediatric services component; CAPTA-mandated screening at child welfare intake; Braidwood-preserved USPSTF Grade B adolescent depression/anxiety screening (D2-V S134); PA CASSP system-of-care framework; Philadelphia DBHIDS CASSP coordination; DBHIDS-SDP-DHS interagency authority.

**Element 2 (actual receipt):** Medicaid-enrolled children receive structurally comprehensive coverage but face 3-6 month child psychiatry wait, BHRS quality variation, RTF placement out-of-Philadelphia for higher-acuity needs. SDP school-based BH faces post-ESSER sustainability cliff. Dependent-court children face placement instability + service plan coordination challenges. Uninsured + § 330-dependent children face capacity-constrained access through FQHCs with December 2026 funding horizon. Multi-system children (DHS + SDP + CBH + juvenile probation) experience coordination gaps at the system seams.

**Element 3 (account for the gap):** Multiple compounding causes: (a) pediatric BH workforce shortage concentrated in child/adolescent psychiatry; (b) Medicaid rate inadequacy for pediatric specialty driving network erosion; (c) post-ESSER sustainability gap for school-based BH workforce + parallel BSCA $1B school-MH funding termination April 29, 2025 [TC-02]; (d) RTF capacity in Philadelphia constrained → out-of-city placement; (e) BHRS-quality-shift uneven implementation; (f) IDEA ED placement racial disparity replicating national pattern at SDP scale; (g) cumulative-disadvantage geography reproducing service-quality variation; (h) federal agency capacity erosion at SAMHSA + ED OSEP + ACF threatening pediatric BH grant + TA infrastructure [F11 → resolved per TC-06]; (i) OBBBA implementation phased FY28-34 [F13 → resolved per TC-03]: state-directed payment caps + provider tax reduction affect rate-setting; CCBHC/FQHC/RHC exemptions protect key pediatric BH streams; EPSDT framework unchanged.

**Element 4 (meaning for representation analysis):** SD4 represents the **EPSDT formal-strength + multi-system operational gap pattern** within D3. The pediatric architectural floor is among the strongest in the domain (EPSDT + IDEA + FFPSA + CCBHC + CASSP + Braidwood-preserved screening — the formal entitlement architecture is more robust than at SD3 community-services or SD5 commercial parity). Yet operational shortfall manifests at the integration seams: schools-Medicaid coordination, child welfare-BH coordination, juvenile justice-BH coordination, RTF-community transition. The representation question for SD4 is therefore not "is there an entitlement?" (yes — strongly) but "is the multi-system operational architecture sufficient to deliver the entitlement?" (gap documented at every seam). Federal-rep leverage points: (1) Title IV-E prevention services + QRTP implementation; (2) IDEA monitoring + ED OSEP enforcement posture; (3) SAMHSA pediatric BH grants (Project AWARE; System of Care discretionary); (4) post-ESSER federal replacement-funding for school-based BH (cross-domain to D11 Education); (5) HRSA Title V MCH + school-based health center program; (6) CMS pediatric Medicaid managed care oversight + EPSDT enforcement; (7) Braidwood-preserved USPSTF preventive services framework persistence.

---

### SD4 Gap Analysis


**Sub-Domain:** SD4 Children's Behavioral Health
**Slug:** childBH
**Date:** 2026-04-30
**Cycle:** RD2 first-pass; Gap Analysis SD4
**Authority:** RD2 §STEP 4; RD0 Standards 5.B/C/D/E
**Inputs:** track1-phase2-SD4; track2-phase2-SD4; substructure cross-cutting threads

---

#### §1 SD4 Gap Findings

##### G1-SD4-01: EPSDT formal-strength + multi-system operational-gap pattern

**Characterization.** EPSDT (42 U.S.C. § 1396d(r)) provides a stronger pediatric BH entitlement architecture than D3's adult community-services framework, yet operational shortfall manifests at the multi-system seams: schools-Medicaid coordination, child welfare-BH coordination, juvenile justice-BH coordination, RTF-community transition. The representation question for pediatric BH is therefore not entitlement-existence but multi-system-architecture-sufficiency. **Largest single SD4 representation-gap finding.**

**Evidence trace.** T1P2 §2.1 (EPSDT central architecture); T2P2 §4 four pathway breakdowns; T2P2 §5 Element 4.
**Type:** [SD] structural derivation from documented architecture + operational pattern.
**Premises:** (1) EPSDT entitlement breadth documented (federal statute + CMS guidance); (2) multi-system children typified by DHS + SDP + CBH + juvenile probation overlap; (3) coordination at the seams documented as straining at scale; (4) shortfall manifests in operational outcomes (RTF out-of-city placement; ESSER cliff; BHRS quality variation; ED placement disparity).
**Confidence:** HIGH for the pattern; MEDIUM-HIGH for current quantification [F-verify FY26 multi-system population overlap data].
**Representation implication.** Federal-rep leverage at: Title IV-E QRTP implementation; IDEA monitoring + ED OSEP enforcement; SAMHSA System of Care + Project AWARE; post-ESSER federal replacement-funding for school-based BH; CMS pediatric Medicaid + EPSDT enforcement. State/local lever at: PA OCYF Title IV-E plan; PA OMHSAS pediatric standards; DBHIDS CASSP + interagency MOU operations.

---

##### G1-SD4-02: Post-ESSER school-based BH sustainability cliff + parallel BSCA termination (revised per TC-02)

*Verification update (2026-04-30): The ESSER cliff finding now applies in parallel to BSCA $1B school-MH funding, which the Trump administration moved to terminate via April 29, 2025 letters citing civil-rights concerns about diversity-in-MH-workforce framing. See Material Changes Summary TC-02.*

**Characterization.** ESSER III obligation deadline September 30, 2024 / liquidation deadline January 28, 2025 marks a forward-looking funding cliff for SDP school-based BH workforce expanded 2021-2024 with ESSER funds. **Verified parallel disruption (TC-02):** BSCA-authorized $1B school-MH grant stream (P.L. 117-159) was targeted for termination via April 29, 2025 Education Department letters; KFF documents federal grant share for school MH dropped 53% (2021-22) → 33% (2024-25); 16-state AG lawsuit filed November 2025; 19 Community Schools 5-year grants terminated Dec 20, 2025; 2,416 federal Department of Education employees lost jobs in 2025. State + city replacement-funding has not structurally replaced either federal stream. The finding is **most temporally urgent** in the SD4 inventory.

**Evidence trace.** T1P2 §2.7 (ESSER + BSCA framework); T1P2 §6.3 (SDP); T2P2 §4 Pathway B Step 3 + §5 Element 3; KFF Sept 2025 report; April 29, 2025 ED termination letters.
**Type:** [D] direct documentation of statutory expiration + administrative termination + [SD] structural derivation of cliff.
**Premises:** (1) ESSER III obligation deadline statutory and known; (2) BSCA termination via April 29, 2025 letters verified per TC-02; (3) PA + Philadelphia replacement-funding levels documented as not structurally replacing ESSER scale; (4) school-based BH workforce expansion 2021-2024 was substantially ESSER- + BSCA-funded; (5) the dual-stream disruption therefore translates directly to workforce contraction without intervention.
**Confidence:** HIGH for the architectural cliff (now dual-stream); SDP-specific FY26 workforce numbers remain unverified [F2 partially resolved per TC-02; SDP quantification pending per UV-02].
**Representation implication.** Cross-domain primary lever at D11 Education (federal replacement-funding mechanism); SD4 establishes BH-side structural finding. State/local lever at PA general fund + city general fund prioritization. 16-state AG lawsuit creates parallel litigation lever.

---

##### G1-SD4-03: RTF out-of-Philadelphia placement geography as family-separation + racial-equity finding

**Characterization.** Philadelphia children placed in RTFs are frequently placed outside the city; national + PA literature documents Black children disproportionately represented in RTF placement and at greater geographic distance from family. The combined effect: Black PA-3 children with severe BH needs are more likely than White children to be placed outside Philadelphia, with documented family-separation effects + reduced family-contact frequency.

**Evidence trace.** T1P2 §5.3 (PA RTF regulation); T1P2 §7 (cumulative racial disadvantage); T2P2 §3.5 (boundary-adjacent RTFs); T2P2 §4 Pathway A Step 6.
**Type:** [SD] structural derivation from national + PA pattern applied to Philadelphia.
**Premises:** (1) National literature documents RTF placement racial disparity; (2) Philadelphia in-city RTF capacity constrained; (3) FFPSA QRTP designation reshaped landscape; (4) family-separation effect documented; (5) racial-equity dimension follows from disparity + geography compound.
**Confidence:** HIGH for the structural pattern; MEDIUM-HIGH for current PA-3 quantification [F-verify FY26 RTF placement count + out-of-Philadelphia rate + racial breakdown].
**Representation implication.** Federal-rep leverage at FFPSA Title IV-E QRTP implementation + ACF state plan oversight; state lever at PA RTF capacity expansion; local lever at DBHIDS-DHS in-city RTF investment + RTF-alternative community-based services. Cross-cutting to SD7 synthesis (racial-equity chain extension to pediatric scale).

---

##### G1-SD4-04: BHRS quality-variation as service-quality cumulative-disadvantage finding

**Characterization.** PA BHRS authorizations historically skewed toward paraprofessional TSS services vs. evidence-based modalities; PA + DBHIDS course-correction toward family-based therapy (MST, FFT) is uneven across providers + neighborhoods. Lower-resource neighborhoods receive higher proportion of TSS-heavy authorizations vs. evidence-based therapy authorizations. The cumulative-disadvantage geography reproduces in service-quality variation.

**Evidence trace.** T1P2 §5.2 (BHRS regulation); T1P2 §7 (cumulative racial disadvantage thread 3); T2P2 §2.3 + §4 Pathway A Step 5.
**Type:** [SD] structural derivation.
**Premises:** (1) BHRS authorization mix documented historically TSS-heavy; (2) PA + DBHIDS shift toward evidence-based; (3) provider distribution variable across geography; (4) service-quality variation racially traceable through geography correlation.
**Confidence:** MEDIUM-HIGH; specific FY26 authorization mix data [F-verify DBHIDS reporting].
**Representation implication.** State-level lever at OMHSAS authorization standards + provider training; local lever at DBHIDS contract management + evidence-based therapy provider expansion. Federal lever modest.

---

##### G1-SD4-05: Child psychiatry wait time (3-6 month) as pediatric-specialty network adequacy operational failure

**Characterization.** 3-6 month wait time for child + adolescent psychiatry specialty exceeds reasonable network-adequacy benchmarks for pediatric urgent BH need. CBH formally compliant; operationally degraded. Pediatric specialty workforce shortage + Medicaid rate inadequacy primary drivers.

**Evidence trace.** T1P2 §6.2 (CBH pediatric services); T2P2 §2.3 + §4 Pathway A Step 3.
**Type:** [SD] structural derivation.
**Premises:** (1) Pediatric clinical urgency benchmarks (AAP); (2) documented wait time; (3) network-adequacy regulation interpretation; (4) workforce shortage in child/adolescent psychiatry national + state pattern.
**Confidence:** MEDIUM-HIGH; FY26 [F-verify].
**Representation implication.** Federal lever at Sept 2024 MHPAEA Final Rule network-adequacy NQTL + CMS oversight via 42 C.F.R. § 438.68 (with pediatric-specific weighting); HRSA pediatric workforce (NHSC + Title V MCH); state OMHSAS enforcement.

---

##### G1-SD4-06: IDEA ED placement racial disparity at SDP

**Characterization.** SDP IDEA Emotional Disturbance category placement decisions have documented racial disparity at the national pattern (Black students overrepresented in ED placement; Black students more likely placed in restrictive settings). Philadelphia data follows national pattern with SDP-specific quantification flagged.

**Evidence trace.** T1P2 §2.2 (IDEA ED); T1P2 §7 (schools-side disparity thread 4); T2P2 §4 Pathway B Step 4.
**Type:** [SD] structural derivation from national pattern applied to SDP.
**Premises:** (1) National OSEP + research literature documents ED placement racial disparity; (2) SDP demographic baseline; (3) ED placement decisions navigate inclusion vs. separate-program; (4) restrictive-placement disparity translates to peer-separation + reduced inclusion.
**Confidence:** MEDIUM-HIGH for the pattern; specific SDP quantification [F-verify].
**Representation implication.** Federal lever at IDEA monitoring + ED OSEP enforcement + state performance plan oversight; state lever at PDE; local lever at SDP IEP team practice + LRE compliance review.

---

##### G1-SD4-07: Pediatric MH ED visit elevation as upstream-services-failure transmitted to crisis settings

**Characterization.** Pediatric MH ED visits in Philadelphia documented as elevated post-COVID; the volume is functionally an attrition outcome of upstream wait times + capacity constraints (Pathway A Step 3 wait time pushes some to crisis pathway by attrition). Crisis-setting volume is a downstream marker of upstream insufficiency.

**Evidence trace.** T2P2 §4 Pathway A breakdown; T2P2 §6 (F-verify ED visit volume); §2 anticipated structural finding.
**Type:** [SD] structural derivation.
**Premises:** (1) Wait time documented; (2) ED visit elevation documented; (3) attrition pathway from outpatient to crisis follows from inability to access timely outpatient; (4) volume increase therefore signals upstream capacity insufficiency, not increase in baseline acuity alone.
**Confidence:** MEDIUM-HIGH; specific FY26 pediatric MH ED volume Philadelphia [F-verify].
**Representation implication.** Cross-cutting to SD7 synthesis (psychiatric boarding crisis + crisis-system inadequacy operational outcomes). Federal-rep leverage at upstream pediatric BH workforce + capacity expansion.

---

**Characterization.** The multi-system coordination gap pattern documented at SD4 (CBH-SDP-DHS-juvenile probation seams) shares structural shape with the multi-system gap forthcoming at SD6 (corrections MH + court diversion + reentry coordination). Both are systems where the formal entitlement exists but operational coordination at agency-boundary seams produces shortfall. Pattern recognition for synthesis.

## Sub-Domain 5: Mental Health Parity

### SD5 Track 1 — Legal & Institutional Architecture


**Sub-Domain:** SD5 Mental Health Parity
**Slug:** parity
**Date:** 2026-04-30
**Cycle:** RD2 first-pass; Track 1 Phase 2 SD5
**Authority:** RD2 §STEP 2 Phase 2 structural template; RD0 Standards 9, 10, 11; depth caps Standard 16.C
**Source pairing:** source-workspace_D3_2026-04-30.md §§1-7
**Regime:** training-data primary; current-state items F-flagged

This document maps the legal architecture for mental health parity in PA-3 — the federal MHPAEA stack, ACA parity expansion, CAA 2021 NQTL framework, the Sept 2024 Final MHPAEA Rule [F1 → resolved per TC-01], the PA Mental Health Parity Act, ERISA preemption for self-funded plans, and the Philadelphia anchor-institution employer-plan implication.

The analytical move SD5 establishes: **Parity formally exists across multiple statutory layers (MHPAEA + ACA + CAA + PA Act + state Medicaid MCO rules) but operationally breaks down at three distinct architectural seams: NQTL enforcement gaps (especially network adequacy + prior-auth + medical-necessity criteria), ERISA preemption of self-funded employer plans (the anchor-institution-employee paradox), and rate-driven access barrier in CBH that creates formal-compliance + operational-shortfall coexistence.**

---

#### §1 Constitutional Authority

Federal constitutional grounding for parity is Spending Clause + Commerce Clause: MHPAEA + ACA invoke Spending Clause for Medicaid + ACA-regulated plans + Commerce Clause for ERISA-plan oversight. ERISA preemption of state law for self-funded employer plans (29 U.S.C. § 1144) is the constitutive jurisdictional architecture limiting state-level parity reach. PA Const. Art. I § 1 provides PA-specific procedural floor; PA's police-power authority grounds state insurance regulation including the PA Mental Health Parity Act (40 P.S. § 764f).

Constitutional gap relevant to SD5: ERISA preemption is statutory not constitutional, but operates with constitutional-doctrine force in limiting state parity reach for self-funded plans. Federal constitutional minimums for parity are not directly articulated in case law; parity operates principally through statutory + regulatory architecture rather than constitutional doctrine.

---

#### §2 Federal Statutory Layer

##### 2.1 MHPAEA — the central parity statute

Mental Health Parity and Addiction Equity Act of 2008 (P.L. 110-343 Div C); 29 U.S.C. § 1185a; 42 U.S.C. § 300gg-26; 26 U.S.C. § 9812. Replaced Mental Health Parity Act 1996 (which addressed only annual + lifetime dollar limits for groups of 50+). MHPAEA expanded to financial requirements (deductibles, copays, coinsurance, OOP maximums) + quantitative treatment limitations (QTL — visit limits, day limits) + non-quantitative treatment limitations (NQTL — prior auth, medical necessity criteria, fail-first protocols, network composition + adequacy, provider reimbursement methodology, plan design generally). NQTL parity is enforced through "comparative analysis" — MH/SUD NQTL must be applied no more stringently than predominant medical/surgical NQTL.

MHPAEA applies to: large group health plans (50+ employees originally; ACA modifications); self-funded ERISA plans (DOL EBSA enforcement); health insurance issuers offering coverage to large groups; Medicaid MCOs; CHIP. **MHPAEA does NOT apply to:** Medicare; small group plans grandfathered; certain TRICARE elements; individual short-term limited-duration insurance pre-ACA. **Statutory stability: STABLE textually**.

##### 2.2 ACA parity expansion

42 U.S.C. § 18022(b)(1)(E) — Essential Health Benefits including MH/SUD as one of ten EHB categories; 42 U.S.C. § 18031(j) — qualified health plan parity; 42 C.F.R. Part 438 Subpart K (438.900 et seq.) — Medicaid MCO parity. ACA expanded MHPAEA scope to individual market plans + small group + Medicaid MCOs + Medicaid expansion population.

##### 2.3 CAA 2021 NQTL comparative analysis requirement

Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2021 (P.L. 116-260 Div BB Title II) — required group health plans + insurers to perform + document comparative analysis of NQTLs; granted DOL/HHS/Treasury authority to request the analysis; required annual DOL report to Congress on NQTL findings + enforcement actions. Operationalized prior NQTL parity requirement; established documentation + audit framework.

##### 2.4 Sept 2024 Final MHPAEA Rule

*Verification update (2026-04-30): Prior pass framed the Sept 2024 Final Rule's status under Trump 2 administration as "uncertain; possible enforcement rollback, modification, or delay." Verified state: Tri-Agencies (DOL, HHS, Treasury) issued a joint non-enforcement statement May 15, 2025 in response to the ERIC v. DOL/HHS/Treasury litigation (D.D.C., filed Jan 17, 2025; abeyance granted May 12, 2025). The 2024 Final Rule's new provisions will not be enforced until a final litigation decision plus an additional 18 months. See Material Changes Summary TC-01.*

89 Fed. Reg. 77,586 (HHS/DOL/Treasury, September 2024) — Final Rule strengthening NQTL comparative analysis standards including network composition adequacy as NQTL; medical necessity criteria evidentiary standards; provider reimbursement methodology comparability; expanded enforcement framework. **Status [F1/P1 → resolved per TC-01]:** non-enforcement of the new 2024 provisions per the May 15, 2025 Tri-Agency statement; statute (MHPAEA + CAA 2021) and 2013 Final Rule's NQTL comparative-analysis obligation remain in force. Reconsideration of 2024 Rule pending litigation outcome.

##### 2.5 No Surprises Act

P.L. 116-260 Div BB Title I — addresses surprise out-of-network billing including emergency MH services + behavioral health emergencies. Independent dispute resolution framework. **Statutory stability: STABLE; implementation regulations in administrative motion**.

##### 2.6 ERISA preemption — the architectural seam

29 U.S.C. § 1144. ERISA preempts state law as it "relates to" employee benefit plans, with savings clause for state insurance + banking + securities laws. The result: **self-funded employer health plans** (where the employer assumes financial risk and contracts a third-party administrator for claims processing) are governed by ERISA + DOL exclusively, NOT by state insurance regulation including state parity laws. PA Mental Health Parity Act + state Insurance Department parity enforcement do NOT reach self-funded plans. **Fully insured** employer plans (where the employer purchases coverage from an insurance carrier that bears the risk) ARE subject to state parity law via the savings clause.

The architectural consequence for Philadelphia anchor institutions: large employers (Penn, Temple, Jefferson, CHOP, Drexel + the major hospitals + the universities) typically operate self-funded plans. Their employees have weaker formal parity protection than Medicaid beneficiaries through CBH or fully-insured commercial plan members through PA-regulated carriers. **Statutory stability: STABLE textually; Congressional reform of ERISA preemption rare and contested**.

##### 2.7 Other federal anchors

- **HIPAA Title I** — pre-existing condition + group market reform precursor to MHPAEA's modern architecture.
- **CHIPRA 2009** — children's BH parity in CHIP.
- **CARES Act 2020 + ARPA 2021** telehealth-parity expansions for MH/SUD via Medicare and other federal-payer pathways. **PA Act 98 of 2022** [TC-10] permanently removed PA-state audio-only telehealth restrictions in outpatient psych + D&A clinic settings, complementing the federal pandemic-era expansions at the PA state level.

---

#### §3 Federal Regulatory Layer

- **42 C.F.R. Part 438 Subpart K (438.900 et seq.)** — Medicaid MCO parity, applies to CBH; defines parity requirements for managed Medicaid.
- **45 C.F.R. § 146.136 + Part 147** — HHS implementation of MHPAEA for individual + group market.
- **29 C.F.R. § 2590.712** — DOL EBSA implementation of MHPAEA for ERISA group health plans.
- **26 C.F.R. § 54.9812-1** — Treasury implementation of MHPAEA for tax-qualified plans.
- **The Sept 2024 Final Rule** [F1 → resolved per TC-01] — codified at 89 Fed. Reg. 77,586; cross-implements at HHS + DOL + Treasury regulatory locations.

The tri-agency structure (HHS + DOL + Treasury) reflects MHPAEA's dual ERISA + ACA + tax-qualified-plan jurisdictional reach.

---

#### §4 Federal Agency Layer

##### 4.1 DOL EBSA — primary ERISA-plan enforcement

Employee Benefits Security Administration enforces MHPAEA for self-funded + fully-insured ERISA plans. Administrative authority: investigation; civil penalties; voluntary compliance program; CAA 2021 comparative-analysis-request authority; annual Congressional reporting. EBSA Philadelphia Regional Office serves PA (jurisdictional). **Vulnerability: MODERATE-HIGH** under Trump 2 admin; enforcement posture transition [F-verify FY26 EBSA enforcement priorities].

##### 4.2 HHS CCIIO + CMS

Center for Consumer Information and Insurance Oversight (CCIIO within CMS) enforces MHPAEA for non-ERISA group + individual market + state-based exchange plans; CMS enforces MHPAEA for Medicaid MCO + Medicare Advantage. **Vulnerability: MODERATE-HIGH** [F1 → resolved per TC-01: 2024 Rule paused; 2013 Rule + CAA 2021 in force; F13 → resolved per TC-03: OBBBA implementation phased FY28-34].

##### 4.3 Treasury IRS

Treasury enforces MHPAEA for tax-qualified plans under § 9812. Administrative role primarily through plan-document compliance; limited active enforcement. **Vulnerability: MODERATE**.

##### 4.4 The tri-agency joint role

DOL + HHS + Treasury joint guidance on MHPAEA — including the 2013, 2016, 2018, 2024 final rules — operates through the tri-agency framework. Coordination + joint-regulation tradition is well-established but resource-dependent. Any agency reduction in capacity reduces joint-rule output velocity.

##### 4.5 HHS OCR

HHS Office for Civil Rights enforces Section 504 + ADA Title II including parity-as-discrimination claims. Limited primary parity authority; secondary route for parity-related civil rights claims. **Vulnerability: MODERATE-HIGH** [F-verify].

##### 4.6 Federal courts as enforcement venue

Private rights of action under MHPAEA + ERISA produce parity litigation in federal courts. Class-action + administrative-record-review pathways. Operative for systemic enforcement; resource-intensive.

---

#### §5 State Layer

##### 5.1 PA Mental Health Parity Act — 40 P.S. § 764f

Enacted 1998; PA-specific MH parity floor pre-MHPAEA. Defines "biologically based mental illness" list (a narrower set than the full DSM range — typically including schizophrenia, bipolar disorder, major depression, OCD, panic disorder, anorexia/bulimia, schizoaffective, and some others); requires parity for the listed conditions in fully-insured PA plans. The "biologically based" framework is narrower than MHPAEA's universal-MH-condition coverage. Practical effect: MHPAEA federal floor exceeds PA Act for non-listed conditions; PA Act adds a narrow floor for the listed conditions in state-regulated plans. **Stability: STABLE; modernization possible but not imminent**.

##### 5.2 PA Insurance Department enforcement

Compliance bulletins; market conduct exams; annual filings (rate + form); consumer complaint processing. PA ID can require comparative analysis under CAA 2021 framework for fully-insured plans. ERISA self-funded plans outside reach.

##### 5.3 PA Medicaid MCO parity through HealthChoices

CBH as HealthChoices BH carve-out MCO subject to 42 C.F.R. Part 438 Subpart K parity requirements. PA OMHSAS oversight + CMS oversight. CBH parity attestation + comparative analysis as required.

##### 5.4 PA Office of the Attorney General

Civil parity enforcement narrow lever; consumer protection authority operative for misrepresentation claims; cross-coordination with PA ID + DOL.

---

#### §6 Local Layer

##### 6.1 CBH parity compliance

CBH operates under 42 C.F.R. Part 438 Subpart K + PA OMHSAS oversight. Parity compliance attestations + NQTL comparative analyses. Operationally: CBH formally meets parity requirements; rate-driven access barrier (provider-network capacity strained by Medicaid rate inadequacy) creates de facto access asymmetry that is not directly cognizable as a parity violation under current NQTL frameworks because the comparable medical/surgical Medicaid network faces similar rate constraints.

##### 6.2 Anchor-institution employer plans — the ERISA self-funded gap

The major Philadelphia anchor institutions — University of Pennsylvania, Penn Medicine (separate), Temple University + Temple Health, Jefferson University + Jefferson Health, Drexel University, CHOP, Children's Hospital pediatric subsidiaries — typically operate self-funded employee health plans. Self-funded plans are governed exclusively by ERISA + DOL EBSA + federal MHPAEA enforcement, NOT by PA Insurance Department or PA Mental Health Parity Act. **The structural consequence:** higher-income workers at anchor institutions have weaker state-level parity protection than lower-income Medicaid beneficiaries through CBH or fully-insured commercial plan members through PA-regulated carriers. State-level reform (e.g., PA Mental Health Parity Act expansion) cannot reach these plans; only federal action (DOL EBSA enforcement; Congressional ERISA reform) can.

##### 6.3 Philadelphia consumer protection narrow lever

City consumer-protection enforcement narrow vis-à-vis insurance regulation (state preempts most); consumer education + complaint-coordination role.

##### 6.4 Home Rule limits on parity

Philadelphia Home Rule cannot supersede federal MHPAEA, state parity rules, or ERISA preemption. Home Rule is permissive on consumer protection coordination + workforce + provider-network expansion through DBHIDS contracting (which indirectly affects parity-cognizable network adequacy).

---

#### §7 Cumulative Racial Disadvantage

SD5 cumulative-disadvantage operates through three threads:

**Insurance-coverage racial disparity**. Black + Hispanic PA-3 residents are disproportionately Medicaid-enrolled vs. employer-plan-covered, per D2-V coverage architecture inheritance. The CBH formal-compliance + rate-driven-access-barrier coexistence (§6.1) therefore disproportionately affects Black + Hispanic PA-3 residents at population scale. The parity architecture nominally protects Medicaid beneficiaries, but the operational expression of that protection is degraded by the rate-network compound.

**Out-of-network MH utilization racial pattern**. National literature (e.g., RAND, GAO parity reports) documents 3-8× higher out-of-network MH utilization vs. medical/surgical for commercially-insured population [F-verify currency]. Black + Hispanic patients face additional out-of-network barriers (provider workforce diversity gap; cultural-competency gap; trauma-informed-care gap). The compound: parity on paper produces unequal practical access along racial + socioeconomic lines.

**ERISA self-funded employer-plan workforce demographics**. Anchor-institution self-funded plans cover a workforce that is itself racially structured by Philadelphia higher-education + healthcare workforce composition. The ERISA self-funded gap therefore intersects with workforce racial composition: Black + Hispanic anchor-institution employees in clinical, food service, custodial, security, and other service roles experience the same ERISA preemption + weaker-state-protection framework as higher-paid clinical + administrative employees. The gap is not uniformly distributed by employee role.

**SD5-specific implication**: parity equity extends across both Medicaid + commercial + ERISA pathways, with the parity-protection architecture variably aligned with racial-equity outcome. Parity reform that addresses only one pathway leaves the others; comprehensive parity equity requires action across all three.

---

#### §8 Anchor Institution Thread

Per RD0 Standard 10 triple-role coverage:

**Penn (University of Pennsylvania) + Penn Medicine (separate plan)**. (i) Regulator/regulated: regulated by ERISA + DOL EBSA for self-funded plans; (regulator-influencing) employer-plan-design influence + clinical workforce position. (ii) Resource/burden: substantial employer plan covering tens of thousands of employees + dependents; (burden) financial obligation of self-funded plan including MH/SUD claims volume. (iii) Structural beneficiary: ERISA preemption of state regulation; tax-favored employer-plan treatment; (excluded constituent) employees with weaker state-parity-protection vs. fully-insured peer plans.

**Temple Health + Temple University**. (i)-(iii) Similar pattern; per D2-V S118-V, Temple Health BBB investment-grade maintained + FY25 financial recovery; structurally similar self-funded ERISA position.

**Jefferson Health + Jefferson University**. (i)-(iii) Similar pattern; large self-funded plan presence.

**CHOP**. (i)-(iii) Similar; pediatric BH employer-plan exposure intersects SD4 finding G1-SD4-01.

**Drexel University**. (i)-(iii) Similar; smaller scale than university medical centers but structurally similar ERISA position.

**CBH**. (i) Regulator/regulated: regulated by 42 C.F.R. Part 438 Subpart K + PA OMHSAS + CMS; (regulator-influencing) network management + parity compliance design. (ii) Resource: parity-protection floor for Medicaid-enrolled PA-3 residents; (burden) operational resourcing of parity compliance. (iii) Structural beneficiary: HealthChoices contract; (excluded constituent advocate) operates partially as advocate for parity-floor adequacy for Medicaid-enrolled.

The anchor institution profile for SD5 is distinctive: anchors appear primarily as employer-plan-sponsors rather than as service-providers (the latter being SD1 + SD2 + SD3 + SD4 + SD7 dimensions). This is the SD5-specific anchor-institution analytical seam.

### SD5 Track 2 — Outcomes, Geography & Representation


**Sub-Domain:** SD5 Mental Health Parity
**Slug:** parity

#### §1 Data Provenance Statement

Philadelphia-specific direct: PA-3 ACS demographic baseline; D2-V coverage architecture findings (Medicaid + commercial + uninsured distribution); CBH single-MCO HealthChoices BH model (D2-V S131); anchor-institution employer presence + workforce composition (Penn, Temple, Jefferson, CHOP, Drexel + university medical centers).

National benchmarks applied with structural inference: GAO + RAND parity reports on out-of-network utilization 3-8× medical/surgical; DOL EBSA annual MHPAEA enforcement reports; Mental Health America + NAMI parity tracking; KFF employer health benefits surveys re: self-funded plan prevalence.

Statistical caveats: parity-violation quantification at population scale is structurally difficult — violations manifest as access denials + out-of-network necessity + treatment-discontinuation that are not fully captured in claims data. Operational-shortfall measures rely on inference from utilization patterns + complaint data + audit findings. F-flags assigned for current-state items.

---

#### §2 PA-3 Statistical Profile — Mental Health Parity

##### 2.1 Coverage architecture distribution (carry-forward from D2-V)

PA-3 health coverage distribution per D2-V SD6 framework:
- Medicaid (CBH for BH): ~220-260K PA-3 residents (carry-forward SD3 §2.2)
- Commercial fully-insured (PA Insurance Department-regulated): substantial share of employer-sponsored + individual market
- Commercial self-funded ERISA: substantial share of employer-sponsored, particularly among anchor-institution employees + larger private employers
- Medicare: PA-3 senior + disabled subset
- Uninsured: residual

Specific PA-3 coverage shares FY26 [F-verify ACS + KFF-PA].

##### 2.2 Parity-relevant utilization patterns

National + applied baselines:
- Out-of-network MH utilization 3-8× medical/surgical for commercially-insured (RAND + GAO baseline; FY26 currency [F-verify])
- Out-of-network SUD utilization comparable or higher
- Prior-auth denial rate for MH/SUD higher than medical/surgical in many plans
- Provider-network adequacy gap for MH/SUD relative to medical/surgical
- BH-specific deductible + cost-sharing equivalence post-MHPAEA: largely achieved at the financial-requirement level; NQTL gaps persist

##### 2.3 Anchor-institution self-funded plan scale

Major Philadelphia anchor institutions operate self-funded employer plans covering substantial workforce + dependent populations. Specific covered-life counts [F-verify FY26]; collectively the anchor self-funded population is a significant share of the PA-3 commercial-plan-covered base. The ERISA preemption gap (T1P2 §6.2) therefore operates at population scale within PA-3, not as an isolated phenomenon.

##### 2.4 PA Insurance Department + DOL EBSA enforcement baseline

PA ID complaint volume re: parity [F-verify]; market-conduct exam frequency [F-verify]; DOL EBSA Philadelphia regional MHPAEA case dispositions [F-verify FY26]. CAA 2021 comparative-analysis-request volume + findings [F-verify].

---

#### §3 Geographic Variation

Parity-architecture geography differs from service-provision geography because parity operates at the insurance-coverage layer rather than the provider-location layer. Geographic variation manifests through:

##### 3.1 North/Northwest Philadelphia Core

Higher Medicaid + uninsured share + lower employer-sponsored share. Parity protection through CBH (Medicaid MCO parity); fewer residents covered by commercial parity-protection layers; ERISA self-funded gap less directly experienced at population scale here. The interaction with provider-network adequacy at this geography compounds (CBH formal compliance + rate-driven access barrier per §6.1 T1P2).

##### 3.2 West Philadelphia Core

Bifurcated. University City anchor employees on self-funded plans (Penn + Drexel workforce); adjacent neighborhoods closer to North Core pattern. The same geography contains both ERISA-self-funded-population concentration and CBH-Medicaid-population concentration with structurally different parity architecture experiences.

##### 3.3 Northwest Philadelphia

Heterogeneous. Higher-income tracts (Chestnut Hill, parts Mt. Airy) employer-sponsored + commercial-plan concentration; lower-income tracts (Germantown, Stenton, West Oak Lane) Medicaid + uninsured concentration.

##### 3.4 South/Southwest Philadelphia

Mixed coverage; hospital-system employer (Jefferson + Penn Center City) self-funded population; commercial fully-insured for smaller employers + individual market; Medicaid concentration in lower-income tracts.

##### 3.5 Boundary-adjacent

Anchor-institution workforce extends beyond PA-3 (Penn + Temple + Jefferson + CHOP + Drexel employees commute from suburban + adjacent areas); the ERISA self-funded gap therefore extends regionally; PA-3-resident anchor employees are a subset of total anchor self-funded population.

---

#### §4 Aggregate Pathway Tracing

**Pathway A: PA-3 anchor-institution employee with depression seeking outpatient MH:**
1. Employee covered by self-funded ERISA plan (Penn / Temple / Jefferson / CHOP / Drexel + others)
2. Plan handbook references mental health benefits subject to MHPAEA federal floor; no PA Mental Health Parity Act coverage (ERISA preempted)
3. Employee seeks in-network outpatient therapist; finds in-network availability constrained (typical NQTL gap); options: in-network with wait + driving distance, or out-of-network with full or partial cost
4. Out-of-network choice: claims for reimbursement; partial reimbursement at out-of-network rate (typically 60-80% of UCR); employee absorbs balance + cost-sharing
5. If parity dispute (denied claim; medical necessity disagreement; in-network adequacy challenge): internal appeal → ERISA-administered appeal → federal court if pursued; OR DOL EBSA complaint (administrative); OR no recourse if employee declines to pursue (many cases)

Breakdown points: Step 3 in-network adequacy NQTL gap; Step 4 financial burden imposes selection effect against access; Step 5 procedural complexity deters individual enforcement.

**Pathway B: PA-3 Medicaid-enrolled adult with SMI through CBH:**
1. CBH Member Services routes per Pathway A SD3 (already developed); parity protection through 42 C.F.R. Part 438 Subpart K + PA OMHSAS oversight
2. Wait time + phantom-provider problem operates (SD3 G1-SD3-06)
3. Parity recourse: CBH grievance + appeal; PA Department of Human Services fair hearing; DRP advocacy
4. Network adequacy disputes: state-level + CMS oversight; comparative analysis under Sept 2024 Rule [F1/F14]

Breakdown points: rate-driven access barrier creates de facto access asymmetry not directly cognizable as parity violation given comparable medical/surgical Medicaid network constraint; the operational shortfall + formal compliance coexist.

**Pathway C: PA-3 fully-insured commercial plan member (small employer / individual market) with anxiety:**
1. Plan covered by ACA EHB MH/SUD inclusion + PA Mental Health Parity Act (for biologically-based MI subset) + MHPAEA federal floor
2. In-network search; out-of-network if no in-network availability; reimbursement-pathway complexity
3. State-level recourse: PA ID complaint; market conduct exam if systemic; PA-Act-coverage if biologically-based MI condition
4. Federal recourse: HHS CCIIO + DOL/Treasury

Breakdown points: PA Mental Health Parity Act narrower than MHPAEA + state-regulated + reaches fully-insured only; complexity of layered protection.

**Pathway D: PA-3 Medicare beneficiary with MH need:**
1. Original Medicare Part B covers outpatient MH at standard cost-sharing (post-2014 elimination of higher MH cost-sharing); Medicare Advantage plan parity through 42 C.F.R. § 422 etc.
2. Provider acceptance of Medicare; supply-side gap for MH providers accepting Medicare
3. Limited Medicaid dual-eligibility for low-income seniors

Breakdown: provider acceptance gap; coverage layered with non-MHPAEA pathway.

---

#### §5 Representation Question

**Element 1 (formal provision):** MHPAEA 2008 + ACA EHB + CAA 2021 NQTL framework + Sept 2024 Final Rule [F1 → resolved per TC-01]; PA Mental Health Parity Act 1998 (biologically-based MI list); 42 C.F.R. Part 438 Subpart K Medicaid MCO parity; ERISA-plan parity through DOL EBSA enforcement; No Surprises Act for emergency MH; CBH parity attestation framework; PA Insurance Department market conduct + complaint authority.

**Element 2 (actual receipt):** Out-of-network MH utilization 3-8× medical/surgical baseline reflects operational shortfall despite formal protection; NQTL enforcement gaps documented at network adequacy + prior-auth + medical-necessity criteria; ERISA self-funded plan members at anchor institutions experience weaker state-parity protection than Medicaid-enrolled or fully-insured peers; CBH formal compliance + rate-driven access barrier creates Medicaid-pathway operational shortfall distinct from formal-parity-violation; complaint + enforcement procedural complexity reduces individual + class enforcement; Sept 2024 Final Rule status uncertainty creates forward-looking enforcement risk.

**Element 3 (account for the gap):** Multiple compounding causes: (a) NQTL framework operationalizes parity but enforcement requires resourcing + comparative-analysis volume; (b) ERISA preemption is constitutive jurisdictional architecture limiting state reach; (c) self-funded plans are predominant at large employers including anchor institutions, producing population-scale ERISA-gap exposure; (d) Medicaid rate inadequacy is not technically a parity violation when comparable medical/surgical networks face similar rate constraints, but produces operational access asymmetry; (e) commercial NQTL parity requires rigorous comparative analysis that plans + insurers structurally resist; (f) federal enforcement under Trump 2 admin uncertain [F1]; (g) provider workforce shortage is upstream of parity (cannot achieve in-network adequacy without provider supply); (h) procedural complexity of individual enforcement deters access.

**Element 4 (meaning for representation analysis):** SD5 represents the **parity-formal-existence + parity-operational-shortfall coexistence pattern** within D3, with three distinctive architectural seams: NQTL enforcement gap (federal + state); ERISA preemption gap (federal-only-reach); rate-driven access barrier (Medicaid pathway). Federal-rep leverage points: (1) Sept 2024 Final MHPAEA Rule implementation + enforcement [F1/P1; primary]; (2) CAA 2021 comparative analysis enforcement at DOL EBSA + HHS CCIIO + Treasury; (3) Congressional ERISA reform (rare but possible); (4) HHS OCR Section 504 + ADA parity-as-discrimination secondary route; (5) CMS pediatric Medicaid managed care parity oversight (intersects SD4); (6) federal court parity litigation (private right of action). State/local lever at PA Insurance Department + PA Mental Health Parity Act expansion + DBHIDS coordination. **MC5 cross-domain to D10 Labor** is the architectural-level cross-domain pathway for ERISA self-funded gap resolution.
