### SD5 Gap Analysis


**Sub-Domain:** SD5 Mental Health Parity
**Slug:** parity
**Date:** 2026-04-30
**Cycle:** RD2 first-pass; Gap Analysis SD5
**Authority:** RD2 §STEP 4; RD0 Standards 5.B/C/D/E
**Inputs:** track1-phase2-SD5; track2-phase2-SD5; substructure cross-cutting threads

---

#### §1 SD5 Gap Findings

##### G1-SD5-01: Three architectural seams of parity operational shortfall

**Characterization.** Parity formal-existence + operational-shortfall coexistence operates through three distinct architectural seams: (a) **NQTL enforcement gap** at network adequacy + prior-auth + medical-necessity criteria + provider reimbursement methodology; (b) **ERISA preemption gap** removing self-funded employer plans from state parity reach; (c) **rate-driven access barrier** in CBH that produces formal-parity-compliance + operational-access-asymmetry coexistence. The three seams operate through different statutory + jurisdictional mechanisms and require different remedial pathways. **Central SD5 representation-gap finding.**

**Evidence trace.** T1P2 §2.1-§2.6 (parity statutory architecture); §6.1-§6.2 (CBH + ERISA self-funded); T2P2 §4 four pathways; T2P2 §5 Element 4.
**Type:** [SD] structural derivation from architecture + documented operational pattern.
**Confidence:** HIGH for the three-seam pattern.
**Representation implication.** Federal-rep leverage at: Sept 2024 Final Rule enforcement [F1 → resolved per TC-01]; CAA 2021 comparative analysis at DOL/HHS/Treasury; Congressional ERISA reform (rare); HHS OCR secondary parity-as-discrimination route. State/local at PA ID + PA Mental Health Parity Act expansion + DBHIDS coordination.

---

##### G1-SD5-02: ERISA self-funded gap at anchor institutions as MC5 cross-domain finding

**Characterization.** Major Philadelphia anchor institutions (Penn, Temple, Jefferson, CHOP, Drexel + others) operate self-funded employer health plans subject to ERISA + DOL EBSA enforcement of MHPAEA but NOT to PA Mental Health Parity Act or PA Insurance Department oversight. Result: anchor-institution employees have weaker state-level parity protection than Medicaid beneficiaries through CBH or fully-insured commercial plan members through PA-regulated carriers. The gap is jurisdictional-architectural, not enforcement-level — state law is structurally preempted.

**Evidence trace.** T1P2 §2.6 (ERISA preemption); T1P2 §6.2 (anchor-institution gap); T1P2 §8 anchor analysis; T2P2 §4 Pathway A.
**Type:** [D] direct statutory documentation of ERISA preemption + [SD] structural derivation of anchor-institution-employee effect.
**Premises:** (1) ERISA § 514 preemption + savings clause documented; (2) anchor institutions documented as predominantly self-funded; (3) workforce composition includes substantial PA-3-resident share; (4) the gap therefore operates at population scale within PA-3.
**Confidence:** HIGH for the architectural finding; MEDIUM-HIGH for current PA-3 anchor-employee count [F-verify].
**Representation implication.** **MC5 cross-domain to D10 Labor** is the architectural-level home for this finding. SD5 establishes parity-side; D10 will receive the employer-plan-design dimension. Federal-rep lever: Congressional ERISA reform.

---

##### G1-SD5-03: Sept 2024 Final MHPAEA Rule status (revised per TC-01: formally paused)

*Verification update (2026-04-30): The status uncertainty noted in the original characterization has been resolved. See Material Changes Summary TC-01.*

**Characterization.** Sept 2024 Final MHPAEA Rule (89 Fed. Reg. 77,586) strengthened NQTL comparative analysis standards including network composition adequacy as NQTL. **Status verified 2026-04-30:** Tri-Agencies issued a non-enforcement statement May 15, 2025; the 2024 Rule's new provisions will not be enforced until ERIC litigation resolves + 18 months. The 2013 Final Rule and CAA 2021 NQTL obligations remain in force. The Rule was the central federal-rep parity advancement of recent years; the non-enforcement posture removes its forward-looking enforcement effect for the foreseeable horizon while the statute and 2013 Rule continue to govern.

**Evidence trace.** T1P2 §2.4; T1P2 §3 (regulatory layer); T1P2 §4.1 (DOL EBSA); T2P2 §5 Element 3; verification per Tri-Agency joint statement May 15, 2025 + ERIC v. DOL/HHS/Treasury (D.D.C., filed Jan 17, 2025).
**Type:** [D] direct documentation of Rule + [SD] structural derivation of forward-looking risk (now resolved).
**Confidence:** HIGH for the Rule + non-enforcement pattern (verified 2026-04-30); specific litigation timeline [F1 → resolved per TC-01].
**Representation implication.** Primary federal-rep verification flag for SD5. State-level adaptation possible via PA ID independent comparative-analysis-request authority under CAA 2021 framework, but federal lever is principal.

---

##### G1-SD5-04: Out-of-network MH utilization 3-8× medical/surgical baseline as parity operational marker

**Characterization.** National literature (RAND, GAO parity reports) documents 3-8× higher out-of-network MH/SUD utilization vs. medical/surgical for commercially-insured. Pattern reflects in-network adequacy NQTL gap; financial-burden selection effect; provider-acceptance asymmetry. Operative as parity-shortfall quantification baseline; specific PA-3 currency requires verification [F-verify FY26].

**Evidence trace.** T1P2 §6.1 (CBH parity); T2P2 §2.2 (utilization); T2P2 §4 Pathway A Step 4.
**Type:** [SD] structural derivation from national literature applied.
**Premises:** (1) National literature consistently documents 3-8× pattern; (2) PA-3 commercial market structurally similar to national; (3) anchor-institution self-funded plans not reported in PA ID data therefore obscured from state-level visibility; (4) PA-3 quantification structurally inferable from national pattern.
**Confidence:** MEDIUM-HIGH for national pattern; current PA-3 [F-verify].
**Representation implication.** Operational-shortfall measure useful for parity-enforcement prioritization. Federal lever at DOL + CCIIO comparative analysis prioritization.

---

##### G1-SD5-05: NQTL enforcement gap at network adequacy + prior-auth + medical-necessity criteria

**Characterization.** NQTL parity enforcement requires plan-level comparative analysis demonstrating MH/SUD NQTL applied no more stringently than medical/surgical. Documented gaps at: (a) network-composition adequacy (in-network MH provider count, geographic distribution, specialty mix); (b) prior-authorization frequency + denial rates; (c) medical-necessity criteria (often more restrictive for MH/SUD); (d) provider reimbursement methodology (lower MH/SUD rates relative to medical/surgical compounds network adequacy). Sept 2024 Rule strengthens enforcement framework; implementation pace uncertain [F1].

**Evidence trace.** T1P2 §2.1 (MHPAEA NQTL); T1P2 §2.4 (Sept 2024 Rule); T2P2 §2.2; §4 Pathway A.
**Type:** [SD] structural derivation.
**Confidence:** HIGH for the framework + gap pattern; specific FY26 enforcement metrics [F-verify].
**Representation implication.** Federal-rep lever at Sept 2024 Rule implementation; CMS Medicaid MCO comparative analysis oversight; PA ID fully-insured market authority.

---

##### G1-SD5-06: CBH formal compliance + rate-driven access barrier as Medicaid-pathway parity-distinct shortfall

**Characterization.** CBH operates under 42 C.F.R. Part 438 Subpart K + PA OMHSAS oversight; formally meets parity requirements. Rate-driven access barrier (provider-network capacity strained by Medicaid rate inadequacy) creates de facto access asymmetry that is not directly cognizable as parity violation under current NQTL frameworks because the comparable medical/surgical Medicaid network faces similar rate constraints. The result: formal compliance + operational shortfall coexist in Medicaid pathway in a way structurally distinct from commercial-pathway NQTL gaps. Resolution requires Medicaid rate adequacy intervention rather than parity-rule strengthening.

**Evidence trace.** T1P2 §6.1; T2P2 §4 Pathway B.
**Type:** [SD] structural derivation.
**Premises:** (1) CBH parity compliance attestation framework; (2) provider network capacity documented as rate-constrained; (3) medical/surgical Medicaid network similarly constrained; (4) the cross-network similarity removes the comparative-NQTL claim.
**Confidence:** MEDIUM-HIGH; specific quantification [F-verify].
**Representation implication.** Federal-rep lever at Medicaid rate methodology + CCBHC PPS expansion (rate-floor mechanism); state lever at PA Medicaid rate-setting.

## Sub-Domain 6: Forensic Mental Health

### SD6 Track 1 — Legal & Institutional Architecture


**Sub-Domain:** SD6 Forensic Mental Health
**Slug:** forensic
**Date:** 2026-04-30
**Cycle:** RD2 first-pass; Track 1 Phase 2 SD6
**Authority:** RD2 §STEP 2 Phase 2 structural template; RD0 Standards 9, 10, 11; depth caps Standard 16.C
**Source pairing:** source-workspace_D3_2026-04-30.md §§1-7
**Regime:** training-data primary; current-state items F-flagged

This document maps the legal architecture for forensic mental health in PA-3 — the constitutional foundation of competency + custody-MH doctrine, federal MH-court funding architecture, PA competency/NGRI/GBMI procedures, Philadelphia Mental Health Court + CIT + DBHIDS forensic unit, and the Philadelphia Prison System (PPS) as the operational center of the transinstitutionalization finding.

The analytical move SD6 establishes: **PPS as functionally Philadelphia's largest psychiatric facility (per documented daily MH-diagnosis rate among PPS daily census) is the operational fingerprint of transinstitutionalization, anchoring the cross-SD synthesis at SD7; Mental Health Court + CIT + DBHIDS forensic unit operate as diversion-and-reentry infrastructure that reaches a structurally small fraction of the eligible population; the reentry Medicaid suspension/reinstatement gap is the highest-leverage operational finding within the constitutional + statutory architecture.**

---

#### §1 Constitutional Authority

Forensic MH operates from a multi-case constitutional foundation:

- ***Dusky v. United States***, 362 U.S. 402 (1960): competency-to-stand-trial standard — "sufficient present ability to consult with his lawyer with a reasonable degree of rational understanding" + "rational as well as factual understanding of the proceedings." Operative for PA 50 P.S. § 7402 competency procedures.
- ***Jackson v. Indiana***, 406 U.S. 715 (1972): limit on competency-restoration detention — cannot detain incompetent defendant indefinitely if substantial probability of restoration not established. The "Jackson hold" framework.
- ***Sell v. United States***, 539 U.S. 166 (2003): four-prong test for forced medication to restore competency in non-emergency.
- ***Estelle v. Gamble***, 429 U.S. 97 (1976): Eighth Amendment deliberate-indifference-to-serious-medical-needs in custody; SMI = serious medical need framework.
- ***Brown v. Plata***, 563 U.S. 493 (2011): California-prison-MH-conditions Eighth Amendment ceiling — affirmed remedial population-cap order; central modern Eighth Amendment-MH-in-custody case.
- ***Pa. Dept. of Corr. v. Yeskey***, 524 U.S. 206 (1998): ADA Title II applies to state prisons. Operative across federal + state custodial settings.
- ***Washington v. Harper***, 494 U.S. 210 (1990): medication-over-objection in custody via internal administrative process meeting due-process requirements.

PA Const. Art. I § 13 (cruel punishments) provides PA-specific Eighth Amendment-floor; Art. I § 9 (criminal due process) operative for competency proceedings. 14th Amendment Equal Protection has been raised in race-disparate-MH-treatment-in-custody cases.

Constitutional gap relevant to SD6: federal Eighth Amendment + Fourteenth Amendment minimums set custody-MH-treatment ceilings + floors; PA architecture meets minimums; Philadelphia operational implementation in PPS subject to ongoing scrutiny [F-verify FY26 Eighth Amendment compliance posture]. The constitutional doctrine sets minimums, not optimums.

---

#### §2 Federal Statutory Layer

##### 2.1 Mentally Ill Offender Treatment and Crime Reduction Act (MIOTCRA)

P.L. 108-414 (2004); reauthorized 2008 (P.L. 110-416), 2016 (P.L. 114-255 § 14002 within Cures Act). Funds: mental health courts; jail diversion; CIT training; MH treatment in jails + prisons; reentry services. Administered through DOJ Bureau of Justice Assistance (BJA) + SAMHSA. Discretionary grant program; Philadelphia Mental Health Court + DBHIDS forensic-related grants pull from this stream historically. **Statutory stability: STABLE; appropriations subject to annual cycle**.

##### 2.2 Second Chance Act

P.L. 110-199 (2008); reauthorized 2018 (P.L. 115-391). Funds reentry services including BH continuity-of-care; specific reentry MH/SUD treatment grants. DOJ BJA administered. **Statutory stability: STABLE**.

##### 2.3 Medicaid suspension-not-termination + reentry framework

42 U.S.C. § 1396a(a)(81) prohibits Medicaid termination on incarceration alone (suspension allowed); state suspension-and-reinstatement frameworks variable. SUPPORT Act 2018 + subsequent legislation modified federal Medicaid-jail-eligibility framework including pre-release pilot programs (most recently the 1115 reentry waiver framework allowing pre-release Medicaid coverage for certain pre-release services). PA's 1115 reentry waiver status [F-verify FY26]. Operational implementation in Philadelphia includes Medicaid suspension on PPS entry + reinstatement on release; the reinstatement gap (delays + paperwork friction + automatic-reinstatement uneven) creates the high-risk first-2-4-weeks-post-release window.

##### 2.4 ADA in custody (Yeskey + Title II)

Operative throughout SD6; ADA covers competency proceedings, custody conditions, reentry services. DOJ CRD pattern-or-practice authority for systemic conditions claims.

##### 2.5 EMTALA (intersection)

42 U.S.C. § 1395dd; operative for individuals in custody who arrive at hospital ED for psychiatric stabilization (Philadelphia EDs receive PPS transports + others); the EMTALA obligation runs to the hospital regardless of custody status.

##### 2.6 Federal-court enforcement venues

§ 1983 + ADA Title II + Eighth Amendment private rights of action enforce conditions claims. Class certification + consent-decree pathways relevant for systemic remedy. *Plata*-style remediation framework precedent.

##### 2.7 Other federal anchors

- **PAIMI** (42 U.S.C. § 10801 et seq.) — DRP authority extends to facility-conditions investigation including PPS MH conditions narrow.
- **PREA** (Prison Rape Elimination Act, P.L. 108-79) — has BH-related compliance dimensions.
- **OBBBA Medicaid implementation [F13 → resolved per TC-03]** — verification confirms reentry continuity provisions preserved: CAA 2023 § 5121 youth Medicaid continuity unchanged; CAA 2024 Medicaid suspension-not-termination requirement (effective 2026) unchanged. OBBBA does not disrupt the reentry Medicaid framework architecture; downstream effects operate via general Medicaid eligibility changes (work requirements; 6-month redeterminations effective Dec 31, 2026) rather than reentry-specific provisions.

---

#### §3 Federal Regulatory Layer

- **28 C.F.R. § 35** — DOJ ADA Title II implementation including custody applications.
- **42 C.F.R. § 482.13(e)** — restraint/seclusion in psychiatric facilities receiving Medicare/Medicaid (operative for transfers from PPS to hospital settings).
- **CMS State Medicaid Director letters** on Medicaid-jail-eligibility framework + § 1115 reentry waiver guidance.
- **DOJ BJA program rules** for MIOTCRA + Second Chance Act grants.
- **DOJ CRD CRIPA** — Civil Rights of Institutionalized Persons Act regulatory framework (jail conditions investigations).

---

#### §4 Federal Agency Layer

##### 4.1 DOJ BJA + DOJ CRD

BJA administers MIOTCRA + Second Chance Act + JAG (Justice Assistance Grant) which can fund MH-court + diversion. CRD (Civil Rights Division) holds CRIPA + ADA Title II + § 1983-pattern-or-practice authority for custody conditions including PPS MH conditions if pursued. **Vulnerability: MODERATE-HIGH** under Trump 2 admin for CRD enforcement [F-verify FY26 CRD enforcement priorities].

##### 4.2 SAMHSA

Co-administrator of MIOTCRA grants with BJA; CIT training grants; jail-based BH treatment; reentry BH grants. **Vulnerability: HIGH** [F11 → resolved per TC-06]: comprehensive SAMHSA capacity erosion verified.

##### 4.3 CMS

Reentry Medicaid framework + § 1115 reentry waiver oversight. **Vulnerability: MODERATE-HIGH** [F13 → resolved per TC-03: CAA 2023 § 5121 youth + CAA 2024 suspension-not-termination frameworks unchanged by OBBBA; reentry continuity preserved].

##### 4.4 BOP (Federal Bureau of Prisons) narrow

Federal facility MH operations; FCI Philadelphia + FDC Philadelphia have MH operations distinct from PPS. **Not central to PA-3 forensic MH analysis**; cross-reference for federal-defendant population.

##### 4.5 HHS OCR + DOJ CRD Olmstead enforcement

Olmstead extends to forensic MH context (community-based alternatives to forensic commitment where appropriate). **Vulnerability: MODERATE-HIGH** under Trump 2 admin [F-verify].

##### 4.6 VA Veterans Justice Outreach (VJO) narrow

VA program embedding outreach specialists in courts to identify justice-involved veterans for VHA MH services; narrow PA-3 implementation but operative at Philadelphia Mental Health Court [F-verify FY26].

---

#### §5 State Layer

##### 5.1 PA competency procedures — 50 P.S. § 7401-7402

Defendant raised as incompetent → court orders competency evaluation by qualified examiner; finding of incompetency triggers Sell-/Jackson-framework treatment commitment if substantial restoration probability + commitment criteria met; Norristown State Hospital + Torrance State Hospital + Warren State Hospital as primary state forensic facilities; jail-based competency restoration narrow. [NSH 100% forensic since 2019 per TC-05; F5 → resolved per TC-05.]

##### 5.2 PA NGRI + GBMI — 18 Pa. C.S. § 314, § 315

NGRI (not guilty by reason of insanity, § 315): defendant proves by preponderance + commitment to MH facility post-acquittal until safe to release (similar to civil commitment 304 framework). GBMI (guilty but mentally ill, § 314): conviction stands, MH treatment ordered alongside sentence, served in DOC custody with MH services. The GBMI verdict in PA is operationally common; functionally a sentence-and-treatment hybrid. **Stability: STABLE**.

##### 5.3 PA Act 185 of 2020 — telehealth + court-mandated MH treatment + PA Act 98 of 2022 audio-only telehealth permanence

Telehealth expansion + court-mandated MH treatment as probation/parole condition framework; operative at probation/parole MH supervision intersection.

**PA Act 98 of 2022 — telehealth audio-only permanence [verified per TC-10]:** permanently removed PA DHS regulations prohibiting payment for audio-only telehealth in outpatient psychiatric clinics and outpatient drug & alcohol clinic services. Practical effect: outpatient BH provider use of audio-only telehealth no longer requires waiver request. Operative for PA-3 outpatient BH access continuity, particularly for transit-burdened or housing-unstable constituents engaged with reentry / probation / parole MH supervision.

##### 5.4 PA DOC + PA Probation/Parole

PA DOC operates state prison MH services for state-sentenced inmates; PA Probation and Parole supervises post-release populations including MH-condition supervision. PPS-to-PA-DOC transfers narrow (most Philadelphia-convicted serve in PA DOC if state sentence; PPS holds detention + Philadelphia-municipal-sentenced populations).

##### 5.5 PA Bureau of Community Corrections + reentry coordination

Coordinates PA DOC reentry; Medicaid reinstatement protocols. Philadelphia reentry coordination integrated.

##### 5.6 PA AOPC + court diversion architecture

Administrative Office of PA Courts oversees uniform court rules including MH diversion programs; Pennsylvania Mental Health Court coordination at state level; PA-AOPC reporting on MH-court outcomes.

---

#### §6 Local Layer

##### 6.1 Philadelphia Prison System

*Verification update (2026-04-30): PPS daily census and SMI subset figures revised per verified Philadelphia Prison Population Reports and Urban Institute SJC data. See Material Changes Summary TC-04.*

Six facilities including Curran-Fromhold Correctional Facility (CFCF), Detention Center, Riverside Correctional Facility, Cambridge Springs (women), and others. PPS daily census FY25-26 = ~3,500-3,700 [verified per TC-04: April 2025 = 3,575; May 2025 = 3,461 — lowest in 33 years since April 17, 1992; November 2025 = 3,674; PDP transition to new jail management system Fall 2024 created data gap October 2024 - February 2025]. SMI = 12.6% of total jail population per FJD 2022 / Urban Institute SJC. 12.6% × ~3,500-3,700 = **~440-470 daily SMI**. **The SMI population in PPS exceeds NSH bed capacity (375 forensic-only since 2019; see TC-05)** — the operational fingerprint of transinstitutionalization (developed at SD7 synthesis). Race + ethnicity now reported separately under new PDP system (March 2025 onward), enabling more precise demographic decomposition than prior reporting.

PPS MH services contracted (historically Corizon, then NaphCare, current contractor [F-verify]); jail-based BH including evaluation + medication management + crisis stabilization; specialized MH housing units including Mental Health Unit (MHU) at CFCF. Discharge planning + reentry MH coordination through DBHIDS forensic unit + community partners.

##### 6.2 Philadelphia Mental Health Court

Operates in Municipal Court (misdemeanor diversion) + Common Pleas (felony diversion). Voluntary participation; MH treatment compliance + court supervision; graduation framework with charge dismissal/reduction if successful. Capacity ~400-600/year [F-verify FY26]. Reaches small fraction of MH-eligible defendants in PPS (the substructure-anticipated finding); structural capacity limit.

##### 6.3 Philadelphia CIT

Crisis Intervention Team — 40-hour officer training program; ~25-30% PPD officer coverage [F-verify FY26]. CIT-trained officers respond to MH crisis calls preferentially when available. Right Care (DBHIDS-PPD interagency mobile crisis + co-responder) operates alongside CIT.

##### 6.4 DBHIDS forensic unit

Court consultations; jail diversion; pre-booking + post-booking diversion; reentry MH coordination; PPS-to-community BH transition planning. Anchor city-side forensic MH coordination structure.

##### 6.5 Philadelphia Family Court juvenile justice-BH intersection

Juvenile probation MH supervision; PA Title 42 Pa. C.S. § 6301 et seq. juvenile-justice framework intersection with SD4 children's BH; narrow tie at SD6.

##### 6.6 Reentry Medicaid suspension/reinstatement

Philadelphia DHS + PPS coordinate Medicaid suspension on entry + reinstatement on release; documented reinstatement-delay gap creating peak-crisis-risk first-2-4-weeks post-release window. The gap is operational — the framework exists but processing friction + automatic-reinstatement uneven creates the gap. **Highest-leverage operational finding** at SD6.

##### 6.7 Home Rule authority

Philadelphia Home Rule cannot supersede PA criminal procedure or PA DOC framework but coordinates pre-/post-booking diversion + Mental Health Court operations + PPS MH service contracting + reentry Medicaid coordination. Home Rule lever at MH-court expansion + CIT coverage expansion + Right Care integration with CIT.

---

#### §7 Cumulative Racial Disadvantage

SD6 is where the cumulative-disadvantage chain compounds at maximum density. Three threads:

**PPS racial composition + MH overlap**. Philadelphia jail population is disproportionately Black + Hispanic relative to PA-3 demographic baseline (national + state-level pattern); the MH-diagnosed PPS population therefore overrepresents Black + Hispanic PA-3 residents at population scale. The SMI subset within PPS — ~440-470 daily [TC-04] — is structurally weighted toward populations who experienced the SD3 voluntary-utilization underutilization → SD2 police-initiated 302 → SD6 PPS overrepresentation chain.

**Mental Health Court access asymmetry**. MH Court eligibility + voluntary-participation + treatment-completion patterns nationally show racial disparity in diversion outcomes. PA-3 specific quantification [F-verify]; structural pattern documented.

**Reentry Medicaid gap racial impact**. The reentry first-2-4-weeks crisis-risk window operates on a population that is racially weighted by the upstream PPS-composition pattern. The Medicaid reinstatement gap therefore disproportionately affects Black + Hispanic PA-3 residents.

**CIT coverage asymmetry**. CIT-trained officer ~25-30% coverage means many police-MH-crisis calls are responded to by non-CIT-trained officers. The geographic distribution of CIT-trained officer assignments + call patterns may produce racial disparity in CIT-vs.-non-CIT response [F-verify].

**SD6-specific implication**: the racial-equity chain culminates at SD6 — the upstream disparities (SD2 + SD3) compound into PPS overrepresentation; reentry Medicaid gap + MH Court access asymmetry + CIT coverage asymmetry compound at re-entry; the chain is documented across multiple steps and is racially traceable across each. Resolution requires upstream + diversion + reentry + CIT-expansion coordinated intervention — single-lever interventions do not interrupt the chain.

---

#### §8 Anchor Institution Thread

Per RD0 Standard 10 triple-role coverage:

**Philadelphia Prison System**. (i) Regulator/regulated: regulated by PA DOC standards (limited applicability) + DOJ CRD (CRIPA + ADA Title II) + PA AOPC + DBHIDS contract + PAIMI/DRP access; (regulator-influencing) operational standards + facility design + contract specifications. (ii) Resource/burden: jail-based BH services (resource within MH-services-in-custody framework); (burden) operational responsibility for de facto largest psychiatric facility in Philadelphia; financial + workforce burden. (iii) Structural beneficiary: state corrections funding + Medicaid limited (PPS-to-hospital transports + § 1115 reentry waivers); (excluded constituent) MH-diagnosed PPS population structurally excluded from community-based alternatives.

**Norristown State Hospital (NSH)**. (i)-(iii) Per SD2 Anchor analysis; SD6-specific role: forensic unit for competency-restoration commitments + NGRI commitments; capacity [F5/P2].

**Philadelphia Mental Health Court**. (i) Regulator-influencing through judicial standard-setting; (regulated by) PA AOPC + general judicial canons. (ii) Resource: diversion + treatment coordination; (burden) capacity-vs.-eligible-population mismatch. (iii) Structural beneficiary: federal + state grant funding; (excluded constituent advocate) operates partially as advocate for diverted population vs. unrepresented eligible population.

**Disability Rights Pennsylvania (DRP)**. PAIMI authority extends to PPS facility conditions investigation. SD6-specific role: structural advocate for PPS MH-diagnosed population.

**DBHIDS forensic unit**. (i)-(iii) City-side forensic MH coordination anchor; resource/burden/beneficiary/excluded-constituent parallels overall DBHIDS pattern.

The anchor institution profile for SD6 is dominated by PPS as the de facto-largest-psychiatric-facility finding — this is the analytical center.

### SD6 Track 2 — Outcomes, Geography & Representation


**Sub-Domain:** SD6 Forensic Mental Health
**Slug:** forensic
**Date:** 2026-04-30
**Cycle:** RD2 first-pass; Track 2 Phase 2 SD6
**Authority:** RD2 §STEP 3 Phase 2; RD0 Standards 1, 6, 7, 8, 13
**Source pairing:** source-workspace_D3_2026-04-30.md
**Regime:** training-data primary; current-state items F-flagged

---

#### §1 Data Provenance Statement

Philadelphia-specific direct: PA-3 ACS demographic baseline; D2-V cumulative racial-equity findings; PPS facility composition (Curran-Fromhold + Detention + Riverside + others); Philadelphia Mental Health Court framework; Right Care + CIT (also developed at SD7); DBHIDS forensic unit role; reentry Medicaid suspension/reinstatement protocols.

National benchmarks applied with structural inference: BJS jail population MH prevalence rates (~30-44% any MH; ~14-26% SMI national jail benchmarks); Treatment Advocacy Center transinstitutionalization analyses; mental health court evaluation literature; CIT coverage benchmarks; reentry Medicaid gap literature.

Statistical caveats: PPS data publication varies by reporting period; MH diagnosis methodology inconsistent across studies; reentry gap quantification structurally difficult. F-flags assigned for current-state.

---

#### §2 PA-3 Statistical Profile — Forensic Mental Health

##### 2.1 PPS population baseline

PPS daily census FY25-26 ~3,500-3,700 [verified per TC-04]. Composition:
- Pre-trial detention: substantial share (cash bail + pretrial-detention pattern post-2017 reforms variable)
- Sentenced (Philadelphia municipal sentences ≤2 years): subset
- State sentenced awaiting transfer to PA DOC: subset

PPS population is disproportionately Black + Hispanic relative to PA-3 demographic baseline; documented overrepresentation per PCS + Justice Department reporting [F-verify FY26].

##### 2.2 MH-diagnosed PPS population

SMI = 12.6% of total jail population per FJD 2022 / Urban Institute SJC [verified per TC-04]. Daily SMI population ~440-470 [12.6% × 3,500-3,700; verified per TC-04]. Daily MH-diagnosed PPS population (broader than SMI) per substructure baseline + national jail-benchmarks remains a verification target for the broader-than-SMI MH category.

The SMI population in PPS — ~440-470 daily [TC-04] — exceeds NSH bed capacity (NSH = 375 forensic-only since 2019 per TC-05; only remaining state psychiatric facility in southeastern PA). PPS therefore functions as Philadelphia's largest single psychiatric facility by SMI-population count.

##### 2.3 Diversion + treatment infrastructure capacity

- Philadelphia Mental Health Court (Municipal + Common Pleas combined): ~400-600/year capacity [F-verify]
- DBHIDS forensic unit court consultations + diversion: capacity [F-verify]
- CIT-trained officers: ~25-30% PPD coverage [F-verify]
- Right Care zone coverage: partial geographic + 24/7 [F3/P1] — also developed SD7

Eligible-vs.-served gap: thousands of PPS-MH-diagnosed individuals annually; MH Court reaches hundreds. The structural capacity-vs.-eligibility mismatch is the SD6 access-pathway finding.

##### 2.4 Reentry Medicaid gap quantification

Philadelphia PPS releases ~24-30K individuals annually [F-verify]; subset MH-diagnosed ~30-40% per PPS baseline = ~7-12K MH-diagnosed releases annually. The first 2-4 weeks post-release window has documented elevated crisis + suicide + overdose risk. Medicaid reinstatement processing time + automatic-reinstatement uneven implementation creates the gap [F-verify FY26 PA reinstatement protocol metrics + § 1115 reentry waiver implementation].

---

#### §3 Geographic Variation

##### 3.1 North/Northwest Philadelphia Core

Highest arrest rates + highest MH-diagnosis among arrested + highest reentry-Medicaid-gap impact. Cumulative-disadvantage geography (redlined-mapped + Black-population-concentrated) maps to PPS-population-source geography. CIT-trained-officer geographic distribution + Right Care zone coverage variable across this sub-area.

##### 3.2 West Philadelphia Core

Bifurcated. University City policing pattern + adjacent neighborhoods closer to North Core arrest rates. Right Care + CIT coverage variable; Drexel-area + Penn-area policing distinct from adjacent neighborhoods.

##### 3.3 Northwest Philadelphia

Heterogeneous; lower-income tracts closer to North Core pattern.

##### 3.4 South/Southwest Philadelphia

Lower arrest rate baseline than North Core; some sub-area variation. PPS-population-source share lower.

##### 3.5 Boundary-adjacent

NSH (Montgomery County) for forensic commitments; Torrance + Warren State Hospitals (more distant) for state-hospital-system forensic commitments. PA DOC facilities receive state-sentenced PPS transfers.

---

#### §4 Aggregate Pathway Tracing

**Pathway A: PA-3 resident with SMI arrested for misdemeanor:**
1. Police-MH-crisis call OR routine-encounter-with-MH-symptoms
2. CIT-trained officer (~25-30% probability) vs. non-CIT response; CIT can route to mobile-crisis or Right Care for diversion vs. arrest; non-CIT structurally arrest-tilted
3. Booking + initial MH screen at PPS intake
4. Pre-arraignment MH evaluation (DBHIDS forensic unit) + MH-Court eligibility screening
5. Decision pathways: bail + community supervision; pretrial detention in PPS; MH-Court diversion (charge resolution + treatment); negotiated plea
6. If detained: MH services in PPS during detention; release with reentry plan + Medicaid reinstatement

Breakdown points: Step 2 CIT coverage; Step 4 MH-Court capacity; Step 6 reentry Medicaid gap.

**Pathway B: PA-3 resident with SMI arrested for felony:**
1. Arrest + booking + PPS detention
2. Competency evaluation if raised (PA 50 P.S. § 7402); finding of incompetency → restoration commitment to NSH or jail-based restoration if available
3. Restoration period; Sell/Jackson constraints applied; restoration vs. dismissal decision
4. If competent: trial + verdict; NGRI / GBMI / standard verdict
5. NGRI: post-acquittal commitment to NSH (parallel to civil 304); GBMI: PA DOC custody with MH services; conviction: PA DOC or PPS for short sentences
6. Reentry: discharge planning; Medicaid reinstatement; community BH coordination

Breakdown points: Step 2 NSH bed capacity for restoration commitments; Step 3 prolonged restoration period; Step 6 reentry gap.

**Pathway C: PPS release with SMI returning to PA-3 community:**
1. Pre-release planning at PPS (DBHIDS forensic unit + PPS contractor + community providers)
2. Release date; transportation home; medication supply
3. First 14-30 days: highest crisis + suicide + overdose risk window (national + PA documented)
4. Medicaid reinstatement: nominal automatic + processing-friction reality; effective coverage may lag release by days to weeks
5. Community BH appointment access: 4-8 week wait standard (Pathway A SD3); structural mismatch with peak-crisis-risk window
6. Re-entry to community: housing + family + employment + BH service coordination

Breakdown points: Steps 4-5 — the Medicaid reinstatement gap + outpatient wait time + peak-crisis-risk window form a compound: peak risk + minimum service access. **Highest-leverage operational finding** at SD6.

**Pathway D: Police-MH-crisis call to PPD dispatch:**
1. 911 or 988 call → PPD dispatch decision
2. Right Care availability + zone coverage [F3] vs. CIT-trained officer + uniformed response
3. On-scene assessment: voluntary transport to ED; involuntary 302 by MD post-scene; arrest if criminal; Right Care de-escalation + community resource referral
4. Outcomes: ED → admission/discharge; PPS booking → forensic pathway; community-resource referral with continuity gap

Breakdown points: Step 1-2 dispatch decision; Step 3 outcome variability; cross-cutting to SD2 + SD7.

---

#### §5 Representation Question

**Element 1 (formal provision):** Constitutional MH-treatment-in-custody floor (Estelle/Plata/Yeskey/Harper); Dusky/Jackson/Sell competency framework; PA NGRI/GBMI verdicts; PA Act 185 court-mandated treatment authority; MIOTCRA + Second Chance Act federal funding for MH-court + diversion + reentry; § 1115 reentry waiver framework; ADA Title II in custody; PAIMI advocacy authority; Philadelphia Mental Health Court + DBHIDS forensic unit coordination.

**Element 2 (actual receipt):** PPS as functionally largest psychiatric facility in Philadelphia (~440-470 SMI daily exceeds NSH 375 forensic-only [TC-04 + TC-05]); Mental Health Court capacity (~400-600/yr) reaches structurally small fraction of eligible (thousands annually); CIT-trained officer ~25-30% coverage means majority of police-MH-crisis calls non-CIT response; reentry Medicaid suspension/reinstatement gap creates first 2-4 week peak-crisis-risk window with effective coverage lagging release; cumulative racial-equity chain culminates at PPS overrepresentation; constitutional-floor PPS MH compliance subject to ongoing scrutiny [F-verify].

**Element 3 (account for the gap):** Multiple compounding causes: (a) deinstitutionalization-without-community-infrastructure chain produces transinstitutionalization to jails; (b) MH Court structural capacity limited by judicial + treatment + supervision resourcing; (c) CIT coverage limited by training capacity + officer rotation; (d) reentry Medicaid suspension framework operationally clunky despite federal + state intent to streamline; (e) federal agency capacity erosion at SAMHSA + DOJ CRD threatens MIOTCRA + civil rights enforcement; (f) PA state-hospital bed reduction over decades constrains forensic-restoration capacity; (g) PPS population overrepresentation by race compounds upstream SD2 + SD3 racial-equity chain.

**Element 4 (meaning for representation analysis):** SD6 is the **operational center of the transinstitutionalization finding** — PPS as functionally largest psychiatric facility is the documented structural outcome of community-infrastructure underbuilt + state-hospital reduction + commitment-pathway dominance. Reentry Medicaid gap is the highest-leverage operational finding in SD6 because it sits at the intersection of federal architecture (Medicaid suspension framework) + state implementation (reinstatement protocol) + local execution (PPS release coordination) — federal-rep leverage exists at every layer. Federal-rep leverage points: (1) § 1115 reentry waiver implementation + CMS oversight; (2) MIOTCRA + Second Chance Act appropriations + administration; (3) DOJ CRD CRIPA + ADA Title II enforcement (vulnerability under Trump 2 admin per F-verify); (4) SAMHSA jail-diversion + reentry MH grants; (5) Medicaid rate-setting affecting community BH absorptive capacity for reentry population. State/local at PA reentry waiver implementation + MH-Court expansion + CIT coverage expansion + Right Care integration. **Cross-cutting** to SD7 synthesis (transinstitutionalization quantified; cumulative racial-equity chain final step).

#### §1 SD6 Gap Findings

##### G1-SD6-01: PPS as functionally largest psychiatric facility in Philadelphia (transinstitutionalization operational fingerprint)

*Verification update (2026-04-30): Prior pass derived ~450-825 daily SMI from PPS census ~4,500-5,500 × 30-40% MH × 10-15% SMI subset, compared against NSH ~300 beds (assumed civil + forensic). Verified state: PPS daily census FY25-26 = ~3,500-3,700 (April 2025 = 3,575; May 2025 = 3,461 — lowest in 33 years since April 17, 1992; November 2025 = 3,674); SMI = 12.6% of total jail population per FJD 2022 / Urban Institute SJC. 12.6% × ~3,500-3,700 = ~440-470 daily SMI. NSH = 375 beds, 100% forensic (255 Regional Forensic + 120 Forensic Stepdown) since civil section closed January 2019 (see TC-05). The central finding HOLDS but at narrower margin than prior pass implied: PPS SMI ~440-470 vs. NSH 375 forensic-only = ~65-95 margin. See Material Changes Summary TC-04 + TC-05.*

**Characterization.** PPS daily SMI-diagnosed population ~440-470 exceeds NSH forensic-only bed capacity (375). PPS therefore functions operationally as Philadelphia's largest single psychiatric facility by SMI-population count. This is the documented operational fingerprint of the transinstitutionalization chain: deinstitutionalization (1960s onward) → community infrastructure underbuilt → state-hospital bed reduction → SMI population displaced into jails (and emergency departments and homelessness). **Central SD6 finding; anchors SD7 transinstitutionalization synthesis.**

**Evidence trace.** T1P2 §6.1 (PPS); T1P2 §7 (cumulative racial disadvantage); T2P2 §2.2 (MH-diagnosed PPS population); substructure §134 transinstitutionalization thread; verification of PPS census and SMI rate via Philadelphia Prison Population Reports (May 2025; November 2025) and Urban Institute SJC March 2023 report.
**Type:** [SD] structural derivation from verified PPS census + verified SMI rate + NSH capacity comparison.
**Premises (revised per TC-04):** (1) PPS daily census FY25-26 = ~3,500-3,700 [verified via PDP monthly snapshot reports]; (2) SMI = 12.6% of total jail population [FJD 2022; Urban Institute SJC, March 2023]; (3) 12.6% × ~3,500-3,700 = ~440-470 daily SMI; (4) NSH = 375 beds, 100% forensic since 2019 [verified via pa.gov; see TC-05]; (5) the comparison establishes PPS-larger-than-NSH for SMI-population by count, at narrower margin (~65-95) than prior pass implied. Forward-looking note: planned Southeast Psychiatric Treatment Center expansion (270 → 420 beds; groundbreaking 2026) will further narrow this margin.
**Confidence:** HIGH for the structural pattern (verified); HIGH for current FY26 quantification [F5/F6 → resolved per TC-04 + TC-05]; the margin is narrower than prior pass implied but the central finding holds.
**Representation implication.** Federal-rep leverage at: MIOTCRA reauthorization + appropriations; § 1115 reentry waiver; CMS reentry framework (CAA 2023 § 5121 + CAA 2024 unchanged by OBBBA per TC-03); DOJ CRD CRIPA enforcement (vulnerability F-verify); SAMHSA jail-diversion grants (TC-06 capacity erosion). State/local at: PA Medicaid + state-hospital sustainability; PPS MH services + capacity; MH-Court expansion. **Cross-cutting** to SD7 synthesis (operational center).

---

##### G1-SD6-02: Reentry Medicaid suspension/reinstatement gap as highest-leverage operational finding

**Characterization.** Medicaid suspension framework on PPS entry + reinstatement on release nominally automatic per federal + state architecture; operational reality includes processing-friction + automatic-reinstatement-uneven gap such that effective Medicaid coverage may lag release by days to weeks. The first 14-30 days post-release window has documented elevated crisis + suicide + overdose risk. The gap therefore sits at the intersection of peak-crisis-risk window + minimum-service-access. Highest-leverage operational finding because federal-rep leverage exists at every architectural layer (federal Medicaid framework + state implementation + local execution).

**Evidence trace.** T1P2 §6.6 (reentry Medicaid coordination); T2P2 §2.4 (reentry quantification); T2P2 §4 Pathway C (reentry pathway).
**Type:** [D] direct documentation of statutory + administrative architecture + [SD] structural derivation of compound effect.
**Premises:** (1) 42 U.S.C. § 1396a(a)(81) Medicaid suspension framework; (2) PA reinstatement protocol [F-verify]; (3) ~7-12K MH-diagnosed PPS releases annually [F-verify]; (4) first-2-4-weeks elevated crisis-risk window documented; (5) outpatient appointment wait time 4-8 weeks (SD3 Pathway A).
**Confidence:** HIGH for the structural compound; MEDIUM-HIGH for current FY26 quantification [F-verify].
**Representation implication.** Multi-layer federal-rep leverage: § 1115 reentry waiver implementation; CMS state plan oversight; congressional Medicaid reform; MIOTCRA reentry funding. State at PA reentry waiver + DHS reinstatement protocol; local at DBHIDS forensic unit + PPS coordination + reentry MH appointment-availability priority.

---

##### G1-SD6-03: Mental Health Court capacity vs. eligible population mismatch

**Characterization.** Philadelphia Mental Health Court (Municipal + Common Pleas combined) capacity ~400-600/year reaches structurally small fraction of MH-eligible PPS population (thousands annually). The capacity-vs.-eligible-population mismatch is structural — not enforcement-level — limited by judicial + treatment-supervision + provider-network-absorption resourcing.

**Evidence trace.** T1P2 §6.2 (MH Court); T2P2 §2.3 (diversion infrastructure capacity); T2P2 §4 Pathway A.
**Type:** [SD] structural derivation.
**Premises:** (1) MH Court capacity baseline ~400-600/yr [F-verify]; (2) PPS MH-diagnosed annual flow thousands [F-verify]; (3) the order-of-magnitude mismatch establishes structural gap.
**Confidence:** MEDIUM-HIGH; specific FY26 [F-verify].
**Representation implication.** Federal-rep leverage at MIOTCRA expansion + Bureau of Justice Assistance grants; state at PA AOPC + PA general fund judicial appropriation; local at DBHIDS forensic unit + provider-network absorptive capacity expansion.

---

##### G1-SD6-04: CIT coverage rate (~25-30% PPD) as MH-crisis-response capacity floor

**Characterization.** Crisis Intervention Team training reaches approximately 25-30% of PPD officers [F-verify]. The result: majority of police-MH-crisis calls are responded to by non-CIT-trained officers. Combined with Right Care zone + 24/7 coverage gaps [F3], MH-crisis response coverage is structurally inadequate. CIT expansion + Right Care expansion are coordinated levers.

**Evidence trace.** T1P2 §6.3 (CIT); T2P2 §2.3 + §4 Pathway D (police-MH-crisis dispatch).
**Type:** [SD] structural derivation.
**Confidence:** MEDIUM-HIGH for the pattern; specific FY26 [F-verify].
**Representation implication.** Federal at MIOTCRA CIT training grants; state lever modest; local at PPD + DBHIDS Right Care integration + city budget allocation. **Cross-cutting** to SD7 (Right Care expansion) and to D17 Public Safety.

##### G1-SD6-05: Cumulative racial-equity chain culminates at PPS overrepresentation + reentry gap

**Characterization.** The cumulative-disadvantage chain — voluntary-utilization underutilization (SD3) → police-initiated 302 disparity (SD2) → PPS SMI overrepresentation (SD6) → reentry gap impact disproportionately on Black + Hispanic releases (SD6) — culminates at SD6. Each step is documented + racially traceable; the chain compounds. **Final-step finding** in the racial-equity chain that SD7 synthesis integrates.

**Type:** [SD] cross-domain pattern recognition; structural derivation.

**Confidence:** HIGH for the chain pattern; MEDIUM-HIGH for current PA-3 quantification [F-verify].
**Representation implication.** Multi-level intervention required to interrupt chain: upstream voluntary-system access (SD3) + police-MH-crisis-response reform (CIT + Right Care) + commitment-system reform (SD2) + diversion expansion + reentry-gap closure. Single-lever interventions documented as insufficient. Resolves at SD7 synthesis as central racial-equity finding.

**Characterization.** PA state-hospital bed reduction over decades constrains forensic-restoration commitment capacity at NSH + Torrance + Warren. The state-hospital capacity constraint compounds with Sell/Jackson constraints (cannot detain indefinitely; restoration must be substantially probable).

Under Trump 2 admin, CRD enforcement priorities under transition [F-verify FY26 enforcement priorities]. PPS conditions including MH services subject to ongoing scrutiny but federal enforcement vulnerability creates forward-looking risk.

## Sub-Domain 7: Psychiatric Crisis Infrastructure

### SD7 Track 1 — Legal & Institutional Architecture


**Sub-Domain:** SD7 Psychiatric Crisis Infrastructure (+ Synthesis Charter)
**Slug:** crisisInfra
**Date:** 2026-04-30
**Cycle:** RD2 first-pass; Track 1 Phase 2 SD7
**Authority:** RD2 §STEP 2 Phase 2 structural template; RD0 Standards 9, 10, 11; depth caps Standard 16.C
**Source pairing:** source-workspace_D3_2026-04-30.md
**Regime:** training-data primary; current-state items F-flagged

This document maps the legal architecture for psychiatric crisis infrastructure in PA-3 — the 988 Lifeline framework, EMTALA, CMS CoP for psychiatric facilities, PA Act 71 of 2014 + OMHSAS mobile crisis + CSU standards, Philadelphia CRC + Right Care + crisis residential infrastructure, and the boarding-crisis intersection where SD2 + SD3 + SD6 capacity gaps converge.

The analytical move SD7 establishes: **Crisis infrastructure is the operational nexus where all prior SDs converge — community-services failures (SD3) and pediatric BH gaps (SD4) terminate operationally in crisis pathway entry; commitment-system patterns (SD2) drive 302 entry through ED and crisis settings; parity gaps (SD5) compound by reducing voluntary-pathway access; forensic patterns (SD6) drive police-routed crisis entry. The crisis system's design therefore reflects every prior SD's gaps, and Right Care expansion + 988 mobile-crisis dispatch capacity + CRC + CSU expansion are coordinated levers. SD7 also functions as substructure-designated synthesis charter for D3 cross-cutting integration (handled in separate synthesis document).**

---

#### §1 Constitutional Authority

EMTALA-grounded right-to-screening-and-stabilization (42 U.S.C. § 1395dd) is statutory not constitutional but provides the operational floor for ED-mediated psychiatric stabilization. 14th Amendment Due Process operative in 302 entry pathways through ED settings (constitutional minimum protections from SD2 carry through). Eighth Amendment operative narrowly in custody-crisis intersection (SD6 carry-through). Olmstead integration mandate operative for community-based crisis alternatives to inpatient + custody. PA Const. Art. I § 1 + § 9 floors operative.

Constitutional gap relevant to SD7: federal + state constitutional minimums set crisis-system baseline; the operational expression of crisis-system architecture (CRC + Right Care + 988 + CSU + crisis residential) operates within constitutional constraint but can fall below constitutional ideal — psychiatric boarding crisis (24-72+ hour ED holds) is documented operational shortfall that has not produced systemic constitutional remedy.

---

#### §2 Federal Statutory Layer

##### 2.1 988 Suicide and Crisis Lifeline Act

42 U.S.C. § 290bb-36c; FCC designation of 988 as universal three-digit suicide + crisis number (effective July 16, 2022). Routes calls + texts to National Suicide Prevention Lifeline successor network; state-level mobile crisis dispatch + crisis-center-coordination architecture varies by state.

*Verification update (2026-04-30): Prior pass framed 988 dispatch nationally (~1-2% national benchmark) and noted PA-3 specifics required verification. Verified state per TC-08: Philadelphia operates the **Philadelphia Crisis Line (PCL) — the only locally-based, in-house 988 response team in the country**. PCL inception January 2023. ~6,000 calls/month. 988 counselors co-located in 911 Radio Room → 36% increase in warm transfers from 911 to 988. 4,288 calls transferred 911 → PCL January 2023 - February 2025. The PCL architecture represents a substantial departure from the national pattern and partially closes the 988-dispatch gap at the PA-3 level. See Material Changes Summary TC-08.*

PA 988 operations under PA OMHSAS coordination; national 988 mobile crisis dispatch rate documented as low (~1-2% national benchmark). **Philadelphia operates the Philadelphia Crisis Line (PCL) — the only locally-based, in-house 988 response team in the country [verified per TC-08]**: ~6,000 calls/month; PCL inception January 2023; 988 counselors co-located in 911 Radio Room produces 36% increase in warm transfers from 911 to 988; 4,288 calls transferred 911 → PCL January 2023 - February 2025. The PCL architecture represents a substantial departure from the national pattern and partially closes the 988-dispatch gap at the PA-3 level [F7 + F12 → partially resolved per TC-08 at PCL coverage; mobile crisis dispatch rate from PCL specifically remains a verification target]. **Statutory stability: STABLE; funding sustainability state-variable**.

##### 2.2 EMTALA

42 U.S.C. § 1395dd. EMTALA requires Medicare-participating hospitals with EDs to provide medical screening examination + stabilization for emergency medical conditions including psychiatric emergencies. Does not require psychiatric inpatient admission; requires stabilization to point of transfer or discharge safety. Operative for ED psychiatric boarding pathway: hospitals fulfill EMTALA by holding patients in ED until inpatient placement available — boarding ensues. **Stability: STABLE; psychiatric-emergency interpretation periodically litigated**.

##### 2.3 CMS Conditions of Participation — restraint/seclusion

42 C.F.R. § 482.13(e). Patient's-rights condition for hospitals participating in Medicare/Medicaid; documentation + medical review + notification requirements for restraint + seclusion. Operative in crisis-receiving facilities. **Stability: STABLE; periodic amendments**.

##### 2.4 No Surprises Act

P.L. 116-260 Div BB Title I. Addresses surprise out-of-network billing for emergency services including emergency MH/SUD; independent dispute resolution framework. Operative in crisis-pathway-with-out-of-network-billing scenarios.

##### 2.5 Bipartisan Safer Communities Act 2022

P.L. 117-159 (June 25, 2022). Expanded CCBHC nationally; mental health funding + 988 implementation support + school-based BH expansion. Operative for crisis-system capacity expansion + 988 build-out.

##### 2.6 IMD exclusion + SUPPORT Act

42 U.S.C. § 1396d(a)(B) IMD exclusion limits Medicaid match for adult inpatient psychiatric facilities >16 beds; SUPPORT Act § 1012 IMD parity for SUD partial waiver. Operative for crisis-pathway-to-inpatient capacity (carry-through SD2 G1-SD2-03 finding). Federal Medicaid reform of IMD exclusion is a primary federal-rep lever for crisis-to-inpatient capacity.

##### 2.7 ACA EHB MH/SUD inclusion

42 U.S.C. § 18022(b)(1)(E). Mandatory MH/SUD inclusion in qualified health plans. Operative for crisis-pathway insurance coverage.

##### 2.8 Other federal anchors

- **Veterans Crisis Line + 988 integration** (Veterans Crisis Line preserved as press-1 routing within 988); operative for veteran population — narrow PA-3 cross-reference [D24].
- **MIOTCRA** (P.L. 108-414) — funds CIT + co-responder + crisis intervention training including DBHIDS-PPD Right Care infrastructure.
- **OBBBA Medicaid implementation** [F13 → resolved per TC-03] — affects general Medicaid eligibility (work requirements + 6-month redeterminations effective Dec 31, 2026) which downstream affects crisis-pathway insurance churn; community capacity affected via state-directed payment caps phase-in FY28-34; CCBHC + FQHC exemptions protect key crisis-system streams.

---

#### §3 Federal Regulatory Layer

- **42 C.F.R. § 482.13(e)** — restraint/seclusion (above).
- **42 C.F.R. Part 482 generally** — hospital CoP including ED psychiatric capacity standards.
- **47 C.F.R. § 52.15** — FCC 988 designation.
- **HHS SAMHSA crisis-care guidance** including 2020 "National Guidelines for Behavioral Health Crisis Care" + subsequent.
- **42 C.F.R. Part 438** — Medicaid managed care including crisis-services coverage requirements.
- **CMS Conditions of Participation for Psychiatric Hospitals** — 42 C.F.R. § 482.60-62.

---

#### §4 Federal Agency Layer

##### 4.1 SAMHSA — primary 988 + crisis-system administrator

988 Lifeline operations + SAMHSA crisis-services grants + State Opioid Response (SOR) for crisis-SUD. SAMHSA Crisis Care Continuum framework (someone-to-call + someone-to-respond + safe-place-to-be) is the structural model crisis-system planning operates against. **Vulnerability: HIGH** [F11 → resolved per TC-06]: comprehensive SAMHSA capacity erosion verified; HHS contingency plan (Jan 30, 2026) retains 988 as excepted in shutdown — partial protection for crisis line continuity.

##### 4.2 CMS

Medicaid coverage of crisis services + MCO oversight of crisis-network adequacy + IMD exclusion + § 1115 SUD IMD waivers. **Vulnerability: MODERATE-HIGH** [F13 → resolved per TC-03: IMD exclusion architecture unchanged by OBBBA; CCBHC/FQHC/RHC cost-sharing exemption protects key crisis-pathway streams].

##### 4.3 FCC

988 number designation + telecommunications infrastructure for crisis-line routing. **Vulnerability: MODERATE**.

##### 4.4 VA

Veterans Crisis Line operations + integration with 988. **Vulnerability: MODERATE**.

##### 4.5 HHS OCR + DOJ CRD

Olmstead enforcement re: community-based crisis alternatives. **Vulnerability: MODERATE-HIGH** under Trump 2 admin [F-verify].

##### 4.6 DOJ BJA

MIOTCRA + Justice Assistance Grants funding co-responder + CIT + crisis intervention. **Vulnerability: MODERATE-HIGH**.

The federal agency layer for SD7 spreads across SAMHSA + CMS + FCC + VA + DOJ — multi-agency coordination required for full crisis-system functioning. Cumulative erosion across multiple agencies compounds.

---

#### §5 State Layer

##### 5.1 PA Act 71 of 2014 — MH crisis services authorization

Provides state authorization framework for MH crisis services including mobile crisis + crisis residential + crisis stabilization unit (CSU). PA OMHSAS administers crisis-service standards.

##### 5.2 PA OMHSAS mobile crisis + CSU standards

State licensure + service standards for mobile crisis + crisis stabilization units. PA-3 mobile crisis operations through DBHIDS contract; CSU operations through DBHIDS-contracted crisis residential providers.

##### 5.3 PA 988 implementation

PA OMHSAS coordinates 988 state-level operations + 988 dispatch architecture. State funding sustainability [F12/P2]; some states have telecom surcharge funding mechanism, others rely on general fund + federal grants.

##### 5.4 PA DOH ED psychiatric standards

PA DOH licenses hospitals + EDs; psychiatric-emergency-services standards within hospital licensure framework.

##### 5.5 PA Insurance Department + crisis services parity

Crisis services subject to MHPAEA + ACA EHB parity requirements (SD5 carry-through).

---

#### §6 Local Layer

##### 6.1 Philadelphia CRC — Crisis Response Center

1229 N. 3rd Street; 24/7 walk-in psychiatric crisis evaluation; ~25-30 capacity; ~20-25K visits/year [F-verify]. Operates as alternative to ED for psychiatric crisis presentation; partial geographic + walk-in-only access pattern.

##### 6.2 Crisis mobile response architecture (CMCRT + CIRT per TC-07; project shorthand "Right Care")

*Verification update (2026-04-30): Section retitled per TC-07 to reflect verified two-program architecture. CMCRT = Community Mobile Crisis Response Teams (civilian, 4 providers Elwyn / PATH / Consortium / JFK; 29 teams 24/7 across Philadelphia); CIRT = Crisis Intervention Response Team (police+civilian co-responder, 911-routed). "Right Care" terminology not surfaced in current DBHIDS sources; retained in body as project shorthand per remediation §0.3 and UV-03.*

CMCRT operates as the civilian-only mobile crisis component: 4 nonprofit providers (Elwyn / PATH / Consortium / JFK); 29 teams 24/7 across Philadelphia; ~14,793 dispatches January 2023 - February 2025; 70%+ user satisfaction; **17% decrease in involuntary commitment referrals**; CMCRT response time averaging 50 min (down from 60+; goal 30 min). Pilot started 2021 after Walter Wallace Jr. death (October 2020). CIRT operates as the police+civilian co-responder component, 911-routed. Coverage expansion across both components is the highest-impact infrastructure intervention for crisis-system improvement (substructure-anticipated finding; verified per TC-07). Active contract dispute: Mayor Parker administration attempting to sever $3.8M Consortium contract over tax-exempt-status default; Consortium appealing; still operating as of FY26 testimony (May 2025). Integration with 988 dispatch architecture is via PCL co-location (see §6.5 below; TC-08).

##### 6.3 Crisis residential

DBHIDS-contracted crisis residential providers; capacity consistently full per documentation [F-verify]. Alternative to inpatient + jail for sub-acute crisis stabilization.

##### 6.4 Hospital ED psychiatric capacity

Major Philadelphia hospital EDs (Penn Presbyterian, Temple Episcopal, Jefferson, Hahnemann historic [closed July 2019], CHOP for pediatric) operate psychiatric-capable EDs. Psychiatric boarding 24-72+ hour holds documented (SD2 G1-SD2-03 finding); IMD-exclusion + state-hospital-saturation + private-bed-scarcity compound transmits to ED settings.

##### 6.5 988 Philadelphia operations

988 calls + texts route through PA OMHSAS-coordinated network; Philadelphia call-volume + dispatch-rate [F7 + F12 → partially resolved per TC-08: PCL operational data verified — only locally-based, in-house 988 response team in country; ~6,000 calls/month; January 2023 inception]. Mobile crisis dispatch from 988 nationally low (1-2%); PCL routes mobile crisis dispatch to CMCRT teams [TC-07 + TC-08].

##### 6.6 Veterans Crisis Line + 988 integration

Press-1 routing for veterans; PA-3 veteran population MH crisis-line integration with VA Philadelphia Medical Center coordination [F-verify].

##### 6.7 Hahnemann closure precedent

Hahnemann University Hospital closed July 2019; psychiatric ED + inpatient capacity loss precedent. Documented as contributing to current capacity-strain pattern. Historical reference; not active capacity.

##### 6.8 Home Rule authority

DBHIDS Right Care interagency authority + crisis-services coordination + CRC operations (anchor city-side crisis infrastructure); coordination with PPD; CBH crisis-services authorization. Limits: cannot supersede PA OMHSAS clinical standards; cannot relax MHPAEA; subject to PA + federal oversight.

---

#### §7 Cumulative Racial Disadvantage

SD7 is where the cumulative-disadvantage chain compounds at maximum density on the crisis-pathway-entry dimension:

**Crisis-via-police vs. crisis-via-self-direction racial pattern**. Black PA-3 residents documented as more likely to enter crisis system through police (SD6 CIT + Right Care; SD2 police-initiated 302) than through self-direction or family-mediated pathway (988 self-call; CRC walk-in; ED self-presentation). The pattern is the entry-pathway expression of the cumulative-disadvantage chain.

**988 dispatch + Right Care coverage geographic asymmetry**. 988 mobile-crisis dispatch + Right Care zone coverage variable across PA-3 sub-areas; lower-resource neighborhoods (cumulative-disadvantage geography) may have lower coverage [F-verify].

**ED psychiatric boarding burden distribution**. ED boarding burden falls disproportionately on hospitals serving lower-resource populations (Temple Episcopal, Penn Presbyterian for North/Northwest Core) vs. commercial-priority hospitals (CHOP private; Penn Center City). The geographic + demographic distribution of boarding-burden compounds across cumulative-disadvantage geography.

**CRC walk-in access pattern**. CRC at 1229 N. 3rd Street is geographically positioned near North Philadelphia; access pattern includes substantial Black + Hispanic representation but transportation + voluntary-engagement factors variable across PA-3 sub-areas [F-verify utilization demographics].

**SD7-specific implication**: crisis-pathway entry route is racially patterned through the cumulative-disadvantage chain; resolution requires upstream voluntary-system access + Right Care expansion + 988 dispatch capacity + CRC capacity + crisis residential expansion coordinated across cumulative-disadvantage geography. The chain culmination at SD7 closes the racial-equity arc that opens at SD3 (voluntary-utilization) + extends through SD2 + SD6.

---

#### §8 Anchor Institution Thread

Per RD0 Standard 10 triple-role coverage:

**Philadelphia CRC**. (i) Regulator-influencing + regulated by CMS CoP + PA DOH licensure + DBHIDS contract; (ii) Resource: walk-in crisis evaluation capacity; (burden) operational sustainability + capacity-vs.-demand; (iii) Structural beneficiary: federal + state + city funding; (excluded constituent advocate) operates partially as advocate for crisis-presenting population.

**Right Care + DBHIDS-PPD interagency**. (i) Regulator-influencing through interagency MOU + DBHIDS clinical standards + PPD operational standards; (ii) Resource: mobile crisis + co-responder (resource); (burden) capacity + 24/7 + geographic coverage gap; (iii) Structural beneficiary: federal MIOTCRA + state grants + city budget; (excluded constituent advocate) operates as advocate for crisis-presenting population in police-encounter pathway.

**Penn Presbyterian + Temple Episcopal + Jefferson EDs**. (i) Regulator-influencing through clinical standard-setting + hospital CoP compliance; regulated by CMS + PA DOH + Joint Commission; (ii) Resource: psychiatric-capable ED capacity; (burden) psychiatric boarding population financial + operational burden + EMTALA obligation; (iii) Structural beneficiary: Medicaid + Medicare + commercial reimbursement; (excluded constituent) psychiatric boarding population structurally excluded from prompt inpatient placement.

**988 + PA OMHSAS network**. (i) Regulator-influencing through dispatch + crisis-services authorization; (ii) Resource: 988 access; (burden) dispatch-rate gap. (iii) Structural beneficiary: federal + state funding; (excluded constituent advocate) crisis-presenting population.

**CHOP (pediatric)**. (i)-(iii) Pediatric ED with psychiatric capacity; cross-reference SD4.

**VA Philadelphia Medical Center**. (i)-(iii) Veterans Crisis Line + 988 integration anchor; cross-reference D24.

The anchor institution profile for SD7 spreads across CRC + Right Care + ED operators + 988 network + DBHIDS — multi-anchor crisis-system architecture.

#### §1 Data Provenance Statement

Statistical caveats: crisis-system data infrastructure variable; 988 dispatch metrics subject to definition variation; F-flags assigned for current-state. PA-3 specific quantification depends on DBHIDS reporting + CRC reporting + CBH crisis-services data.

#### §2 PA-3 Statistical Profile — Crisis Infrastructure

##### 2.1 Crisis-system capacity baseline

- CRC (1229 N. 3rd Street): ~25-30 capacity; ~20-25K visits/year [F-verify]
- Right Care: zone + 24/7 coverage gap [F3/P1]
- Crisis residential: capacity consistently full per documented pattern [F-verify]
- Mobile crisis dispatch through 988: low rate per national benchmark; PA-3 [F-verify]
- ED psychiatric boarding: 24-72+ hour holds documented (SD2 G1-SD2-03)
- Hospital ED psychiatric capacity: Penn Presbyterian + Temple Episcopal + Jefferson + CHOP (pediatric) + others; specific bed counts [F-verify]

##### 2.2 Crisis-pathway entry volume estimates

- 988 calls + texts PA + PA-3 [F7/F12 P2]
- Police-MH-crisis calls + 911 routing PA-3 [F-verify]
- ED psychiatric presentations PA-3 [F-verify FY26]
- 302 petitions originating from crisis encounters: substantial share of 8-12K Philadelphia 302 volume (SD2 carry-through)

##### 2.3 Coverage architecture intersections

Crisis-pathway insurance coverage follows broader PA-3 pattern: Medicaid (CBH) majority; commercial fully-insured + ERISA self-funded subset; Medicare subset; uninsured residual. EMTALA mandate operates regardless of coverage; downstream care continuity varies by coverage.

##### 2.4 Veteran-specific crisis line

VA Philadelphia Medical Center + Veterans Crisis Line + 988 press-1 integration; PA-3 veteran population [F-verify].

---

#### §3 Geographic Variation

##### 3.1 North/Northwest Philadelphia Core

Highest crisis-pathway-entry volume; CRC geographically positioned in this sub-area (1229 N. 3rd Street). Right Care zone coverage variable [F3]. ED boarding burden falls on Temple Episcopal + Penn Presbyterian. Crisis-via-police pathway pattern most pronounced. Cumulative-disadvantage geography produces highest crisis-presentation rate + highest pathway-entry-by-police rate.

##### 3.2 West Philadelphia Core

Bifurcated. University City CHOP + Penn ED capacity; adjacent neighborhoods closer to North Core pattern. Right Care + CIT coverage variable.

##### 3.3 Northwest Philadelphia

Heterogeneous; lower-income tracts closer to North Core pattern. Crisis residential + outpatient access variable.

##### 3.4 South/Southwest Philadelphia

Better access pattern; Jefferson + Penn Center City accessible; lower crisis-pathway-entry-by-police rate.

##### 3.5 Boundary-adjacent

Hahnemann (closed July 2019) historic capacity; capacity-loss precedent. NSH (Montgomery County) + state-hospital network beyond PA-3 boundary affecting commitment-pathway capacity.

---

#### §4 Aggregate Pathway Tracing

**Pathway A: PA-3 resident in psychiatric crisis self-presents:**
1. Decision: 988 call/text; CRC walk-in; ED self-presentation; outpatient call
2. 988: routed via PCL (Philadelphia Crisis Line — only locally-based, in-house 988 response team in country per TC-08); 36% increase in warm transfers from 911 to 988 via PCL co-location in 911 Radio Room; mobile crisis dispatch from PCL routes to CMCRT teams; referral to CRC/ED/outpatient
3. CRC walk-in: 24/7 evaluation; potential admission to crisis residential or ED transfer or community discharge
4. ED self-presentation: EMTALA screening; psychiatric evaluation; admission/transfer/discharge; potential boarding
5. Outpatient call: 4-8 week wait standard pushes acute presentation to crisis pathway by attrition

Breakdown points: Step 2 988 dispatch capacity gap; Step 3 CRC capacity-vs.-demand; Step 4 boarding pattern; Step 5 outpatient wait time pushing to crisis.

**Pathway B: PA-3 resident in psychiatric crisis encountered by police:**
1. 911 call (family member; bystander; self) → PPD dispatch decision
2. Right Care availability + zone coverage [F3] vs. CIT-trained officer + uniformed response (~25-30% CIT)
3. On-scene assessment: voluntary transport to ED/CRC; Right Care de-escalation + community resource referral; involuntary 302 by MD post-scene; arrest if criminal
4. Outcomes: ED → admission/discharge/boarding; CRC → evaluation pathway; 302 → ED → inpatient/discharge; arrest → PPS pathway (SD6)

Breakdown points: Step 1-2 dispatch decision; Step 3 outcome variability; cumulative-disadvantage racial pattern in police-pathway rate.

**Pathway C: Pediatric crisis pathway:**
1. Family / school / pediatrician identifies child or adolescent crisis
2. CHOP pediatric ED; CCTC; pediatric crisis line if available; 988 pediatric routing
3. Psychiatric evaluation; admission to pediatric inpatient (CHOP limited beds; out-of-city placement common); CSU; discharge with safety plan
4. Family + school + outpatient coordination

Breakdown points: Step 3 pediatric inpatient capacity constraint; out-of-city placement family-separation effect (carry-through SD4 G1-SD4-03).

**Pathway D: Reentry crisis pathway (PPS release):**
1. PPS release; first-2-4-weeks peak-crisis-risk window (carry-through SD6 G1-SD6-02)
2. Medicaid reinstatement gap; outpatient appointment unavailable in window
3. Crisis presentation: 988 call; ED presentation; police encounter
4. Crisis pathway entry; potential re-arrest if behavioral; potential 302; potential overdose

Breakdown points: Steps 1-3 are the structural compound of reentry-gap + crisis-system intake.

**Pathway E: Veteran crisis pathway:**
1. Veterans Crisis Line + 988 press-1 routing
2. VA Philadelphia coordination; VA Medical Center MH services; VA crisis-pathway-to-VA-treatment continuity
3. Non-VA-eligible veteran or VA-saturation: civilian crisis pathway as Pathway A

Breakdown points: VA capacity + non-VA-eligibility for some veterans.

---

#### §5 Representation Question

**Element 1 (formal provision):** 988 Lifeline framework + EMTALA emergency stabilization + CMS CoP for psychiatric facilities + PA Act 71 of 2014 MH crisis services + OMHSAS mobile crisis + CSU standards + Philadelphia CRC + Right Care + DBHIDS crisis-services coordination + ACA EHB MH/SUD inclusion + MHPAEA crisis-services parity. Multi-layer architecture.

**Element 2 (actual receipt):** 988 dispatch rate low (1-2% national, PA-3 [F-verify]); Right Care zone + 24/7 coverage gap [F3]; CRC capacity-vs.-demand strain [F-verify]; ED psychiatric boarding 24-72+ hour holds documented; crisis residential consistently full [F-verify]; pediatric crisis-pathway capacity constrained; reentry crisis-pathway compound (G1-SD6-02); cumulative racial-equity chain culminates in crisis-via-police pathway pattern.

**Element 3 (account for the gap):** Multiple compounding causes integrated from prior SDs: (a) IMD exclusion + state-hospital-saturation + private-bed-scarcity compound (SD2 G1-SD2-03 carry-through); (b) community-services capacity gap (SD3 multiple findings) producing attrition to crisis pathway; (c) pediatric BH gaps (SD4) producing pediatric crisis pathway burden; (d) parity gaps (SD5) compounding by reducing voluntary-pathway access; (e) forensic-system patterns (SD6) producing police-routed crisis entry; (f) federal agency capacity erosion at SAMHSA + CMS + DOJ CRD threatening crisis-system architecture. Crisis-system shortfall is therefore not isolated to crisis-system design — it is the operational expression of every prior SD's gap.

**Element 4 (meaning for representation analysis):** SD7 is the **operational nexus where all prior D3 SDs converge** — and the substructure-designated synthesis charter for the domain (handled in separate domain synthesis document). The crisis system's design reflects every prior SD's gaps; therefore, crisis-system reform alone cannot resolve the upstream-driven shortfall. Highest-impact federal-rep leverage points: (1) **Right Care expansion** [F3/P1] as the highest-impact infrastructure intervention per substructure-anticipated finding; (2) 988 funding sustainability + state implementation [F12/P2]; (3) IMD exclusion modification (federal Medicaid reform; SD2/SD3 carry-through); (4) MIOTCRA reauthorization + appropriations for crisis-intervention training; (5) SAMHSA crisis-services grants + capacity restoration; (6) CMS Medicaid crisis-services coverage + § 1115 SUD IMD waivers; (7) BSCA implementation including pediatric crisis. State/local at: PA OMHSAS crisis-services standards; DBHIDS Right Care + CRC + crisis residential expansion; PPD CIT integration. **Cross-cutting** synthesis findings: transinstitutionalization (SD6 fingerprint); cumulative racial-equity chain (SD2 → SD3 → SD6 → SD7 culmination); multi-system coordination (G1-XC-05/06 pattern); federal-rep-leverage richness (G1-XC-04 D3 federal-rep-leverage profile).
